1
Introduction
Citation and commencement
This Document is the draft Macquarie Coal
Complex Transformation Precinct Master Plan 2026
(abbreviated as Master Plan).
Land to which this
Master Plan applies
The draft Master Plan applies to the Macquarie
Coal Complex Precinct (the Precinct) as shown in
the aerial below (Figure 1). Land subject to this
draft Master Plan is owned by Macquarie Coal Joint
Venture.
Post mining land use policy context
The Macquarie Coal Complex Pilot Project was
initiated as part of the NSW Government’s wider
response to the Beneficial and productive post-
mining land use Parliamentary Inquiry (2025). The
Inquiry recommended a comprehensive review of
the post mining regulatory framework to support
innovative, productive and employment-generating
reuse of mined land, including provision for
multiple successive land uses and the modification
of existing consents.
In July 2025, the NSW Government committed to
undertake two pilot projects to demonstrate how
alternative post mining land uses can be delivered
under the existing planning and regulatory
framework. The Macquarie Coal Complex is one of
these pilots and the rezoning was progressed by
the Department of Planning, Housing and
Infrastructure (the Department) in partnership with
Lake Macquarie City Council (Council), Australian
Government through Net Zero Economy Authority
(NZEA), Glencore and other NSW Government

--- Page 5 [Matched: ['vision', 'principles', 'precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use']] ---
Figure 2: Process of the Precinct transforming to an employment generating precinct
Consistent with this policy direction, the
Department and Council have undertaken master
planning informed by technical studies to support
the coordinated transformation of the former mine
site to alternative, employment-generating land
uses in the Precinct (Figure 2). The draft Master
Plan establishes the design framework to realise
this opportunity, providing the certainty needed
by investors, industry partners and the
community, demonstrating a transferable model
for productive post mining land use across NSW.
Relationship to other
planning instruments
State Environmental Planning Policy (Precincts –
Regional) 2021
A new chapter will be prepared for the Macquarie
Coal Complex Precinct in the State Environmental
Planning Policy (Precincts – Regional) 2021
(Precincts – Regional SEPP). The chapter will:
•
Provide zoning and land use controls
•
Determine permissible and prohibited land
uses
•
Set general and site-specific provisions
•
Present the role and requirements of the draft
Master Plan
•
Excise pilot project sites from relevant Local
Environmental Plans.
Existing planning pathways will be utilised.
Macquarie Coal Complex Post-Mining

--- Page 6 [Matched: ['vision', 'precinct', 'land use', 'access']] ---
Purpose and how to
use this draft Master Plan
The purpose of this draft Master Plan is to provide
a hierarchy of Precinct-wide aims and establish
performance criteria to support the delivery of a
diverse range of land uses that support regional
economic diversification, attract investment and
create local jobs and protect high value biodiversity
(Figure 4). It is intended to establish a broad
framework for implementation where detailed
design, operational and market feasibility must be
balanced to achieve the vision. The draft Master
Plan is to be applied with flexibility and the consent
authority may allow for alternate viable solutions
so that otherwise permissible development can be
carried out.
This draft Master Plan details the overall planning
direction for the Precinct and is not prescriptive with
regards to built form design solutions, rather sets
outcomes to be achieved through flexible solutions,
such as adaptive reuse and staged delivery.
Development applications must:
•
Consider the relevant aims and performance
criteria
•
Provide site-specific technical assessments
(such as access, flooding, drainage,
water quality, tree preservation, utilities,
contamination and acoustic separation) as
required
•
Demonstrate the development’s proposed use is
consistent with this draft Master Plan
•

--- Page 7 [Matched: ['vision', 'principles', 'precinct', 'land use', 'access']] ---
•
Guidance on what technical reports (e.g., traffic,
noise, heritage, environmental) are needed at
the development application (DA) stage for
permissible land uses.
•
Clear performance criteria and development
controls for built form, amenity, and
environment.
•
Identification of sensitive land and hazards
(e.g., flood risk, biodiversity, geotechnical,
contamination).
•
Buffers and controls to manage land use
conflicts, especially between industrial,
sensitive uses and environmentally constrained
land.
•
Information on infrastructure, public spaces,
and how these will be delivered.
•
Urban design principles, including Connecting
with Country.
•
Access and mobility strategy, including freight
and transport upgrades.
•
Water management strategy (efficiency,
stormwater, sustainability).
•
Any other matters the Planning Secretary
requires.

2	 Strategic context
Precinct location and strategic fit
The Precinct comprises approximately 1160
hectares within the Lake Macquarie Local
Government Area on the southern periphery of
greater Newcastle. Macquarie Coal Complex is a
former mining operation comprising West Wallsend
Colliery, Teralba Colliery, Westside Mine, the
Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant (MCPP), Tailings
Storage Facility (TSF) and associated
infrastructure.
Coal mining has been a defining land use in the
area since the early 19th Century, shaping both the
physical landscape and the economic profile of
surrounding communities. The mining operations in
the Precinct are currently closed and undergoing
rehabilitation, presenting a large, consolidated
landholding with significant potential for new
employment-generating uses.
The Precinct is characterised by a diverse post
mining landscape that includes rehabilitating
landforms, residual industrial infrastructure, water
management features and areas of ecological
regeneration. These attributes create a complex
but opportunity-rich setting. Extensive brownfield
areas with existing access and servicing potential
are complemented by areas of environmental value
requiring protection and integration into future
land use outcomes.
Transforming the Macquarie Coal Complex aligns
with the strategic directions of the Hunter Regional
Plan 2041, which seeks to diversify the regional
economy, leverage existing infrastructure and
repurpose former mining land to support long-term
employment growth. At a local level, the Precinct

--- Page 10 [Matched: ['precinct', 'access']] ---
Precinct opportunities
A generational-scale opportunity to transform former mining land into
the Lower Hunter’s premier employment precinct, delivering diverse jobs
and much-needed employment land alongside lasting community and
environmental outcomes.
Unmatched scale under single ownership – one of the largest master planned
employment land releases under single ownership in NSW, providing flexible
lot configurations, co-location of complementary industries and the ability to
accommodate large-footprint uses.
A skilled workforce – one of Australia’s deepest pools of transferable
industrial skills in mining, heavy industry, engineering, logistics,
construction and environmental management, directly applicable to the
advanced manufacturing, circular economy models, energy-intensive,
technology and logistics uses targeted for the Precinct.
Strategic multi-modal connectivity – direct access to motorway, arterial, rail,
port and airport networks supports a diverse range of employment uses over
the long term.
Development-ready brownfield land – extensive previously cleared and
disturbed land, established internal access roads, hardstand, servicing
corridors and modified landforms reduce lead times and upfront costs
compared with greenfield alternatives.
Existing energy and utility infrastructure – proximity to electricity transmission
and utility infrastructure provides a foundation for diverse employment uses,
including energy-intensive industries that are increasingly constrained by grid
access elsewhere.
Integrated environmental, cultural and employment outcomes – rehabilitating
landforms, native bushland, waterways and ecological corridors provide
the framework for a distinctive precinct identity that integrates diverse
employment opportunities with high-quality landscape, biodiversity, recreation
and Connecting with Country outcomes.

--- Page 11 [Matched: ['vision', 'precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use', 'access']] ---
Precinct constraints
The Precinct presents a range of environmental, physical and infrastructure
constraints that are managed through the structure plan and addressed
progressively through staged development and site-specific assessment at the
development application stage.
Geotechnical stability and risk management – Development on the TSF and the
land located in the dam break inundation zone to the north is currently limited.
Soft alluvial soils located around the coastal wetlands in the north of the site
influence construction methods.
Environmental – Existing areas of high biodiversity values, high bush fire risk
and flood affected areas require further investigation to inform development
siting and design.
Sensitive uses – Interfaces between existing land uses, including residential
receivers and potential future industrial land uses will need to be managed to
mitigate negative noise, odour and visual impacts.
Infrastructure – Heavy vehicle access to parts of the site is currently limited
due to steep gradients, access through local residential areas and existing rail
crossings. The location of the existing oil pipeline, electrical transmission lines
and their associated easements, setbacks and design measures will need to be
considered.
Utility infrastructure – Viability and sequencing of the provision of utilities,
particularly to the West Lake sub-precinct, will need further investigation.

A generational-scale opportunity to transform

premier employment precinct, delivering diverse
jobs and well-located employment land at a

diversification, attract investment and create
local jobs.

A master planned, single-ownership Precinct

configurations, co-location of complementary
industries and accommodation of large-footprint
uses.
•
A flexible, staged delivery framework that
aligns with mine closure commitments,
infrastructure capacity and changing economic,
social and market conditions, for the orderly
transformation from rehabilitation to productive
post mining uses.

•
Leveraging existing transport connections to
deliver an efficient, well-serviced employment
precinct, with a legible internal road hierarchy
that supports safe and equitable access for
freight, workforce, public transport and active
transport users.

Utilities and services that meet the current

--- Page 15 [Matched: ['principles', 'precinct', 'land use']] ---
mining land uses. It establishes the foundation for
planning, design and development to be guided by
the principles of the Government Architect NSW
Connecting with Country Framework.
As the draft Master Plan is exhibited and refined,
meaningful engagement with Lake Macquarie
Aboriginal community stakeholders will form
an important part of the process to refine and
finalise the draft Master Plan. This should include
engagement with Traditional Custodians, Aboriginal
knowledge holders, the Awabakal Local Aboriginal
Land Council, the Biraban Local Aboriginal Land
Council and other Aboriginal organisations or
representatives identified through the engagement
process.
Connecting with Country
The Precinct is located on Awabakal Country,
adjacent to the shores of Cockle Bay in the north
of Lake Macquarie. The draft Master Plan
recognises Country as a living system of
interconnected cultural, ecological and physical
relationships, including land, waters, skies, plants,
animals, cultural practices, stories and community.
It also recognises that planning for the
transformation of a former mining landscape must
be informed by an understanding of Country and
respectful engagement with Aboriginal people
who hold cultural knowledge, rights and
responsibilities for the area.
The draft Master Plan has been prepared as an
early framework for the transformation of the
Precinct from mining to sustainable and
productive post
Credit: Glencore

Structure plan
The structure plan (Figure 9) is the principal
spatial framework for the draft Master Plan. It
identifies the arrangement of developable areas,
environmentally constrained areas, recreational
areas, movement networks and key constraints
across the Precinct to guide development.
The structure plan outlines the spatial logic for
future development at a precinct scale, providing
a framework within which detailed design,
subdivision and development assessment will
occur. The structure plan is supported by technical
studies.
The following categories are used to describe land
within the structure plan:
•
Developable areas – Land that is potentially
suitable for development, subject to further
technical investigation and site-specific
assessment at the development application
stage.
•
Environmentally constrained areas – Land
identified as having biodiversity values or where
natural features (watercourses, wetlands, steep
slope, flooding) constrain development.
•
Recreation areas – Land potentially suitable for
active and passive recreation facilities,
balanced with protecting biodiversity and
cultural values.
•
Further investigation – Potentially developable
land that is constrained in the short to medium
term by geotechnical, dam safety, biodiversity

4  Structure plan and sub-precinct framework

Sub-precinct framework
The structure plan organises the Precinct into
five development sub-precincts (Figure 10), set
within a connected network of environmentally
constrained areas that define the Precinct’s
landscape framework. Each sub-precinct presents
a distinctive opportunity, shaped by its topography,
legacy mining features, environmental values,
access and proximity to existing communities and
infrastructure.
Together the sub-precincts enable a coordinated,
staged transformation of the Precinct that
responds to physical setting while delivering
a diverse range of employment, recreation
and community outcomes. Each sub-precinct
description presents key characteristics, land use
intent and opportunities for future planning and
development, against which development proposals
will be assessed.
Figure 10: Sub-precincts

Land use intent
Future development will be anchored by
employment-generating activity that supports
regional economic diversification, attracts
investment and creates local jobs. Complementary
commercial, recreation, community and visitor land
uses will support the workforce, deliver community
benefit and contribute to the broader public value
of the post mining tranformation.

--- Page 20 [Matched: ['precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use', 'access']] ---
Cockle Creek
Characteristics
Cockle Creek sub-precinct is located in the eastern
portion of the Precinct and encompasses the
former MCPP, TSF, mine rail loop and associated
operational infrastructure. Its direct interface
with the Main Northern Railway, together with
established access from Racecourse Road,
Railway Street and The Weir Road, positions the
sub-precinct as a strategically connected gateway
within the broader Precinct.
Historic industrial use has shaped a substantially
modified landscape of previously disturbed land
and legacy infrastructure. These attributes provide
a strong foundation for early activation and support
the efficient reuse of land.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s
development context and inform a flexible,
opportunity-led planning response:
•
Reuse of legacy industrial assets – The former
coal preparation plant, mine rail loop and
existing rail interface with the Main Northern
railway provide a platform for new industrial,
manufacturing, energy and rail-related uses that
leverage available infrastructure.
•
Early activation of available land – Areas outside
the TSF and dam break inundation zone are well-
positioned for near-term development, with
further land progressively released as TSF
rehabilitation advances.
•
Enhanced connectivity – A potential new bridge

--- Page 21 [Matched: ['vision', 'precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use', 'access']] ---
Killingworth
Characteristics
Killingworth sub-precinct is located in the north-
western portion of the Precinct, near established
residential areas of Killingworth and Barnsley.
Accessible topography, established road
connections via Wakefield Road and The Broadway,
and a direct interface with environmentally
constrained areas and biodiversity corridors to the
south and east define its place-based setting.
Its proximity to existing communities and the
prevalence of previously disturbed land from
former mining operations position the sub-precinct
as a strong candidate for early activation. These
attributes provide a practical platform for short-
term development opportunities, aligned with
rehabilitation outcomes and sequenced provision of
servicing.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s
development context and inform an opportunity-led
planning response:
•
Compatible community interface – Interface
with established residential areas of
Killingworth and Barnsley is managed through
appropriate buffers, setbacks and acoustic
treatments to make sure land use compatibility
and protect amenity.
•
Efficient access and movement – Accessible
topography and established connections
through Wakefield Road and The Broadway
enable efficient site access, with heavy vehicle
routing strategy that directs freight to approved

Barnsley West
Characteristics
Barnsley West sub-precinct is in the north-western
portion of the Precinct, comprising separate parcels
of land associated with the former Wakefield
Colliery. Accessed via Wakefield Road and Charlton
Street, the sub-precinct sits within a distinctive
landscape setting shaped by its interface with
adjoining residential areas, environmentally
constrained lands and established transmission
infrastructure. High biodiversity land between
Barnsley West and the Killingworth sub-precinct
reinforces its discrete identity.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s
development context and inform an opportunity-
led, planning response:
•
Small-scale urban services and light industry
– The sub-precinct’s compact scale and
adjacency to residential areas provide for a
curated mix of small-scale urban services, light
industrial and complementary employment
uses that meet local needs while supporting the
Precinct’s broader economic diversification.
•
Amenity-led interface with Barnsley village –
The residential interface informs a high-quality
edge response, including built form transitions,
landscape buffers, acoustic treatments and
operational controls, that protect the amenity
and character of the existing community.
•
Coordinated infrastructure interface – Existing
transmission easements are integrated into

Teralba
Characteristics
The Teralba sub-precinct is located approximately
two kilometres south-east of the main Precinct,
adjoining the established Teralba local centre. The
sub-precinct is defined by its direct proximity to
two rail stations, its interface with the heritage-
listed Teralba Conservation Area, and its integration
with the established residential and commercial
fabric of the Teralba village.
Together, these attributes position Teralba as the
Precinct’s mixed use sub-precinct, a compact,
well-serviced setting where new housing and
complementary uses can support the ongoing
vitality of the local centre and contribute to the
broader regional transformation envisaged by the
Master Plan.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s
development context and inform an opportunity-led
planning response:
•
Conveniently connected and serviced –
Positioned approximately 300 metres from
Booragul Station and 400 metres from Teralba
Station, the sub-precinct enables direct access
to the regional passenger rail network and
supports a walkable, low-car neighbourhood
model.
•
Reinforcement of the Teralba local centre –
Proximity to established retail, community and
commercial services provides the foundation
for residential and complementary uses that
strengthen the centre’s economic base, activate

--- Page 25 [Matched: ['precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use']] ---
Environmentally constrained areas
Characteristics
Environmentally constrained areas form an integral
and defining element of the Precinct’s structure,
comprising tracts of remnant native vegetation,
rehabilitation areas, riparian corridors, waterways
and ecological networks distributed across
the Precinct. Together, these areas establish a
connected environmental framework that supports
biodiversity, water cycle management, landscape
identity and Connecting with Country outcomes.
Environmentally constrained areas anchor the
structure plan in a high-quality landscape setting
and reinforce ecological connectivity across the
region. The intent of these lands is to protect and
enhance environmental, hydrological, cultural and
landscape values over the long-term, while
enabling sensitive land use where suitable.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s
development context and inform an opportunity-
led, planning response:
•
Anchoring the Precinct’s ecological framework
– High biodiversity areas, key fish habitat
and terrestrial corridors provide north–south and
east–west ecological connectivity across the
Precinct. Biodiversity corridors, coastal
wetlands, mapped waterways and areas subject
to Voluntary Conservation Agreements
informing a clear protection framework aligned
with statutory requirements.
•
Considered, evidence-based approach to
compatible use – Where supported by detailed

--- Page 26 [Matched: ['vision', 'precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use']] ---
Implementation and sequencing
Implementation
The implementation and delivery of the entire
Precinct vision is intended to be sequenced over
several decades, reflecting the need to manage
mine closure and rehabilitation responsibilities and
to allow the future land uses to evolve in alignment
with development uptake. Delivery of supporting
infrastructure is intended to be staged to correlate
with the development as it is implemented over
time.
Development sequencing provides for
infrastructure delivery that is proactive but demand
led and enables early activation while supporting
long-term, efficient and resilient precinct
development. Infrastructure delivery is to be
adaptive, demand driven and coordinated, ensuring
that development does not outpace enabling
infrastructure while maintaining flexibility for
market led uptake. Development is to be staged in
accordance with available infrastructure capacity,
with priority given to feasibility of connecting
with existing utilities and transport networks.
Infrastructure delivery should adopt an integrated
and coordinated approach across utilities and
transport to minimise disruption and duplication.

--- Page 29 [Matched: ['precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use', 'access', 'staging']] ---
Short-term activation
Areas identified as being capable of early release
for post mining land use development share
common characteristics, including land where
rehabilitation is at or near completion, existing
road access is available, servicing connections are
feasible and the risk profile is manageable without
requiring major investigations or infrastructure
upgrades.
Early phases are expected to leverage available
capacity within the Argenton Substation, existing
potable water connections and established regional
freight corridors, minimising the need for major
upfront augmentation. These areas also align with
transport accessibility and heavy vehicle routing
capability, supporting early industrial and logistics
uses.
These short-term activation areas provide the
opportunity to demonstrate viability of post mining
land use shift, generate investment confidence and
deliver tangible employment and community
outcomes.
Cockle Creek
Previously disturbed areas outside the TSF and
dam break inundation zone with existing access to
the regional road network. Early activation within
this sub-precinct could leverage the existing rail
loop with opportunities for related manufacturing
facilities.
This sub-precinct benefits from the strategic role
of freight and logistics in this area, leveraging
proximity to supporting flexible, freight-adjacent
land uses as infrastructure staging evolves.
Development in this sub-precinct is staged to align
with infrastructure delivery. A graduated approach

--- Page 30 [Matched: ['precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use', 'access']] ---
Teralba
Teralba sub-precinct is strategically located
between the Teralba and Booragul train stations.
Proximity to existing utility networks reduces
servicing lead times and upfront infrastructure
costs. Established road access and adjacency
to nearby rail stations, residential communities
and employment areas provide a ready platform
for early development. Together, these attributes
present a compelling opportunity to deliver
residential outcomes that complement the
surrounding context and contribute to the
Precinct’s early activation and investment
momentum.
Medium-term activation
Medium-term activation areas require the delivery
of enabling infrastructure, resolution of key
transport and access constraints and progressive
risk reduction through ongoing rehabilitation and
technical investigations. These areas represent the
core employment-generating lands of the Precinct
and will require coordinated investment in roads,
utilities and potentially rail infrastructure.
Progressive development toward the southern and
more constrained areas will require coordinated
upgrades to transport, utilities and Precinct scale
water cycle management. This includes delivery of
wastewater infrastructure (currently absent across
much of the Precinct), expansion of water networks
and potential major electricity augmentation or
new substations where demand thresholds are
exceeded. Concurrently, transport upgrades,
including intersection capacity improvements,
new internal road links and potential additional
crossings of Cockle Creek, must be staged

--- Page 31 [Matched: ['precinct', 'structure plan', 'staging']] ---
Staging triggers
The progressive release of developable land across
the Precinct is linked to the completion of key
technical investigations and the achievement of
rehabilitation milestones. The following staging
triggers apply as relevant across the Precinct:
•
Dam safety and Geotech – Geotechnical
monitoring and assessment of TSF, with the view
to de-declaring the dam. Updated dam break
and consequence assessments demonstrating
a reduction in the dam break inundation zone.
Refer to Section 9 Sustainability and resilience
on page 51 for detailed dam safety aims and
performance criteria.
•
Contamination – Completion of staged
contamination investigations and, where
required, remediation or management
measures appropriate to the proposed land
use, in accordance with the Contaminated Land
Management Act 1997 and State Environmental
Planning Policy (Resilience and Hazards) 2021.
•
Geotechnical stability – Geotechnical
assessments confirming the suitability of land
for the proposed development type, particularly
rehabilitated landforms.
•
Flooding – Detailed flood modelling confirming
flood planning levels, developable areas
and appropriate mitigation measures for
development within or adjacent to flood-
affected land, including climate change and
dam break interaction scenarios.

--- Page 33 [Matched: ['precinct', 'land use']] ---
Desired future
character
The Precinct offers the
opportunity to deliver a
distinctive future character,
shaped by its post mining
landscape and a diverse mix
of land uses. Employment-
generating activity will anchor
the Precinct, complemented
by commercial, recreation,
community and visitor uses.
The Precinct will transform a legacy mining
landscape into the Lower Hunter’s leading post
mining employment precinct, becoming a well-
connected, productive and resilient destination
that is an attractive place to work, invest,
recreate and visit, with high-quality urban form
and integrated landscape.
5	 Built form and landscape
33

--- Page 34 [Matched: ['vision', 'principles', 'precinct', 'access']] ---
The following character principles guide Precinct development.
Built form
The Precinct supports employment generating development responsive to its
landscape context setting. Building orientation, scale and siting is carefully
considered to integrate with the site’s natural features.
Landscape
Building on existing high environmental value, the Precinct protects and
enhances its natural landscape, with remnant vegetation and waterways
treated as defining landscape elements that shape the Precinct’s character.
Biodiversity can be protected near development areas through the
establishment of green corridors, landscape buffers and setbacks that
respond to topography and ecological systems.
Place and identity
The Precinct offers the opportunity to create a distinctive identity that
reflects its natural qualities, cultural values and industrial history.
Public realm and mobility
Well-connected to surrounding networks, the site supports safe, clear
and legible movement systems that connect sub-precincts, support active
transport and prioritise pedestrian amenity and landscape integration at key
interfaces. Access and circulation are designed to respond to the scale of
employment uses.
Sustainability and resilience
Built form and landscape design embed water sensitive urban design
(WSUD), ecological restoration, efficient resource use and climate resilience,
delivering a precinct that performs as a productive landscape.
Interface and transition
Development at precinct edges manages the transition to environmentally
constrained areas and surrounding communities through graduated built
form, generous setbacks and landscape buffers that protect amenity and
maintain natural character.
Flexibility and staged activation
Built form and subdivision patterns accommodate staged delivery and
evolving uses over time, ensuring that interim conditions and early activation
areas contribute positively to precinct character without compromising long-
term outcomes.

--- Page 35 [Matched: ['vision', 'precinct', 'access']] ---
Urban design and public realm
Urban design is guided by shift from mining to
employment-generating uses, as well as
opportunities for tourism and recreation. The
design approach prioritises functional, robust
and adaptable built form outcomes that support
a range of industrial, logistics and contemporary
employment uses.
Aims
•
Achieve high-quality urban design outcomes
consistent with the Better Placed (GANSW, 2017)
objectives.
•
Provide a mix of contemporary, high-quality
building types and sizes to support employment
opportunities that evolve in line with changing
economic drivers
•
Built form to be of high quality, with facades
that address the street and that have
articulation, modulation, passive surveillance
and street activation
•
Make sure building siting and scale respond to
landscape, context, environmental values and
interface conditions.
•
Strengthen precinct identity through landscape,
signage, landmarks and heritage-informed
placemaking.
•
Provide landscape setbacks and public realm
outcomes that support amenity, biodiversity and
movement.

--- Page 36 [Matched: ['precinct', 'access']] ---
Sub-precinct interface consideration
The Precinct incorporates a range of contextual
interfaces, including environmentally constrained
areas, open space and recreation areas, and
external sensitive receivers such as established
residential communities. Three interface typologies
guide the design of the Precinct’s edges, each
shaped by the values of the adjoining land and
the role of the interface in protecting amenity,
biodiversity and bushfire safety outcomes:
•
Typology 1 – Development adjoining
environmentally constrained areas (including
heavily vegetated and bushfire-prone areas)
•
Typology 2 – Developable area to open space
and recreation areas
•
Typology 3 – Precinct boundary to external
context (including adjoining residential and
other sensitive receivers).
Each typology is illustrated separately in Figure 25
to Figure 27. Together, these treatments establish
high-quality, well-managed edges that retain
the values of adjoining land while supporting the
Precinct’s long-term productive use.
Aims
•
Establish clear, durable transitions between
developable areas, environmentally constrained
areas, open space and surrounding
communities.
•
Protect biodiversity values, ecological corridors,
waterways and cultural heritage from the

--- Page 37 [Matched: ['principles', 'precinct', 'land use', 'access']] ---
maintenance arrangements and clear physical
demarcation.
Typology 2 – Developable area to open space and
recreation areas
A.	 Development adjoining open space or recreation
areas must provide a positive, legible and active
interface to the public realm.
B.	 Pedestrian access points and landscape
treatments are to integrate seamlessly with
adjoining open space.
C.	 Buildings must be oriented to support Crime
Prevention Through Environmental Design
(CPTED) principles and provide passive
surveillance of public open space, where
appropriate to the proposed use.
D.	 Open space at sub-precinct interfaces are
to function as a connected system that links
environmentally constrained areas, walking and
cycling routes, significant vegetation and natural
landforms.
E.	 Lighting at open space interfaces must support
safe pedestrian movement while applying
CPTED and wildlife-friendly lighting principles
where the open space adjoins or connects to
environmentally constrained areas.
Typology 3 – Precinct boundary to external context
A.	 Development adjoining residential areas or other
sensitive receivers must provide setbacks and
landscape buffers informed by detailed land use
compatibility and acoustic assessment.
B.	 Buildings at sensitive interfaces must be
sited and designed to minimise overlooking,
overshadowing and visual dominance.
C.	 Acoustic, air quality and operational mitigation
measures must be incorporated where required

Biodiversity and habitat corridors
The Precinct consists of a combination of
previously disturbed land, rehabilitation areas and
undisturbed, remnant native bushland. Vegetation
includes existing cleared areas associated with the
historical operation of several former mine sites,
some of which have been rehabilitated back to
native bushland.
Aims
•
Protect areas of high biodiversity values from
development to avoid serious and irreversible
impacts on threatened ecological communities
and threatened species.
•
Promote a development outcome that is
consistent with the requirements of the avoid,
minimise and offset hierarchy of applicable
NSW and Commonwealth biodiversity
legislation.
•
Maintain the function of key linkages that
facilitate wildlife movement within the site and
the wider landscape.
•
Identify and secure environmentally constrained
land with an appropriate size and configuration
to be protected under suitable conservation
mechanisms.
•
Protect wetlands, watercourses, and associated
riparian corridors and aquatic habitats from
development.
•
Implement ecological restoration or

--- Page 42 [Matched: ['precinct', 'structure plan', 'land use', 'access', 'staging']] ---
Road network, freight and access
Road network and access is influenced by
the Precinct’s legacy as former coal mine
operations. Historic mining operations have shaped
both the physical terrain and created a framework
that continues to inform movement and access in
the post mining context. Former haul routes and
access tracks, originally designed to support
extraction, processing and transport activities,
establish a foundational spatial logic for the post
mining land use movement network.
The approach to managing transport and mobility is
multi-layered, combining infrastructure planning
and staging through demand management and
integration with land use outcomes. To facilitate
efficient movement within and around the Precinct,
the road network of sub-arterial, collector and local
roads is structured as a clear hierarchy, linking
developable land to the broader regional network
to minimise reliance on the external local network.
Subject to further investigation, a potential new
east–west crossing of Cockle Creek is identified as
an opportunity to strengthen network resilience,
unlock alternative freight access that bypasses
constrained local roads (Racecourse Road, Railway
Street and Rhondda Road) and improve
connectivity to Cockle Creek Station.
Teralba is directly serviced by Teralba and
Booragul Stations and forms a natural anchor for
enhanced pedestrian and cycling connectivity to
the wider Precinct via the Main Northern railway
corridor and the future active transport network.
Aims
•
Provide an efficient, legible and resilient road

--- Page 47 [Matched: ['principles', 'precinct', 'access']] ---
Active transport and connectivity
The Precinct provides a rare opportunity to
plan a connected, safe and direct walking and
cycling network, with preferred active transport
alignments established with vehicle routes for
optimal directness, legibility and separation from
traffic. The active transport network will connect
employment sub-precincts to Cockle Creek, Teralba
and Booragul stations, surrounding residential
communities, existing residential areas and the
broader regional shared-path network.
Aims
•
Establish a connected and practical active
transport network linking the Precinct to
surrounding communities, centres and stations.
•
Improve active transport access across key
barriers including Five Islands Road and the
Main Northern Railway.
•
Integrate active transport with heritage,
topographic and environmental constraints.
•
Provide end-of-trip facilities and safe walking
and cycling infrastructure as part of staged
precinct delivery.
Performance criteria
A.	 Pedestrian and cycling infrastructure must
be delivered early to support activation and
establish sustainable travel behaviour.
B.	 Pedestrian and cycle connections must align
with the indicative active transport network and
connect sub-precincts, public transport nodes
and open space.

--- Page 51 [Matched: ['precinct', 'land use', 'staging']] ---
Tailings storage and dam break
inundation zone
The former operations of the Precinct include a
TSF, which is an engineered structure designed
to safely store and manage rejected materials
generated from coal mining operations, including
solids and water. Closure of the TSF, including
rehabilitation and capping is underway. The TSF
will remain a declared dam until the structure is
stabilised in accordance with the Precinct’s mine
closure, rehabilitation commitments and Dams
Safety NSW approve the formal revocation of the
declaration.
The draft Master Plan applies a staged risk-
informed framework to manage hazards associated
with the TSF and the potential for dam break
downstream (dam break inundation zone) as
described in Figure 9. This framework makes sure
that future land use is compatible with the residual
risk profile of the TSF over its remaining life and
following de-declaration.
Dam break modelling has defined inundation
extents, flow paths and level of hazard under a
failure scenario. These outputs directly inform
spatial planning by establishing compatible land
use scenarios across the TSF areas that would
enable flexibility over the period of rehabilitation
and closure:
•
Sensitive or high-occupancy land uses are
avoided in the short to medium term within
mapped inundation zones and buffers.
•
Lower-intensity or transient uses may be
considered in outer zones (such as passive and

management of the TSF.
H. Utility infrastructure proposed downstream

rehabilitation.
I.
A minimum 10-metre buffer must be maintained
around the TSF footprint, unless a more
conservative requirement is established through
detailed engineering assessment.
J.

any works within the TSF must be accompanied

9  Sustainability and resilience

--- Page 53 [Matched: ['precinct', 'access']] ---
Flooding and risk management
Flooding is a key consideration for future
development within the Precinct due to the
presence of Cockle Creek, Diega Creek, Burkes
Creek and associated floodplain areas. Flood
studies indicate that flood behaviour within and
adjacent to the Precinct is primarily associated
with creek flooding, floodplain inundation and
localised drainage constraints in low-lying areas.
Flood behaviour is influenced by the Cockle Creek
system, associated tributaries and downstream
hydraulic controls including the Barnsley Weir and
Lake Macquarie water levels.
Aims
•
Direct development away from areas of higher
flood risk and drainage constraint where
practicable.
•
Protect flood conveyance, flood storage and
waterway function.
•
Make sure development is compatible with flood
hazard and does not increase flood risk
elsewhere.
•
Integrate flood resilience, emergency access
and isolation risk into precinct planning.
•
Require detailed flood, drainage and emergency
management assessment for flood-affected
land.
Performance criteria
A. Development must have regard to the flood-
affected areas identified in Figure 38 and any

--- Page 55 [Matched: ['precinct', 'land use', 'access']] ---
Bush fire resilience
Bush fire resilience is a key design parameter
shaping the structure and long-term function of the
precinct. The draft Master Plan adopts a precinct
approach to bush fire planning, responding to
the site’s classification as bush fire prone land
and the complex interaction between vegetation,
topography and development. The draft Master
Plan prioritises consolidated development
footprints, clearly defined edges and integrated
interface treatments that support both bush fire
resilience and biodiversity outcomes.
Aims
•
Protect life and property from bush fire risk
while responding to development potential,
constraints, operational requirements,
rehabilitation outcomes and the protection of
biodiversity and habitat corridors.
•
Enable ongoing land management practices
that concurrently support bush fire resilience,
protection of high value biodiversity, and the
long-term health and diversity of rehabilitated
and retained landscapes.
•
Integrate road hierarchy, access, egress and
emergency response to support operations,
firefighting and evacuation.
•
Enable suppression, staged responses and
long-term resilient precinct activation through
strategic separation, managed land and
services, including compliant water supply.

appropriately to geotechnical and possible
mine subsidence constraints.

for employment generating land uses

around 36 hectares of new open space

outline the proposed planning control changes, considerations and intent of the draft Rezoning
Proposal.

1.3
Rezoning pathway
The Department’s State Significant Rezoning Policy includes a State Led Rezoning (SLR) pathway
which seeks to identify and delivery strategically important rezonings that can contribute to the
housing and employment needs of NSW’s growing population, where
sites meet certain criteria.
The Rezoning Proposal h
as been identified as a project to proceed
as a State -led rezoning under the
State Significant Rezoning Policy
.
In accordance with the
State Significant Rezoning Policy
the Rezoning Proposal was considered to
meet the eligibility criteria as it will:
•
create up to 1,130 direct jobs
•
deliver beneficial uses for post mining land
•
benefit from a State rezoning pathway due to the complexity of the post mining nature of the
site, and coordination with key agency stakeholders.
1.4
Site location and description
The Rezoning Proposal applies to
1,160 hectares o f land and includes land
at Macquarie Coal
Complex and Teralba Southgate
(the Precinct) across the following areas:
•
West Wallsend (ceased mining in 2016)

1.5
Strategic context
In 2024, the NSW Government launched an inquiry to investigate new and innovative approaches to
post mining land use to
make sure  mining sites continue to bring economic investment to regional
communities after mines close.
The findings of the
inquiry  ‘Report No. 53 Beneficial and productive
post mining land use’
were published including the NSW’s response to the inquiry. The NSW
Government  supported  all of  the recommendations in the inquiry.
Specifically, the following recommendations
are relevant to this project:
•
Recommendation 1
– Review of existing
legislation relating to post mining rehabilitation,
mining  closure  and future land use, to
facilitate  the transformation  of former mining sites.
•
Recommendation 6
– Focus  exploring immediate opportunities for two
pilot projects (Mt
Arthur  Mine and  Macquarie  Coal  Complex)  to identify  practical  impediments  and  necessary
legislative and regulatory changes
to enable land use change, and to
establish  a State -wide
model for post mining land use pathway.
The Rezoning Proposal was progressed by the Department in partnership with Australian
Government through NZEA, Council, other NSW Government agencies and Glencore.
Following the exhibition of the rezoning proposal, a recommendations

3 Considerations
In addition to proposed changes outlined in
Section 2, the Department has also considered other
specific matters that are relevant to this draft Rezoning Proposal.
Other relevant State
Environmental Planning Policies (SEPPs) will continue to apply to the precinct
. These considerations
are explained below.
3.1
State Environmental Planning Policy (Hazards and
Resilience) 2021
State Environmental Planning Policy (Hazards and Resilience) 2021
(Hazards and Resilience SEPP) sets
out provisions for coastal management, hazardous and offensive development and remediation of
land. The Rezoning Proposal is supported with a suite of technical studies which demonstrate
compliance with Hazards and Resilience SEPP. A Biodiversity Assessment Report has been prepared
by SLR to support the proposed rezoning and master plan. The draft struct
ure plan has been
prepared with consideration to biodiv
ersity, including coastal wetlands mapped under the Hazards
and Resilience SEPP.
A Contamination Technical Assessment was prepared by Aurecon, providing a land use compatibility

master plan and notes that all future development will be subject the requirements of the Hazards
and Resilience SEPP.

Executive summary
This Utilities Engineering Report demonstrates that the Macquarie Coal Complex Precinct can be effectively
serviced to support its transition to a post-mining land use and provides a robust technical basis for rezoning.
The Precinct benefits from strong regional infrastructure connectivity, particularly in electricity, and access to
substantial on-site water resources, creating a high level of inherent servicing potential. While legacy mining
infrastructure and fragmented utility networks present constraints, these are considered manageable within a
staged delivery framework. Importantly, the site is uniquely positioned to adopt both traditional “business as
usual” servicing solutions and alternative, decentralised approaches that align with long-term sustainability
and industrial transition objectives.
Water Servicing
The Precinct is well positioned within Hunter Water’s regional supply system, with Grahamstown Water
Treatment Plant providing 246 ML/d of capacity and forecast spare capacity of ~17.5 ML/d in 2029,
exceeding the Precinct’s ultimate extreme day demand of ~16 ML/d. This demonstrates that, at a regional
scale, potable water supply is not a limiting factor to development.
However, constraints exist at a local level, with limited existing infrastructure across the site, fragmented
connections and the need to establish new trunk mains. These constraints are most pronounced in the
southern and western areas. The presence of substantial on-site stored water and proximity to recycled
water sources presents a key opportunity to supplement potable demand, enabling precinct-scale
alternative water systems (e.g. recycled water, raw water use). This creates a pathway to reduce
reliance on trunk augmentations and support a more resilient, lower-cost servicing strategy over time.
Wastewater Servicing
Wastewater represents one of the most significant constraints to development. The majority of the Precinct is
not connected to the Hunter Water wastewater network and historically relied on septic systems. This will
require the delivery of entirely new infrastructure including pump stations and rising mains to connect to
Edgeworth Wastewater Treatment Works.
Critically, Edgeworth WWTW is projected to reach capacity by 2029, with no confirmed timing or funding for
upgrades. This introduces a material risk to staging and delivery, as development is partially dependent on
external infrastructure upgrades.
At a precinct scale, this constraint strongly aligns with opportunities for decentralised servicing, including
on-site wastewater treatment, water recycling and reuse systems. These approaches could reduce reliance
on regional infrastructure in early stages and unlock development ahead of major augmentation works.
Electricity Servicing
Electricity is a major enabling service for the Precinct. The site has access to multiple voltage levels (11 kV
to 330 kV) and is located near key infrastructure including Newcastle BSP and Argenton Zone Substation.

--- Page 4 [Matched: ['power', 'potable water', 'gas', 'telecommunications']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
Gas and Energy Strategy
There is currently no gas infrastructure within the Precinct, with the nearest connection point located at
Racecourse Road and Blair Street. This limits feasibility for widespread gas reticulation, particularly in
western areas where lead-in distances are significant.
Rather than a constraint, this creates a strategic opportunity to minimise reliance on gas and prioritise
electrification and alternative energy systems, including renewable generation, battery storage and
emerging fuels such as hydrogen. Gas is therefore best treated as a targeted, case-by-case utility for
specific users rather than a core servicing backbone.
Telecommunications
Telecommunications is not a fundamental constraint to development but requires staged delivery. Baseline
mobile coverage is strong, and connections can be established from existing infrastructure at Powerhouse
Road and The Broadway.
Demand remains difficult to quantify at this stage, particularly given the potential for high-data users such as
data centres. The servicing approach relies on a developer-led NBN model, with pit and pipe infrastructure
delivered upfront and fibre rolled out incrementally.
Opportunities exist to develop a high-quality digital precinct, with scalable fibre networks, redundancy, and
integration of smart infrastructure. Additional mobile towers and bespoke connections for large data users
may be required as demand grows.
Key Cross-Cutting Constraint: Cockle Creek
Cockle Creek represents a major physical and servicing constraint across the Precinct. The creek creates a
barrier between the site and existing infrastructure networks, requiring new crossings for potable water,
wastewater and other utilities.
While feasible, these crossings introduce significant cost, delivery complexity and approval risk,
particularly where trenchless methods are required. This constraint reinforces the importance of staged
delivery and supports the case for decentralised servicing strategies, particularly in early development
phases.
Overall Conclusion
The assessment confirms that the Precinct can be serviced through a combination of traditional
infrastructure augmentation and staged delivery aligned with development uptake. While constraints such as
wastewater capacity, legacy infrastructure and Cockle Creek present challenges, they are not prohibitive to
development and instead provide a clear framework for infrastructure planning.
Importantly, many of these constraints align with and reinforce the opportunity to adopt alternative
servicing approaches, including decentralised water systems, electrification, renewable energy integration
and scalable telecommunications infrastructure.

Introduction
This Utilities Engineering Report (this Report) has been prepared by Aurecon on behalf of the Department
Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI) to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie Coal
Complex for Post Mining Land Use. The Macquarie Coal Complex Land Transformation Precinct (the
Precinct) forms part of the NSW Government’s investigation into future land use pathways for mining
affected land.
The Report outlines a forward-looking servicing strategy for the Macquarie Coal Complex, positioning the
Precinct to leverage strong regional utility connectivity and inherent site advantages. The site benefits from
proximity to established electricity infrastructure with access to multiple voltage levels and significant
available capacity, enabling both staged development and accommodation of high-demand industrial users
over time. In addition, the presence of substantial on-site stored water and access to nearby treatment
facilities presents a unique opportunity to integrate alternative water sources, supporting more sustainable
and resilient servicing outcomes as the Precinct evolves.
These advantages are complemented by the site’s long history of mining and industrial use, which has
shaped the existing utility environment. Legacy infrastructure, including fragmented potable water
connections and limited wastewater servicing, reflects its former operational requirements, with some areas
reliant on isolated or off-grid systems. While this presents constraints in its current form, it also provides a
clear framework for targeted upgrades and strategic planning, enabling the Precinct to transition from a
legacy mining landscape into a well-serviced, diversified employment and mixed-use hub.
The following areas are herein referred to as the site and form the subject area under assessment in this
report.
Figure 1-1 Precinct Site Boundary

3
 Given the fluid nature of planning and utility services, the advice provided is only applicable at the time of
preparing the report and must be revised over time.
The following general assumptions are noted, specific to this report:
 This assessment undertaken is high level and strategic in nature to identify key constraints, opportunities,
and risks that have a material impact on the development potential of the Precinct. High level
assessments were undertaken however no detailed modelling was undertaken.
 Engineering design services beyond the high-level capacity assessment are excluded from this
assessment.
 No intrusive or invasive site investigations have been undertaken for this report, any investigations are
limited to a review of available desktop information only.
 The advice provided herein remains subject to authority confirmation.

the key servicing opportunities and constraints relevant to future planning.

Due to the large extents of the Precinct, the current conditions, opportunities, constraints, and risks of the
potable water network will be broken down into three separate areas:
 Main Precinct Study Area,
 Teralba Southgate Study Area, and
 Wakefield Study Area.
See Figure 2-3.
Figure 2-3 Potable Water and Wastewater Areas of Investigation
Main Precinct Study Area
The northern section of the Precinct is supplied via two separate DN150 potable water pipelines. To the east,
a DN150 crosses Cockle Creek from Boolaroo and runs through the study area, crossing the railway loop
and running along The Weir Road into Barnsley. At the western side of the site, a DN150 runs along The
Broadway and Wakefield Road between Barnsley and Killingworth, this pipeline currently services the
existing site office off The Broadway. The southern section of the Precinct is largely isolated from the potable
water network. The existing potable water network is shown in Figure 2-2.
Teralba Southgate Study Area
The Teralba Southgate study area is currently serviced by Hunter Water’s potable water network via an
unknown pipe size which branches off a DN150 along York Street. The network within the site is unknown.
The surrounding streets have multiple potable water pipelines ranging from DN150-600. The existing potable
water network is shown in Figure 2-2.

--- Page 17 [Matched: ['potable water', 'recycled water', 'hunter water']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
9
Wakefield Study Area
The Wakefield site has no connection to the Hunter Water network, nor does it have any Hunter Water
assets near the site. There are no other known sources of potable water in this area, and it is known that the
nearby residential properties are off grid for their water supply.
Opportunities
The projected spare capacity at Grahamstown Water Treatment Plant through to 2034 presents an
opportunity for the Precinct to leverage some of this spare capacity to service the proposed development.
The Precinct benefits from having existing infrastructure to the north, Killingworth, Barnsley and Edgeworth,
and to the east, Boolaroo and Argenton. This provides an opportunity to tap into these networks to reduce
the need for significant infrastructure investment, this is dependent on the spare capacity within these
networks which is unknown at this stage. The existing pipeline crossings from Boolaroo, DN315 and DN600,
from which the DN150 through the Precinct branches off from, presents a potential opportunity to connect
into one of the larger pipes to utilise the existing crossings of Cockle Creek.
The Teralba Southgate study area is well positioned for future potable water growth needs, the site has an
existing connecting into the Hunter Water network and is located in close proximity to larger pipelines if
larger volumes of water are required.
There is an opportunity to reduce the reliance on the potable water network by utilising nearby raw or
recycled water sources to supplement potable water supply where land uses do not require potable quality
water. Recycled water from Edgeworth Wastewater Treatment Works presents a potential supply
opportunity, however, this should be further investigated to understand water quality and volume. Waratah
Golf Course in Argenton currently uses effluent flow from the plant to irrigate their land. The large volumes of
stored water onsite also present an opportunity to reduce potable water requirements, again the quality of
water will need to be further investigated to determine if this option is feasible. Both of these opportunities
are dependent on the proposed land uses for the Precinct.
Constraints and Risks
A large risk to the potable water servicing of the Precinct is the unknown capacity within the Hunter Water
potable water transfer networks. At this stage, assumptions have been made as to feasible connection
points, however, these assumptions are susceptible to change once greater engagement with Hunter Water
is undertaken to better understand the potable water network in the surrounding area.
There are large areas of the Precinct that are not currently serviced by Hunter Water, nor are located near
an existing network. These areas include Wakefield study area and the southern section of the main
Precinct. The barriers to connect these areas into the network will be greater and may restrict or delay their
development potential.

2.2
Electricity
This section provides a strategic, desktop-based assessment of existing electrical infrastructure, constraints
and opportunities within the Precinct. It also provides recommended actions for post-exhibition phases. The
assessment considers nearby transmission and distribution infrastructure, potential connection options,
spare network capacity, likely upgrade requirements and the broader regional energy projects. Table 2-5
provides details of the following:
 The existing electricity servicing conditions across the Precinct.
 Overview of opportunities to connect to existing electrical infrastructure.
 Key constraints and risks associated with electricity servicing.
 Recommended actions before and after exhibition to support future precinct planning and development.
A summary of connection levels and their associated risk ratings is provided in Table 2-6 (located toward the
end of Section 2.2).
Table 2-5 Electricity Analysis Summary
Electricity
Description
Current conditions
 There is sufficient electrical infrastructure to connect to up to the 330kV voltage level at
all three “High Development Potential” sites. The remaining areas identified as “further
investigation required” have a similar burden.
 There also exists several options for connection to nearby 11kV, 33kV and 132kV
voltage levels, depending on ultimate load and staging plan. “Future investigation” site

--- Page 23 [Matched: ['electrical', '330kv', '132kv', '33kv', 'substation', 'ausgrid', 'transgrid']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
15
Electricity
Description
− Hunter Transmission Project (HTP): 110km of new 500kV transmission lines, which
will support up to 8GW of transmission in the Hunter region.
 The CEP provides 5800 new jobs created in the warehousing / logistics hub which
represents opportunities to provide complementary industries or infrastructure in the
Precinct Master Plan.
 Newcastle Substation is a key node in the 330kV network for the Hunter region. With
more renewable energy projects coming online, there is potential for the site to provide
REZ support by connecting into this 330kV backbone. This can come in the form of
supporting infrastructure such as BESS or substations, and ancillary equipment in the
Precinct Master Plan.
Constraints and risk
 To accommodate a load of >100MVA, the proponent will have to look to connect into
the 132kV or 330kV bus of Newcastle Substation. This would result in up to 8km of
additional transmission, depending on proximity to the electricity asset.
 11 and 33kV connection options are limited by bay availability, ability for expansion at
the respective substations, and upstream network availability (i.e. feeder and
transformer ratings / utilisation). Consultation with relevant NSPs is required to
understand feasibility of each connection.
 Large loads (>100 MVA) likely require connection to 132 kV or 330 kV; limited by spare
bay availability and upstream infrastructure. Connection to 330 kV Newcastle
Substation requires ∼ 8 km of transmission lines with multiple crossings.
 Consultation with Transgrid/Ausgrid required to confirm feasibility and network
upgrades.
Recommendations
Post-exhibition and as
development
progresses
 Consult with Ausgrid and Transgrid for substation availability and connection
requirements.
 Undertake detailed electrical infrastructure assessment to estimate HV network
capacity.

2.4.1
Gas (Jemena)
Current Conditions
There is no existing gas supply within the study area. The closest Jemema gas main to the site is at the
corner of Racecourse Road and Blair Street south east of the site. This 160mm main (operating at 210kPa)
could be extended to the site to provide supply for new customers. Gas is a non-essential service and new

--- Page 26 [Matched: ['electrical', 'gas']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
18
Figure 2-6 Jemena Newcastle Services Map (Jemena, 2023)
Opportunities
Future gas connection could be made to the Blair Street location for the site. It would be encouraged to
locate future gas users to the eastern side of the Precinct to capitalise on proximity to this connection point.
The lack of gas does help reinforce exploration of non-gas related and renewable sources of energy within
the precinct.
The Teralba Southgate site has good access to gas and could be considered for development of this site.
Constraints
Heavy gas users locating in the western portion of the site may find lead-in works more challenging or
commercially unviable without sufficient critical mass.
The majority of precinct users will likely need to connect to all energy through electrification creating a higher
burden on electrical usage.
2.4.2
Fuel (Ampol)
Current Conditions
A high-pressure liquid petroleum products pipeline crosses the northern portion of the Precinct. Based on the
current utilities mapping, the pipeline generally follows the northern part of the study area along The
Broadway, The Weir Road and through adjoining bushland. The pipeline is owned by Newcastle Pipeline
Company Pty Ltd and operated by Ampol Petroleum Pty Ltd. It forms part of the Sydney-to-Newcastle fuel
supply system, carrying refined petroleum products from Ampol’s Banksmeadow Fuels Terminal to fuel
terminals between Sydney and Newcastle. Ampol’s BYDA response identifies that the pipeline transfers
approximately 4 billion litres of fuel each year, reducing the need for approximately 45,000 road tanker
movements per year between Sydney and Newcastle.
The pipeline is a licensed pipeline under the Pipelines Act 1967 (NSW) and is subject to operation and
maintenance requirements under AS 2885.3:2022: Pipelines: Gas and Liquid Petroleum - Operation and

--- Page 28 [Matched: ['power', 'telecommunications']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
20
design and future staging. The pipeline corridor could potentially be integrated as a linear open space,
access, landscape or infrastructure corridor where it remains accessible, unencumbered by structures and
designed in accordance with Ampol requirements.
Early coordination with Ampol may also allow the pipeline corridor to be spatially resolved in the structure
plan, reducing later uncertainty for developers and infrastructure designers. Future road and service
crossings should be rationalised to minimise the number of pipeline interfaces and to ensure any crossings
are perpendicular, constructible and capable of being approved.
Constraints
The pipeline is a material constraint to land use planning within the northern portion of the Precinct.
Development should avoid placing sensitive or high-occupancy uses within the pipeline measurement length
unless a Land Use Change Safety Management Study confirms that the risk profile is acceptable and that
any required mitigation measures can be implemented. Sensitive land uses such as schools, childcare,
hospitals, aged care and major residential development are likely to require specific assessment if located
within the relevant AS 2885.6 measurement length.
Any works with potential vibration impact within 20 m require Ampol supervision and further assessment.
Recommended Activities
The following are recommended as the project progresses.
 Confirm the registered easement, surveyed pipeline alignment, depth of cover and associated title
restrictions. A 20m easement is registered on title the exact pipeline location within the easement should
be surveyed as DA’s progress.
 Liaise with Ampol to confirm applicable DA planning referral requirements, including the relevant AS
2885.6 measurement length for the pipeline section through the study area. Referral to Ampol will be
automatically enacted for any DA’s that approach/cross or intersect the easement or pipeline for Ampol
approval.
 Formalise a pipeline constraint overlay showing the easement, 5 m structure exclusion zone, 20 m works
/ vibration referral zone, 30 m blasting approval zone and any confirmed AS 2885.6 land use change
referral area. Zones to be confirmed with Ampol.
 Avoid sensitive or high-occupancy land uses within the confirmed measurement length unless supported
by a Land Use Change Safety Management Study.
 Locate buildings, high-value assets, stormwater basins, major earthworks and permanent stockpiles
outside the easement and minimum exclusion areas.
 Rationalise roads and utility crossings to minimise interfaces with the pipeline and ensure crossings are
perpendicular and capable of meeting Ampol’s separation and approval requirements.

27
High Water Consumers
The demand assessment has been based on Hunter Water’s adopted demand rates which do not account
for high water consumers. Any developments with significant water usage, such as data centres, will require
case-by-case investigations to determine if they can be supported by Hunter Water and the Precinct’s
potable water network. These assessments should also consider suitable non-potable water sources to
reduce demand to the potable water network.
3.2.2
Proposed Infrastructure
The Precinct sits within, or in close proximity to, Hunter Water’s Elermore Vale 1 Reservoir Supply Zone
within the South Wallsend and West Lake Macquarie DSP Area. This area is serviced by the Grahamstown
Water Treatment Plant, which has a total treatment capacity of 246 ML/d and is forecast to have 17.5 ML/d of
spare capacity in 2029, see Section 2.1.1. This available capacity would be sufficient to service the entire
Precinct and Teralba Southgate under a peak day demand scenario of 15.3 ML/d, but would not quite be
able to service an extreme day demand scenario of 17.6 ML/d. Excluding Teralba Southgate from the
equation enables the Precinct to be serviced by Grahamstown Water Treatment Plant at an extreme day
demand. It is noted that the Precinct is expected to be delivered in stages, meaning that the requirement for
these flows will likely come into effect incrementally and there will not be a need to service the entire flow
during Stage 1. These initial figures do not consider the addition of other large developments which may be
proposed in the area; however, standard population growth is accounted for in Hunter Water’s numbers.
Hunter Water’s Elermore Vale 1 Reservoir Supply Zone is serviced by two reservoirs which were found to
have a combined total of 110 ML of storage, based on desktop investigations. Elermore Vale 1 Reservoirs
have a single inlet/outlet DN900 main. For the purpose of understanding high level capacity within the supply
zone, it is assumed that the reservoirs are filling for half the day and emptying for the other half at a velocity
of 1.1 m/s which gives a volume of approximately 30 ML per day of flow into the network. Based on these
high level calculations, it is assumed that this reservoir system has spare capacity to service the
development of the Precinct, this assumption is to be confirmed with Hunter Water as the development
progresses.
A formal development application to Hunter Water will be required to confirm the proposed servicing
arrangements. Potable Water demands will continue to evolve as greater clarity is reached on development
types and as the Precinct Master Plan is refined. As planning develops, if Grahamstown Water Treatment
Plant has no spare capacity, it is assumed that Hunter Water would plan upgrades to the facility to meet
growth needs.
All proposed potable water pipelines will follow the existing road corridors and alignments of proposed roads,

This eastern section of the precinct is currently serviced by a Hunter Water DN150 potable water pipeline.

connection off the DN600 is assumed to be feasible, however, consultation with Hunter Water is required to
determine if there is spare capacity in the network to enable this connection. This proposed main would then
cross through the middle of the site and the reticulation network would branch off to service properties. The
proposed servicing strategy is shown in Figure 3-2.
An alternative option is the construction of an onsite reservoir which would fill during low periods and require
a smaller sized pipeline connection into the existing network.

Option 1
The first option connects into an existing DN500 pipeline in Edgeworth, on Main Road between Corden
Street and Garth Street. A new pipeline is then proposed to run within road verges to Area 2 where it would
branch out into Area 2 and down to Area 3. It is noted that the spare capacity within this pipeline is unknown
and the feasibility of this option is to be determined through consultation with Hunter Water.
Figure 3-3 Western Precinct Option 1 Proposed Potable Water Servicing Strategy and Pipe Sizing
Option 2
An alternate servicing strategy, Option 2, utilises the existing DN150 pipeline which runs along The
Broadway to flow into a new proposed reservoir located within Area 2. The reservoir would be located at a
high point and fill during low demand periods within the network. The reticulation network would then be
gravity fed into the surrounding areas from the reservoir. Further investigation is required to determine if a
booster pump station is required to transport flows from the existing pipeline up to the reservoir. Consultation
with Hunter Water is required to determine if the existing pipeline has capacity to service these areas via a
new reservoir and potential booster pump station. This option seeks to use a ‘trickle feed’ to constantly fill up
a new reservoir. This would allow the existing infrastructure surrounding the site to be used and the
difference in Option 1 and 2 then becomes the cost and logistics associated with a new lead-in along
Northville Drive vs a new reservoir and pump station within the site. Both options are viable subject to further
consultation with Hunter Water.

Wakefield Study Area
The Wakefield site is currently identified as environmentally constrained and therefore does not require a
potable water connection. Given its isolated location and the absence of a nearby potable water network,
any future need for potable water would likely need to be managed on site. The site does not fall within a
Hunter Water supply zone, and the infrastructure required to extend the network to this area would be
significant. As such, a substantial and clearly justified water demand would be required to warrant
connection to the Hunter Water system.
3.2.3
Infrastructure Staging
For both potable water and wastewater the proposed staging is similar, however, as there is no existing
wastewater infrastructure within the precinct, excluding Area 4 at Teralba Southgate, there is a higher barrier
of entry to implement wastewater infrastructure.
Proposed infrastructure staging for potable water is shown in Figure 3-6.

Stage 1
The areas with or near existing potable water infrastructure are earmarked for earlier servicing due to the
lower barriers required to supply these sites. This includes; the northern sections of the Precinct, Areas 1
and 2, and Area 4 in Teralba Southgate.
Stage 2
Area 3 is advised to be developed later due to its isolated position and thus the high investment required to
service the area. The development of this site has the opportunity to happen sooner if the site is serviced by
a decentralised, off-grid system, this will reduce the high investment barrier if the development has low
demand.
The servicing of Area 5 is also suggested to be a Stage 2 investment due to it being a standalone system
and the investigations required to determine if it can link into the existing network.

3.3.2
Proposed Infrastructure
Current wastewater connectivity into the Hunter Water network is limited to the Teralba Southgate area, with
the broader Precinct being unconnected but located within close proximity to Hunter Water's existing
network. Two wastewater treatment plants service the broader Precinct, the Teralba Southgate area feeds
into Toronto WWTW and the Precinct is surrounded by sewer system areas which flow into Edgeworth
WWTW. As such the Precinct has been divided into three distinct areas for proposed wastewater servicing,
these areas will have common routes to take wastewater to the associated wastewater treatment plants.
A formal development application to Hunter Water will be required to confirm the proposed servicing
arrangements. Wastewater demands will continue to evolve as greater clarity is reached on development
types and as the Precinct Master Plan is refined.
All proposed rising mains will follow the existing road and alignments of proposed roads, where possible.
The gravity reticulation pipeline network is to be determined in line with development, alignments within road
corridors will be prioritised but it is noted that this configuration is not always feasible, and the pipelines may
be required to pass through developable land to meet flow requirements. The pipelines should be allocated a
location within the road corridor, along with the other utility services. It is noted that servicing plans only show
trunk infrastructure with the reticulation network to be designed in line with development within the Precinct.
The Precinct Study Area
The Precinct is not currently serviced by Hunter Water’s wastewater network and as such does not fall within
an existing sewer system area. To the north, east and west of the site, wastewater flows are pumped to
Edgeworth Wastewater Treatment Works. Edgeworth WWTW is proposed to receive wastewater flows from
the Precinct, the plant is much larger and closer to the site than Toronto WWTW; which services the Teralba
Southgate area .
Edgeworth Wastewater Treatment Works has a current treatment capacity of 70,000 EP (14ML/d), it is
expected to hit this capacity by 2029. Upgrades are planned for the site, but timing is unknown and there is
no allocated funding at this stage. As Edgeworth WWTW is nearing capacity, it is assumed that Hunter
Water will plan upgrades to the facility to meet growth needs in the region.
Due to the large size of the Precinct and the distribution of the developable areas, the site was split into two
distinct sections for the purpose of wastewater servicing strategies. Both sides of the site are proposed to be
serviced by a series of pump stations feeding into one major pump station which will carry flows, via a rising
main, to Edgeworth WWTW. This configuration is in keeping with the standard strategy which has been
utilised in the surrounding suburbs.
Eastern Precinct

--- Page 47 [Matched: ['electrical', 'sewer']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
39
The major pump station for the east is proposed be located at the northern boundary of the site, adjacent to
The Weir Road. A rising main is then proposed to run along The Weir Road and then turn to the north and
run parallel to the electrical easement up to Cockle Creek. All pump stations are located at low points with
the reticulation network proposed to take gravity flows to these locations.

184.85 L/s of wastewater flow. A flow rate of two and a half times the PDWF and a pipe velocity of 1.5 m/s,
were used to find the required pipe sizing and found the main rising main from the site would need to be a

Proposed Servicing
Area 5 is proposed to be serviced separately by connecting into the gravity network at Charlton Street and

Areas 2 and 3 are proposed to be serviced via a series of five pump stations and associated rising mains.
These will service five catchment areas, and the rising mains will take flows to a major pump station to

are located at low points and with the reticulation network proposed to take gravity flows to these locations.
