and the investment to connect this area into the Hunter Water network would be significant. The area has
been included in the servicing noting that there is a significant opportunity to service this site as an off grid
system to reduce the barrier of connecting it into the wider Hunter Water network.

3.4
Electricity Servicing Strategy
The Electricity Servicing Strategy, as summarised in this section, outlines the impacts to electricity supply
infrastructure, and sets out what is needed to ensure the timely delivery of key electricity infrastructure
upgrades for the Precinct and Teralba Southgate, while ensuring existing customers in the surround areas
continue to be serviced as well.
Electricity supply infrastructure will be designed and constructed in accordance with Ausgrid and Transgrid’s
technical, planning and regulatory requirements. The proposed Precinct is located adjacent to the Argenton
132/33/11 kV Zone Substation (ZS), owned and operated by Ausgrid. This substation is downstream of
Transgrid’s 330/132 kV Newcastle Bulk Supply Point (BSP), located approximately 6 km to the west. Figure
3-13 (see next page) provides a geographic diagram showing the location of Ausgrid and Transgrid’s
existing infrastructure.

Figure 3-14 Surrounding Transmission Infrastructure
Based on Ausgrid’s Rosetta Data Portal, the Argenton ZS has residual capacity of up to ~30 MVA at on the
11 kV bus. This capacity may accommodate part of the Precinct development until upgrade thresholds are
reached. As this capacity is progressively utilised, the installation of additional 11 kV feeders, each with a
typical capacity of approximately ~5 MVA, will likely be required to service incremental load. There may be
opportunity to utilise existing 11kV feeders already throughout the site, however, the residual capacity of
these assets is not known.
Beyond ~30 MVA, moderate to major upgrades may be required at Argenton ZS to increase the transfer
capacity. This may include the addition of a new 132/11kV transformer and / or additional 132kV incoming
feeder via Newcastle BSP.
It is noted that ~400 MW of load capacity is available at the 132 kV busbar at Transgrid’s Newcastle Bulk
Supply Point. The load transfer capability at this site is constrained by the three existing 330/132 kV
transformers. Additional load may trigger the installation of a fourth 330/132 kV transformer, noting that
provision for space and switchgear is already available.
3.4.3
Proposed Infrastructure and Staging
From the expected electricity demand of approximately 97 MVA and 176 MVA for a low and high case
(Table 3-22), respectively, infrastructure upgrades will be required to unlock the Precinct’s ultimate forecast
electrical demand. Note that long-term energy needs may evolve over time, and the Precinct’s demand
requirements may track towards either the low or high demand scenario.
Large Energy Consumers
Large energy consumers such as data centres, smelters and heavy industries, should be located in the
north-west section of the Precinct to leverage proximity to Newcastle BSP, whilst immediate / short term
small loads can leverage the existing Argenton ZS. This approach aligns with high water and energy use
requirements and supports efficient resource management across the Precinct.

--- Page 60 [Matched: ['substation', 'ausgrid', 'transgrid']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
52
Network Upgrades
For loads exceeding ~30 MVA, moderate to major substation upgrades are likely required, which would allow
up to ~60 MVA of load. This is likely to include an additional 132/11kV transformer and relevant switchgear
upgrades. Extension or additional 11kV feeders into the Precinct are also likely to be required. Cockle Creek
(Area 1), which is intended to accommodate heavy industrial land uses (as outlined in Table 3-22), benefits
from its proximity to the Argenton ZS. This may reduce the distance and extent of network augmentation
required to service demand from this area.
For loads in the range of approximately 60-90 MVA or greater (the limit at Argenton ZS), the development of
one or more new 132/11 kV zone substations, each with a capacity of ~60-90 MVA may be required to
supply the ultimate Precinct load. These substations would likely be supplied from Transgrid’s Newcastle
BSP. The availability of ~400 MW of capacity at the 132 kV busbar has the potential to support this upgrade,
noting that this may trigger the need for a fourth 330/132 kV transformer – depending on investment timing
and potential competing loads in the region. Killingworth (Area 2) in the north-west sections and located
closer to the Newcastle BSP may benefit from reduced transmission connection distances and
corresponding reduction in the extent of network augmentation required to establish supply.
Another potential option includes building an additional 132 kV feeder between the Newcastle BSP and
Argenton ZS. However, this approach may be constrained by technical and / or space limitations at both
Argenton ZS and Newcastle BSP. Further, this upgrade is expected to be highly challenging as it would
require substantial new easements and corridors.
Teralba Southgate in the southern section is anticipated to be supplied via existing network with 1-2 new 11
kV feeders from the nearest zone substation.
New connections into the network are assessed on a case-by-case basis in collaboration with Ausgrid and
Transgrid and subject to capacity within the existing network.
Figure 3-15 shows the proposed infrastructure staging for electricity network upgrades. A summary of the
staging of required upgrades to Ausgrid and Transgrid’s network is outlined in Table 3-25 further below.

3.4.4
Constraints and Opportunities
Constraints
Development of transmission infrastructure within the Precinct must consider existing on-site infrastructure
assets, including not encroaching on exclusion zones around transmission lines and associated structures.
These include access and earthing, which may limit where infrastructure can be located.
For example, for transmission lines operating above 132kV, the following exclusion zones apply for
Transgrid assets:
 Access exclusion zones are within 20m from any part of structure.
 Earthing exclusion zones are within 30m from any part of structure.
 Centre exclusion zones are within 17m either side from centre of the transmission line.
Figure 3-16 illustrates exclusion zone requirements within Transgrid’s easements.
Figure 3-16 Transgrid’s Easement Requirements for 220 kV and Above
Additionally, new network infrastructure will require dedicated easements to accommodate lines, substations
and access. These easements may need to be incorporated into the Precinct layout and may reduce
developable area or constrain land configuration. Early planning for these corridors is encouraged to avoid
re-work.
Additionally, there is a long-term constraint associated with the potential need for a new zone substation
within the Precinct. The siting of a ZS will need to be considered as part of the development footprint,
including allowance for:
 Adequate land area and suitably topography
 Access for construction and ongoing maintenance
 Integration with existing and future network connections
 Appropriate buffers to surround land uses (e.g. noise, safety, visual considerations)
The inclusion of a ZS may have implications for land use zoning and the overall arrangement of development
within the Precinct. As such, early planning of a suitable site is recommended.
All development within easements is subject to the requirements and approvals of the relevant network
service provider.
Opportunities
While the servicing strategy for the Precinct has been set to meet satisfactory performance electricity
reliability standards, there are still several opportunities to improve the electricity servicing of the expected
development and particularly the sustainability of the Precinct development. These opportunities include:
 On-site renewable energy generation and/or coordinated microgrid could offset the need for some

--- Page 63 [Matched: ['electrical', 'ausgrid', 'transgrid', 'gas']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
55
potential changes to the suggested utilities infrastructure scenarios will need to be determined following
thorough assessment and sizing of potential renewable energy options.
 Transport electrification infrastructure – enabling the transition to electric, hybrid and alternative-fuel
freight vehicles supports both environmental sustainability and electrical load management within the
Precinct. Opportunities to integrate smart charging and energy management systems should also be
explored to optimise demand profiles and align with on-site renewable energy generation. Future-proofing
the development for charging and refuelling infrastructure will enable staged deployment as demand
grows.
 Implementation of a community battery arrangement into the Precinct may provide a level of self-supply
during outages, but more importantly help reduce electricity billing costs for end-users in the Precinct.
This concept could provide resilience to the Precinct by providing islanding capability during outages. The
batteries should be able to operate and supply at least a portion of energy to the Precinct during outages.
 It is noted that development activity is anticipated in the surrounding area, including densification around
train stations. It is expected that growth in the area may expedite the timing of required infrastructure
upgrades.
3.4.5
Planning framework and control recommendations
Further consultation with Ausgrid and Transgrid will be required to facilitate planning of the proposed
upgrades in the Precinct. The final servicing strategy will be informed by Ausgrid and Transgrid’s technical,
planning and regulatory requirements.
It is recommended that the following controls are applied to developments within the Precinct to meet
development requirements and enhance the efficiency of the electricity servicing network:
 Smart pole network infrastructure (incorporating street lighting, public Wi-Fi and CCTV technology)
 On-site rooftop solar panel systems
 Community battery network

3.6
Telecommunication Servicing Strategy
Minor network augmentations are generally required to meet future demand and support development,
including potential augmentation to the nearby fibre access nodes, underground pit and pipe and ducting
works. However, these works are generally reactive and targeted as formal customer requests are
submitted. They have minimal implications to be considered when developing the Precinct Master Plan as
works are generally within the existing and proposed road network.
3.6.1
Demand Assessment
At this stage of planning, telecommunications and data demand across the Precinct is inherently difficult to
quantify with accuracy, as ultimate requirements are highly dependent on the mix of future land uses and
tenant profiles, particularly where high-demand users such as data centres, advanced manufacturing or
logistics hubs may be introduced. While baseline mobile coverage and proximity to existing fibre networks
provide a strong foundation, detailed demand profiles will evolve over time in response to specific
development proposals and technology requirements, and are therefore typically addressed progressively as
part of subdivision and development staging.
For greenfield precincts and subdivisions, connection to the National Broadband Network (NBN) generally
follows a developer-led process, where the proponent engages directly with NBN Co to deliver pit and pipe
infrastructure in accordance with NBN standards and design guidelines. This involves preparing a
telecommunications servicing design, constructing the passive network (conduits, pits and pathways) as part
of civil works, and coordinating installation of active fibre infrastructure by NBN Co once development
thresholds are met. The process is typically aligned with subdivision approvals and staged development, with
costs and delivery arrangements varying depending on development scale, staging and required service
levels.
Notwithstanding the above, access to both the Telstra copper system and NBN (through fibre or satellite) are

--- Page 66 [Matched: ['power', 'telecommunications']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Utilities Engineering Report - Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
58
Active telecommunications infrastructure, including fibre optic cabling, exchanges, and network upgrades,
would be delivered incrementally by service providers such as NBN Co, Telstra or other carriers in response
to confirmed development demand. Large-scale or high-demand users, such as data centres or advanced
industrial facilities, may require dedicated fibre connections or bespoke network solutions. At a precinct
scale, additional infrastructure such as mobile towers, fibre backhaul upgrades and network redundancy may
also be introduced over time to support reliability, capacity and future digital requirements, though these are
typically reactive investments aligned with occupancy and user needs rather than upfront constraints on
development.
The Precinct currently benefits from generally strong baseline 4G and 5G mobile coverage supported by
existing telecommunications infrastructure in the surrounding area. However, the strategic scale and
diversity of proposed land uses, including industrial, logistics and potential high-demand digital users, are
likely to increase data traffic and network load over time.
As development intensifies, there may be a need for additional mobile towers or small cell installations within
the Precinct to maintain service quality, improve coverage in more remote or topographically constrained
areas, and meet increasing capacity demands. This is particularly relevant for large-format industrial estates
where building footprints, equipment and operational activities can impact signal propagation. The
implementation of additional mobile infrastructure would typically occur in response to demonstrated demand
and be delivered in coordination with telecommunications carriers, with opportunities to integrate towers into
the broader precinct design where possible.
Large-scale data users, such as data centres or high-intensity digital infrastructure, would be treated on a
case-by-case basis due to their unique and often significant bandwidth, latency and resiliency requirements.
These users are typically expected to deliver their own dedicated fibre connections and backhaul
infrastructure directly to major network nodes or exchanges, rather than relying solely on the shared precinct
network. This approach ensures that their operational requirements can be met without placing undue
demand on the broader precinct telecommunications infrastructure, while also allowing bespoke solutions
aligned with global standards for redundancy, security and performance.
3.6.3
Infrastructure Staging
Initial NBN staging for the Precinct would follow a phased, developer-led rollout aligned with early
development areas and existing network proximity:
 Stage 1 enabling connections
Initial connections would be established from existing telecommunications corridors at Powerhouse Road
(east) and The Broadway (west), which represent the nearest viable tie-in points to existing fibre and

4
Conclusion
This Utilities Engineering Report demonstrates that the Macquarie Coal Complex Precinct can be effectively
serviced to support its transition to a post-mining land use, providing a strong foundation for the proposed
rezoning. The assessment confirms that, despite legacy constraints associated with historic mining
operations and fragmented infrastructure, the Precinct benefits from key enabling factors including strong
regional electricity connectivity, proximity to existing utility networks, and access to substantial on-site water
resources. These attributes allow for a feasible “business as usual” servicing pathway to be established,
demonstrating that the Precinct can be developed in a manner consistent with standard authority
requirements and development servicing frameworks.
Two of the most significant constraints to servicing the Precinct relate to the absence of existing wastewater
infrastructure and the physical barrier presented by Cockle Creek.
A key constraint is that the majority of the Precinct, is not currently connected to the Hunter Water
wastewater network and historically relied on septic systems during its mining operations. This means that
any future development will require the delivery of entirely new wastewater infrastructure, including pump
stations and rising mains, to connect to regional treatment facilities such as Edgeworth Wastewater
Treatment Works. While the site benefits from proximity to this plant, it is already forecast to reach capacity
by 2029, with upgrades planned but not yet confirmed or funded. This creates both a timing and delivery risk,
with development potentially constrained by the need for significant upfront infrastructure investment and
reliance on external authority upgrades.
In addition, Cockle Creek represents a major physical and servicing barrier across the Precinct. The creek
effectively separates the site from existing utility networks and requires new infrastructure crossings to
enable both potable water and wastewater connections. While such crossings are technically feasible, they
introduce increased cost, complexity and approval requirements, particularly where trenchless construction
methods or environmental considerations are required. As a result, Cockle Creek not only influences the cost
and feasibility of traditional servicing solutions, but also reinforces the need to consider alternative
approaches such as decentralised systems, staged servicing, or precinct-scale infrastructure to reduce
reliance on crossing this constraint in the early stages of development.
Importantly, the report also identifies that the Precinct is uniquely positioned to move beyond traditional
servicing approaches and explore more innovative, precinct-scale solutions. Opportunities such as
alternative water supply, decentralised systems, renewable energy integration, electrification, and scalable
telecommunications infrastructure provide a pathway to enhance resilience, reduce infrastructure burdens,
and align with broader sustainability and decarbonisation objectives. In this context, the proposed servicing

Executive summary
This Geotechnical Engineering Report has been prepared to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie
Coal Complex (MCC) (the Precinct), and Teralba Southgate for post-mining land use. The report provides a
precinct-scale assessment of geotechnical conditions, constraints and risks, and establishes a risk-informed
framework to guide land use planning, infrastructure provision, and staging.
The Precinct and Teralba Southgate comprises a highly modified post-mining landscape characterised by
variable topography, anthropogenic ground conditions, and legacy mining impacts, including mine
subsidence risk, disturbed terrains, a Tailings Storage Facility (TSF) and areas of potentially deep alluvium.
These conditions result in spatially variable development constraints across the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate.
A multi-criteria geotechnical assessment has been undertaken, considering slope, geology, regolith depth,
soil landscapes, groundwater, salinity and acid sulfate soils. The assessment identifies a range of
constraints, from low to high, across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate and confirms that development
feasibility will depend on location, land use intensity and staging.
1.1
Key geotechnical constraints
The principal geotechnical constraints affecting the Precinct and Teralba Southgate include:
 Mine subsidence: all the sub-precincts are underlain by historic underground coal workings, resulting in
potential subsidence risk that must be considered in land use planning and foundation design. Potential
subsidence risks are not currently quantified as further studies are required. Development in these areas
must include geotechnical assessments to characterise the risk and potentially solutions such as grouting
of the former workings, if settlement has not already occurred.
 Tailings Storage Facility: the TSF comprises hydraulically placed tailings and fill materials with high
variability, uncertain composition, and long-term settlement potential. The area is currently undergoing
rehabilitation and remains a declared dam. It represents a high-risk area for development, with settlement
potentially occurring over decades.
 Alluvial and low-lying areas: land adjacent to Cockle Creek and its tributaries is characterised by deep,
compressible soils, shallow groundwater, flooding and acid sulfate soil risk, presenting significant
constraints to structural development.
 Disturbed and anthropogenic terrains: areas of fill and disturbed ground exhibit variable and uncertain
geotechnical properties, reducing bearing capacity and increasing settlement risk.
 Salinity and reactive soils: much of the Precinct and Taralba Southgate are affected by moderate to very

--- Page 8 [Matched: ['mine subsidence', 'subsidence advisory', 'fill']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Geotechnical Engineering Report Rev B_Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
3
1.3
Infrastructure and constructability
The Report identifies several implications for infrastructure design:
 Flood-prone areas require resilient design and scour protection
 Ground improvement (e.g. preloading, wick drains) may be required in soft or compressible soils
 Excavation in coal-bearing strata may require selective removal and treatment
 Utilities may require specialised trenching, dewatering, and corrosion protection
These factors will influence development cost, timing and feasibility across the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate.
1.4
Data gaps and further investigations
The assessment identifies data limitations, including:
 Limited and dated geotechnical investigations
 Incomplete understanding of TSF material properties and depth
 Uncertainty in mine subsidence risks in details and condition of historic fill areas as well as groundwater
behaviour.
To address these uncertainties, following rezoning, further site-specific investigations are required to:
 Confirm ground conditions and groundwater regimes
 Refine geotechnical risk mapping
 Support detailed design and future development approvals.
Development at later stages must be supported by intrusive investigations, modelling, and engineering
validation.
In general, from a geotechnical perspective, the majority of the Precinct  is considered to be suitable for the
purposes of commercial and industrial use, with varying levels of localised mitigation measures based on
geographically specific constraints.
Referral to Subsidence Advisory NSW (SA NSW) will be required to confirm site specific requirements and
more detailed subsidence modelling may be required. It is expected that many of the site constraints can be
managed through engineering intervention such as ground improvement and consideration of mine
subsidence in structural design or ground stabilisation by means such as grouting of former workings.

2
Introduction
This Geotechnical Engineering Report (this Report) has been prepared by Aurecon on behalf of the
Department Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI) to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie
Coal Complex for Post Mining Land Use. The Macquarie Coal Complex Land Transformation Precinct (the
Precinct) and Teralba Southgate form part of the NSW Government’s investigation into future land use
pathways for mining affected land.
This Report provides a desktop based geotechnical assessment to support the rezoning, planning and
staged redevelopment of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate sub-precinct. It establishes an evidence base
to identify, assess and manage geotechnical constraints, and to guide investigation, earthworks and
foundation design requirements across in these areas.
The Report is intended to inform:
 State-led rezoning processes
 Structure planning and sub‑precinct development strategies
 Infrastructure planning and delivery
 Future development applications and site-specific investigations.
The Precinct and Teralba Southgate are characterised by a highly variable ground environment, shaped by:
 Underground mining (West Wallsend Colliery)
 Open cut mining and backfilled voids
 Coal processing infrastructure and tailings deposition
 Progressive rehabilitation and landform recontouring
These legacy conditions result in a range of geotechnical challenges, including:
 Mine subsidence risk
 Variable and uncontrolled fill
 Soft and compressible alluvial soils
 Groundwater variability
 Stability and settlement considerations associated with the Tailings Storage Facility (TSF).
A strategic, risk-informed framework to manage constraints associated with the geotechnical conditions of
the Precinct and Teralba Southgate should therefore be adopted.
2.1
Background
Open cut and underground mining operations have all ceased and current activities are focused on
rehabilitation, closure planning, and long-term environmental management.

--- Page 20 [Matched: ['fill', 'tailings']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Geotechnical Engineering Report Rev B_Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
15
3.2.2
TSF landform
The central portion of the Cockle Creek sub-precinct comprises the TSF landform. The TSF was used for the
disposal of fine coal processing residues, which were hydraulically placed and retained by earth fill
embankments. Reference to the West Wallsend Tailings Dam Report indicates this TSF is currently
undergoing rehabilitation works, including capping, regrading and revegetation. The landform consists of
tailings with associated mine waste materials and should therefore be considered as an area of variable
anthropogenic ground conditions and topography. The area is still under final construction with placement of
fill and final capping in progress at the time of the preparation of this report. The TSF is a declared dam and
the critical failure section is assessed as being the northern embankment, primarily due to the greater
thickness of the eastern embankment. The eastern embankment is sufficiently large that this is the area
where capping materials are being sourced for the final landform.
Reference to Figure 3-4 indicates that the final landform average slopes are designed to be 1V:8H for the
area of borrow (or 7 degrees), 1V:100 H for the upper tailings capping area (or 0.5 degrees) and 1V:4H for
the contour bank stripping area (or 14 degrees) following rehabilitation works (WSP, 2025). Surface drainage
design has also been included in the final landform design.
Figure 3-4 Visual representation of the final planned landform slopes, with the area of borrow in orange, upper
tailings capping area in yellow, and contour bank stripping area in red (WSP, 2025)
3.3
Regional geology and geomorphology
Reference to the Seamless Geology layer via MinView (Colquhoun et al., 2019) indicates that the Precinct
and Teralba Southgate are predominantly underlain by alluvial deposits (Q_ab, Q_af, Q_ap, Q_at and Q_av)
along Cockle Creek and its tributaries in low-lying areas, potentially including soft to firm clays, silts and
loose cohesionless materials.
The rock units of the Newcastle Coal Measures (Pnb, Pnmc and Pnme) are expected in relatively higher
elevation areas. The Narrabeen Group (Tnc) and anthropogenic deposits are also present locally to the
southwest and northeast of the site, respectively, with anthropogenic deposits occurring west of Cockle
Creek around Black Swan Lake and its surrounds.

--- Page 21 [Matched: ['great northern', 'fassifern']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Geotechnical Engineering Report Rev B_Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
16
In structural geological terms, the area lies close to the northern margin of the Sydney Basin and at the edge
of the relatively undisturbed central region and closer to the more disturbed and deformed northern marginal
region of the basin. The Precinct and Teralba Southgate are well mapped due to the local mining history and
a number of north-west to south-east trending structural features are recorded including:
 The Macquarie Syncline, which extends approximately 50km from Wyong to Buttai with the axial trace
running less than 1 km to the west of the TSF site
 The Lochinvar Anticline, located to the northwest of the site
 Unnamed thrust fault mapped to the south of the rail loop
 Faulting, including thrust and normal faults, predominantly trending NW–SE
 Igneous intrusions, comprising a dominant NW–SE dyke trending series aligned with the Macquarie
Syncline to the west, and an additional E–W trending dyke set to the north of the site
 Surface exposures of named coal seams (e.g. Fassifern coal seam and Great Northern coal seam).
These structural features only affect the Newcastle Coal Measures rock units in the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate and will be more relevant once certain development types become known. Especially where new
development will be above former mine workings, and the structural details will affect settlement predictions.
Refer to Figure 3-5 for the geological setting. A higher-resolution version of this figure is provided in Figure
A4 of Appendix A – Figures.
Figure 3-5 Geological setting

3.4
Depth of regolith
Reference to the Soil and Landscape Grid National Soil Attribute Maps – Soil Depth, published by CSIRO on
the MinView website indicates generally shallow surface soils (0 - 3 m) across higher elevations, including
the TSF area, where they predominantly comprise residual soils derived from underlying rock units. Regolith
thickness increases towards low-lying areas, particularly around Cockle Creek, Diega Creek and associated
tributaries, where depths locally reach 10 - 20 m and are expected to comprise alluvium over residual soils.
This trend is reflected across the Structure Plan sub-precincts, with thinner residual soils on higher ground
and thicker alluvial-residual sequences in drainage lines.  It is noted regolith CSIRO defines regolith as all
soil, sediment and extremely weathered material overlying competent bedrock. Regolith depth therefore
indicates depth to fresh rock only and does not reflect material consistency or relative density.
Refer to Figure 3-6 for the depth of regolith across the site. A higher-resolution version of this figure is
provided in in Figure A10 of Appendix A – Figures.
Figure 3-6 Depth of regolith

3.7.3
Open cut lake site
This site is well revegetated and the lake apparently stocked with fish. The uppermost portion of the original
open cut slopes are still visible above the lake edge on the western side. Glencore Staff mentioned ongoing
water testing and that the bathymetry of the open cut was partly backfilled prior to allowing water levels to
rise. Some areas of fill material could be seen around the lake edge and in the access road material
including limited man-made material.
3.8
Soil landscapes
According to the eSPADE 2.2 tool from the NSW Department of Planning, Industry and Environment
(Department of Planning and Environment, 2024), the Precinct and Teralba Southgate include various soil
landscapes, predominantly Killingworth (9232ki) (~ 34 %) in higher areas and Cockle Creek (9232cc) (~ 35
%) along Cockle Creek and its tributaries in low-lying areas. This is followed by Doyalson (9232do) (~ 11 %),
Disturbed Terrain (9232xx) (~ 10 %), and more localised occurrences of Warners Bay (9232wa), Gateshead
(9232ga), and Stockrington (9232sn) (each ≤ 5 %).
Upland areas (Killingworth soil landscape) are constrained by erosion susceptibility, steep slope, and mine
subsidence risk. Alluvial areas (Cockle Creek) are constrained by flooding, waterlogging, elevated water
tables, run-on effects, and dispersive soils. Disturbed Terrain presents the greatest uncertainty due to highly
variable ground conditions, potential uncontrolled fill, and thus severe geotechnical constraints (e.g. low
bearing capacity, compressible or loose materials, potential buried obstructions and voids, etc.). The
remaining soil landscapes contribute to localised constraints including steeper slopes, shallow soils, and
variable foundation conditions.
Refer to Figure 3-8 for the soil landscapes across the site. A higher-resolution version of this figure is
provided in Figure A5 of Appendix A – Figures.
Figure 3-8 Soil landscapes
Detailed characteristics and typical limitations of the mapped soil landscapes are summarised in Table 3-8.

The Warners Bay soil landscape comprises undulating to

3.9
Acid Sufalte Soils
Acid Sulfate Soils (ASS) risk maps indicate the likely distribution of soils containing iron sulphides, primarily
pyrite (FeS₂) based on geomorphic setting. These soils are generally benign when undisturbed but, when
exposed to air through excavation or drainage, oxidise to produce sulphuric acid (pH < 4), potentially
reducing the durability of concrete and buried foundations and infrastructure. In addition, the costs
associated with safe management of excavations and removal of construction spoil is significantly increased
if ASS are present.
ASS potential across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate has been assessed using the eSPADE portal
(Department of Planning and Environment, 2024), which indicates ASS risk broadly consistent with alluvial
deposits in low-lying areas along Cockle Creek and its tributaries. Mapped risk ranges from low to high,
notably predominantly low across sub-precinct No.6B, with higher risk closer to Cockle Creek in the north.
Upland areas generally don’t present known occurrence of ASS, and disturbed terrain is also present to the
northeast of the MCC site. Sub-precinct No. 6D exhibits the highest likelihood of ASS across most of its
area.
Refer to Figure 3-9 for the soil ASS probability across the site. A higher-resolution version of this figure is
provided in Figure A5 of Appendix A – Figures.
Figure 3-9 ASS probability
ASS probability for each sub-precinct is summarised in Table 3-10.

are expected to include potential for ASS.

height and depth of cover. The depths to underground mine workings typically vary with local topography
and bedrock geology and therefore the level of subsidence risk is also likely to vary across other areas
where monitoring has not been completed. But it is noted from this data that the majority of subsidence may
have already occurred above former Longwalls, which is to be expected as this is part of Longwall mining
design considerations.

4
Structure Plan preliminary ground models
The anticipated ground models across the Structure Plan sub-precincts are primarily based on the nearest
existing ground investigation data to the sub-precinct and are summarised in Table 4-1. It is noted that site-
specific geotechnical data is limited for much of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate, particularly within sub-
precincts No.2, 5, 6A and 6C. For these areas, the anticipated ground profile has been primarily informed by
a review of available geotechnical reports and regional geology mapping.
Groundwater inflows were at depth of approximately 1.5 to 2.8 m BGL in boreholes 91-HE89-B4A, 91-HE89-
B5, 91-HE89-TP5, 91-HE89-TP10, 91-HE89-B6 and 91-HE89-B7, located east of sub-precinct No.6D along
Racecourse Road (Public Works Engineering and Surveying Branch, 1992). These observations suggest
potential shallow groundwater levels in the vicinity of Cockle Creek. Standing groundwater was also
recorded at depths of 3.8 to 4.2 m BGL in monitoring wells GW202446, GW202445 and GW202444 further
north of this sub-precinct (WaterNSW). Boreholes 91-HE89-B13, 91-HE89-B14 and 91-HE89-B15 within
Teralba Southgate were dry upon completion. For the remaining investigation locations reviewed as part of
this Report, groundwater observations were not recorded on the available logs.
Groundwater levels are expected to be relatively shallow in areas adjacent to Cockle Creek, Diega Creek
and associated tributaries, particularly within low-lying areas of the MCC Precinct. Groundwater in these
areas may become locally shallower following prolonged heavy rainfall.
Perched groundwater may be present in cohesionless fill or alluvial deposits where these materials overlie
less permeable soils or bedrock. Groundwater conditions and hydraulic conductivity regime should be
confirmed through future site investigations.
Given the limited geotechnical data available across much of the MCC Precinct and Teralba Southgate,
additional investigations are recommended to verify the anticipated ground profile, improve confidence in the
current interpretation, and inform future development and design following the rezoning.

5
Geotechnical constraints assessment
5.1
Key geotechnical criteria
The overall site setting, including slope, geology, depth of regolith, soil landscapes, ASS, salinity and mine
subsidence described in the preceding sections of this Report presents geotechnical constraints that must be
addressed in infrastructure planning and design, and opportunities that may be optimised from the outset.
To support the geotechnical constraints assessment, and inform the Master Plan, these constraint categories
have been defined as key geotechnical criteria for the development of the overall constraint map, using their
associated primary datasets listed in Table 5-1, and combined risk rankings presented in the subsequent
sections and Appendix B figures.
The overall constraints map is provided in Appendix C – Near surface geotechnical constraints map for
Structure Plan.
Table 5-1 Summary of constraint categories and associated datasets

 Coal combustion hazard (Sponcom) is not represented as part of the constraint maps as it is not mapped
or available digitally at the time of writing. The risk is associated with two main forms: existing coal seams
that remain in the natural geology, and coal materials that remain in mine spoils that have been stockpiled
or placed as fill across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate.
 Mine subsidence: all the sub-precincts are underlain by historic underground coal workings, resulting in
potential subsidence risk that must be considered in land use planning and foundation design. Potential
subsidence risks are not currently quantified as further studies are required.
 Limited geotechnical investigation information for strength, consistency or thicknesses of shallow soils
and rocks. Existing records are generally dated, with incomplete descriptions and limited laboratory
testing, and in many locations, bedrock was not intersected, meaning soil thickness cannot be confirmed.
As such, foundation conditions have not been assessed in detail. Slope stability assessment is also
preliminary and based primarily on available topographic data.
5.3
Methodology
The identified geotechnical criteria were classified into three ranking levels (low, moderate, and high) based
on their characteristics and relative constraints to development, including foundations, earthworks, and
excavations. The risk rankings adopted for each geotechnical criterion are presented in Table 5-2.

5.5
TSF area risk ranking
At the time of the preparation of this Report the TSF area was undergoing placement of capping to achieve
the final landform. As such, final settlement has not commenced yet. Large settlements are expected and no
development can occur until such time that the TSF risk rating is reduced sufficiently to allow the structure to
be de-declared.
The TSF is constrained by:
 Dam Safety management requirement
 Ongoing and historical earthworks
 Hydraulically placed coal residue
 Groundwater levels with the storage
 Underlying natural ground profile that has potential regolith depths of up to 10 m.
 Steep surrounding slopes
 Variable storage depth
The ATC Williams report indicates that the depth of the storage area is variable and is expected to be formed
of the original valley profile that drains to the original dam wall within the eastern side of the storage. No
records are available that include estimates of long-term settlement; however, it would be expected to be in
the order of metres over decades.
The storage embankments are wider on the eastern slopes and it is understood that this is where the final
landform capping material is also being won from currently.
Around the TSF are natural and man-made slopes. Some of which are steep. Along the southern margin is
an excavated batter.
These factors result in significant geotechnical constraints to future development, particularly due to
settlement potential and variable subsurface conditions.
Given the current earthworks, the existing datasets are considered to provide a limited representation of
geotechnical risk within the TSF area, as they ground conditions that are unlikely to correspond to the final
rehabilitated landform. The risk ranking presented in Table 5-18 for the TSF footprint is therefore provided for
reference only.

6
Land use scenario
Section 7 of this Report provides a summary of the scenario development during the Enquiry by Design
workshops held on 7 and 8 May 2026, then on 1 June 2026 which involved implementing visions and
concepts, identifying challenges and developing innovative solutions at a precinct-wide level across all
technical streams. Scenarios were developed and refined by GHD for the Precinct Structure Plan boundary.
They considered land use, transport, infrastructure, PFAS, environmental, social, aboriginal heritage and
economic matters in conjunction with the MCC vision. Each of the scenarios tested identifies the
development limitations, constraints management and required infrastructure that would be required to
support the respective structure plan’s proposed development. This information was subsequently utilised at
a second Enquiry by Design workshop to inform the Structure Plan.
6.1
Key scenario findings
As part of the scenario testing the methodology described in Section 5 was used to assess each option.
From a geotechnical perspective the scenarios were relatively comparable based on the testing criteria used
in Section 5.1.
A key finding from the scenario testing phase was that development west of Cockle Creek and Teralba
Southgate is likely to be more cost-effective, due to the greater extent of potential ASS, deep soft organic
soils near the creek and tributaries. Areas of disturbed ground and anthropogenic fill within the Cockle
Creek, Killingworth and West Lake sub-precincts, as well as around the coastal wetland in the north of the
Precinct, may also present ongoing geotechnical constraints to development.
It is also noted that limited geotechnical data was available across the MCC structure plan area to further
refine the assessment criteria (as outlined in Section 3.13). Accordingly, additional geotechnical
investigations and laboratory testing will be required during subsequent stages of the MCC planning and
design process to confirm ground conditions and better define the extent and distribution of the soils present
within each scenario area.

Project number 531168-000  File Geotechnical Engineering Report Rev B_Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
67
7.4
Constructability
7.4.1
Flood management
Riverine flooding would be expected to induce scour on any developments that border the flood risk areas or
if developments are planned within these areas. Failing to mitigate flooding may cause excessive total or
differential settlements in the underlying soft clays soils potentially present in the sub-precincts No.5, 3, 6A,
6B, 6C and 6D.
As such, any new development planned in or close to modelled flood regions should be designed to
incorporate flood resilience features:
 Scour protection may be necessary for fill embankments or considered in foundation designs of bridges
or culverts and other structures.
 New developments, such as ground floor car parking and first floor offices will reduce damage and the
need for inspections and repair after flood events.
 Fill embankments should be suitably specified to use free draining materials, adequate erosion control
measures to avoid fines washout, or incorporate draining base layers. The same is applicable to
associated structures such as bridge abutments or retaining walls.
 Levees if required along the proposed boundaries to flood risk areas typically need to be constructed
using clay rich fill or other less permeable soil material as liner material.
Reference should be made to the Flooding and Water Cycle Management Engineering Report for details
related to flood management.
7.4.2
Earthworks considerations
Alluviums and residual soils are generally expected to be readily excavatable using conventional excavator
equipment (20 tonne mass excavator). Where harder soil consistencies are encountered, the use of a
toothed bucket / ripper tyne may be required. Suitability for re-use may be limited to general fill depending on
erosion potential, reactivity, material consistency and presence of organic soils, noting that residual soils are
expected to be reactive based on the constraints associated with the mapped soil landscapes. Existing fill
areas may contain contaminants and are therefore considered unsuitable for reuse without further
assessment.
Within the Narrabeen Group and Newcastle Coal Measures, excavatability is likely to vary depending on
rock properties such as strength and degree of natural fracturing. Typically, excavation within the less
weathered, higher strength and intact sandstone rock may require rock saws and/or hydraulic rock breakers.

--- Page 73 [Matched: ['fill', 'foundations']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Geotechnical Engineering Report Rev B_Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
68
The risk of spontaneous combustion is also potentially present in reworked fill material that include coal
material.
Earthworks planning should incorporate segregation of coal-impacted materials, appropriate stockpile
management (including temperature and moisture monitoring where required), and controls to prevent
oxidation and ignition risk.
Details regarding gas management are provided in the Contamination Engineering Report.
7.4.3
Shallow foundations
Shallow foundations are anticipated across sub-precincts No. 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5. In sub-precincts No. 1, 2, 3
and 5, where shallow groundwater conditions are expected, the use of shallow foundation solutions is likely
to reduce geotechnical challenges. In these areas, founding above typical groundwater levels may minimise
the need for temporary excavation support and dewatering, thereby reducing construction risk and cost.
Additionally:
 The risk of differential and total settlements beneath new structures should be assessed and addressed
during the design stage. If differential settlement is likely to exceed acceptable limits, foundation
treatments such as removing reactive soils within the suction zone and replacing them with non-reactive
engineered granular fill should be considered.
 Another solution may include surcharge preloading of proposed footprints to improve underlying soils
(particularly sandy soils) to support fill pads. Preloading is also effective in reducing long-term creep
deformations for soft to firm clay deposits beneath embankments. The suitability and extents of
preloading will depend on the proposed infrastructure and underlying ground conditions. Typically,
cohesive soils (clays) require longer consolidation periods than free draining soils such as sands. Where
mixed soil profiles are encountered, ground treatment approaches will need to be adapted accordingly.
 The surcharge period required to achieve acceptable settlement performance in accordance with
serviceability requirements depends on the thickness of the soft/compressible clay deposits and the
magnitude of applied preload. While increasing surcharge loads can reduce consolidation timeframes,
care must be taken to avoid instability in weak ground conditions. Typical preload durations may range
from 3 to18 months; however, project specific analysis is required to confirm suitable durations. Vertical
drains may be installed to accelerate consolidation. The use of lightweight fill materials in combination
with surcharge loading is also an accepted construction approach. Where final levels permit, non-
engineered fill may be used as surcharge material above required grade levels.
 For light industrial developments such as warehousing and offices buildings, shallow foundations are
generally expected to be sufficient. However, heavily loaded point structures within warehouses, such as

--- Page 74 [Matched: ['mine subsidence', 'subsidence advisory', 'fill', 'foundations']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Geotechnical Engineering Report Rev B_Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
69
7.4.5
Utilities
Utilities associated with light industrial, commercial, residential and general industrial development, primarily
within Sub-precincts No. 1, 2, 4 and 5, should consider the following:
 Underground services are typically installed within trench excavations. Shallow groundwater conditions,
together with the potential presence of ASS, may increase construction complexity and cost.
 Most service trenches are expected to require shoring and dewatering, particularly in areas of shallow
groundwater.
 Surficial soils are expected to be easy to excavate. Where shallow rock may be encountered in sub-
precincts No.2, No. 4, and in localised areas of sub-precinct No.1, the excavatability of rock will depend
on its strength, weathering and degree of fracturing. Excavation of less weathered, higher-strength rock
may require specialised equipment such as rock saws or hydraulic rock breakers.
 Above ground utilities such as overhead power lines may require deep foundations to prevent differential
settlement issues.
7.4.6
Pavements and roads
The surficial soils across much of the Precinct, particularly soft alluvial deposits, are expected to exhibit low
California Bearing Ratio values and therefore limited subgrade strength.
Organic soils may be present near Cockle Creek and its tributaries, while highly reactive clays within alluvial
and residual soils are expected across much of the Precinct. These conditions may require ground treatment
measures such as removal and replacement or ground improvement. Where road embankments or fill
platforms are proposed over compressible soils, preloading and/or basal reinforcement may be necessary to
manage settlement associated with long-term consolidation.
7.4.7
Acid Sulfate Soils
Areas identified as having a higher potential for ASS, particularly within sub-precincts No.2, 6B, 6C and 6D,
will require ASS testing as part of future site investigations. Where ASS are confirmed, preparation and
implementation of an ASS management plan is likely to be required during construction.
7.4.8
Mine subsidence
Given the historical mining activities across the entire MCC Precinct and Teralba Southgate and Mine
Subsidence District zoning, future development applications would trigger a referral to the SA pursuant to the
Coal Mine Subsidence Compensation Act 2027 (integrated development).

--- Page 75 [Matched: ['mine subsidence', 'subsidence advisory']] ---

70
3. Meet with SA NSW to get informal advice on classification of site with respect to Mine Subsidence
conditions
4. If applicable, lodge RFI with SA NSW to get formal notification of Mine Subsidence requirements (if
any).  This may include any, or all, of the followings:
1. Confirmation that the site is in a proclaimed Mine Subsidence district
2. Prescriptive advice on specific types of development that are allowed / not allowed
3. Prescriptive advice on the Mine Subsidence Surface Movement Parameters that must be
applied to development of the site (e.g. Total maximum subsidence, ground tilt, ground
strain, radius of curvature etc.)
4. Requirements for additional site investigation (e.g. Drilling to identify existing mine voids in
the case of existing bord and pillar workings, drilling to identify existing site geology and
geotechnical conditions etc.)
5. Requirements for additional analysis and report on predicted mine subsidence induced
surface movement parameters
5. Request mining records from the NSW resource regulator (a small payment is often required)
6. Preparation of a desktop review of historical mine layouts and assessment of the quality of the
records and associated risk assessment
7. If applicable, preparation of a pillar stability assessment numerical modelling report
8. If applicable, geotechnical investigation to confirm extent, depth and condition of workings
9. If applicable, preparation of a Mine Subsidence Impact Statement (MSIS) for submission with SA
NSW
10. Follow up meeting with SA NSW to discuss the recommendations of the MSIS and agree on final
design requirements
11. SA NSW issue final conditions for the development
The initial step of the desktop based assessment will provide a better understanding of the  overall mining
history and associated subsidence risks and as such is a key first step to understand development pathways
and clarity on require further works for development approval and costs.
Typically, the following subsidence parameters would be specified in the conditions of approval:
 Vertical Subsidence (x mm)
 Compressive Strains (y mm/m)
 Tensile Strains (z mm/m)
 Tilts (w mm/m)
 Radius of Curvature (v km)

Recommendations
The majority of the Structure Plan sub-precincts have limited existing geotechnical information available. It is
therefore strongly recommended that these areas be subject to targeted geotechnical investigations to
support future development post rezoning.
The scope of investigations should be proportionate to the proposed land use intensity, sub‑precinct
characteristics and constraint profile, and structured to inform both precinct-scale planning and site-specific
development outcomes.
These should be integrated and applied in conjunction with the Dam, Flooding, Water Cycle Management
and Contamination Report recommendations.
The proposed investigation scope and alignment with the Precinct application areas, and to be coordinated
with other disciplines such as Contamination, is summarised in Table 8-1.

9
Conclusion
The Geotechnical Assessment considers that the Precinct can support future urban and employment uses
for commercial and industrial uses; however, rezoning must be explicitly aligned with geotechnical
constraints, risk levels, and rehabilitation. The site is highly heterogeneous, characterised by legacy mining
impacts, variable fill, alluvial soils, and subsidence risk, meaning that development suitability is location
dependent.
Lower-constrained areas such as Teralba Southgate and Killingworth present clear opportunities for urban
land use such as residential and mixed use, while moderate constraint areas can be progressively released
subject to detailed investigations and engineered solutions. High constraint areas, including the TSF,
floodplains, and deep alluvial zones should be reserved for conservation, open space, or deferred long-term
uses, with development informed by geotechnical investigations.
The desktop analysis highlights that key geotechnical risks including mine subsidence, settlement, flooding,
slopes, areas of historic filling, ASS and salinity occur within the Precinct and inform the required site-specific
detailed investigations infrastructure design following rezoning at the DA stage to support future
development. Investigations should be tailored to land use intent and constraint profile of the sub-precinct
outlined in this report. It is considered possible that further subsidence assessment work may indicate that
development types with low tolerance for ground movement or structural deformation could prove
uneconomical.
The Tailings Storage Facility represents a long-term redevelopment constraint, requiring completion of
rehabilitation, settlement stabilisation and regulatory de‑declaration before any development can be
contemplated in this area. Similarly, floodplain and estuarine environments which occur within the Precinct
will remain constrained and are better suited to conservation and recreational land uses that don’t impact
flood conveyance and increase risk.
In consideration of the above, future development must be informed by detailed geotechnical assessment
addressing:
 Mine subsidence
 Settlement and fill behaviour
 Slope stability
 Ground conditions and rehabilitation status
 Earthworks
Overall, the appropriate planning response is to implement a flexible, adaptive rezoning framework that:
 Prioritises development in low-risk, serviced, and previously disturbed areas

Executive summary
This Contamination Technical Assessment (CTA) has been prepared by Aurecon Australasia Pty Ltd
(Aurecon) on behalf of the Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI) to support the State-
led rezoning of the Macquarie Coal Complex (the Precinct) and Teralba Southgate, located approximately 20
km southwest of Newcastle within the Lake Macquarie region, NSW.
The purpose of this CTA is to reinterpret existing contamination data, originally collected to support mine
closure and rehabilitation, within the context of proposed rezoning and future land use scenarios identified in
the Structure and Master Plan and Teralba Southgate. The assessment is scoped at the rezoning stage to
inform land use compatibility and identification of developable land. It does not constitute development
application (DA) level investigation or detailed remediation design.
Site Context
The Precinct comprises four former mining operations: including West Wallsend Colliery, Macquarie Coal,
Preparation Plant, Westside Mine (open-cut), and Teralba Colliery (Northgate). Teralba Southgate is located
approximately 2.5 kilometres south of the Precinct and is occupied by former mine infrastructure including
shafts. Mining activities ceased progressively between 2001 and 2016. Current activities are limited to
environmental management and rehabilitation in accordance with the Rehabilitation Management Plan (RMP
2022). The Precinct and Teralba Southgate are surrounded primarily by residential and mixed-use industrial
areas.
The rezoning aims to facilitate employment-led development supported by residential, conservation, and
open space land uses. A figure showing the extent of Teralba Southgate and the current Structure Plan for
the Precinct is provided in Figure E1.
Figure E1 – Layout and extent of Teralba Southgate and the Structure Plan for the Precinct

--- Page 4 [Matched: ['contamination', 'hydrocarbons', 'heavy metals', 'pfas']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
Summary of Contamination Conditions
The Precinct encompasses five former mining operations: West Wallsend Colliery, Macquarie Coal
Preparation Plant, Westside Mine (open-cut), and Teralba Colliery (Northgate). Mining operations ceased
progressively across the Precinct, with Teralba Colliery closing in 2001, Westside Mine ceasing operations in
2012, and West Wallsend Colliery and Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant concluding operations in 2016.
Current activities focused on long-term environmental management and rehabilitation in accordance with the
Rehabilitation Management Plan (RMP 2022). The Precinct and Teralba Southgate is primarily surrounded
by residential and mixed-use industrial land uses. The proposed rezoning seeks to establish employment-led
development across multiple sub-precincts, with designated residential, conservation and open space.
Contamination investigations across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate have been undertaken
progressively since 2005, involving:
◼ Approximately 400 soil and sediment sampling locations.
◼ 17 groundwater monitoring wells.
◼ Several ground gas monitoring locations.
These investigations define 16 “Domains” with variable contamination profiles. Key findings from previous
investigations completed at the Precinct and Teralba Southgate include:
◼ Soil and sediment contamination - Elevated concentrations of heavy metals (notably zinc), total
recoverable hydrocarbons (TRH), and polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs), primarily exceeding
ecological criteria.
◼ Human health risks - No exceedances of commercial/industrial human health criteria identified to date.
◼ Asbestos - Asbestos-containing materials (ACM) confirmed in several domains.
◼ Acid Sulfate Soils (ASS/PASS) - Actual and potential ASS identified in specific domains at the Precinct
◼ Salinity - Moderate to high salinity risk across large portions of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate.
◼ PFAS – Principally PFOS detected above adopted ecological criteria in groundwater and surface water
across multiple domains, including within the Precinct and Teralba Southgate
◼ Ground gas - Methane gas concentrations exceeding the lower explosive limit (LEL) identified in
underground mine workings, posing a potential vapour intrusion risk, including within the Precinct
(Domain 11) and Teralba Southgate (Domain 13).
A figure showing the location and extents of domains at the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is provided in
Figure E2.

--- Page 5 [Matched: ['contamination', 'tailings']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
Figure E2 – Location and extent of the closure domains at the Precinct and Teralba Southgate
Data Gaps and Limitations
Significant data gaps limit ability for full characterisation of contamination and land suitability, including:
◼ Multiple domains with no intrusive investigation (lacking soil, groundwater, surface water and sediment
data).
◼ Incomplete ASS and groundwater characterisation.
◼ Limited assessment beneath buildings and inaccessible areas/structures.
◼ Incomplete delineation of buried asbestos materials.
◼ Limited ground gas assessments.
Land Use Suitability
Based on available data and acknowledging limitations:
◼ Commercial and industrial uses - Generally compatible across most of the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate, subject to targeted investigation and management.
◼ Sensitive uses (residential, conservation, recreational) - Require further investigation and risk assessment
to confirm suitability.
◼ Key constraint - The Tailings Storage Facility (Domain 7) presents a significant constraint requiring
detailed, multidisciplinary assessment.

--- Page 6 [Matched: ['contamination', 'tsf', 'tailings', 'pfas', 'remediation', 'site audit']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
Implementation Approach and Key Recommendations
A staged, risk-based approach to investigation and management is recommended to support rezoning and
future development. With appropriate controls and management (including detailed site investigations and
appropriate remediation), the Precinct and Teralba Southgate can be made suitable for its intended uses
while protecting human health and environmental values, including Cockle Creek and Lake Macquarie.
Based on available data and with consideration to identified data gaps, the assessment concludes that
known or potential contamination is unlikely to preclude commercial/industrial rezoning for the majority of the
Precinct and Teralba Southgate, provided that targeted investigation and appropriate management controls
are implemented during future design and construction phases.  However, suitability determinations vary by
sub-precinct and proposed land use. Employment-led commercial and industrial uses are generally
considered compatible with the current contamination profile across most sub-precincts. However, more
sensitive land uses including medium or high-density residential, conservation, and recreational uses,
require targeted investigation and risk assessment to confirm suitability. This is particularly important at
Teralba Southgate where detailed ground gas assessments have not been undertaken. The Tailings Storage
Facility (Domain 7) also represents a material constraint requiring detailed assessment and integrated
management across multiple environmental and engineering disciplines.
Implementation of the management framework and performance criteria outlined in this CTA, combined with
progressive investigation and remediation through the DA approval process, will enable the Precinct and
Teralba Southgate to support sustainable post-mining land use while protecting human health and
environmental values in Cockle Creek and Lake Macquarie.
Based on the findings of this assessment, and subject to the limitations outlined in Section 11, Aurecon
presents the following recommendations:
◼ Implement a risk-based, staged investigation program for all future development proposals, with Detailed
Site Investigations (DSIs) and Tier 2/3 quantitative risk assessments undertaken where elevated COPC
concentrations are present or data gaps are identified, ensuring land use suitability is confirmed prior to
development approval
◼ Prioritise targeted investigation of Domains 2, 5, 7, 14, and 16 (uninvestigated domains), comprehensive
groundwater assessment across all domains, ASS characterisation in low-lying areas, and asbestos
delineation sampling to resolve material data gaps constraining sensitive land use suitability
determination
◼ Undertake integrated assessment of the Tailings Storage Facility (TSF) across water management,
geotechnical stability, dam safety, and regulatory compliance frameworks, including geochemical
characterisation of tailings materials and assessment of leachate generation and Acid Mine Drainage
(AMD) pathways to inform remediation and long-term management strategy

--- Page 7 [Matched: ['contamination', 'remediation', 'site audit']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
preparation of Long-Term Environmental Management Plans (LTEMPs) where residual contamination
risks remain. As per Lake Macquarie City Council’s (LMCCs) requirements, an EPA certified consultant
must be engaged where reports are submitted to the NSW EPA or where required by the consent
authority
◼ All future works should be conducted by an appropriately qualified environmental consultant under the
direction of a CEnvP-SC or equivalent accreditation. An accredited NSW EPA Auditor may be required to
endorse the investigation, remediation/management and validation works where more sensitive land uses
are proposed or where there are elevated risks to ecological receptors.
Pathway for Residential Development (Teralba Southgate)
Residential development at Teralba Southgate will require a standard but targeted contaminated land
assessment and approval process, including:
◼ Detailed Site Investigation (DSI) involving intrusive assessment to fully characterise the nature and extent
of contamination across the site in potentially affected media (i.e. ground gas and to a lesser extent soil,
groundwater, surface water, sediment) with data comparison made to applicable land use criteria as per
the NEPM (e.g. medium to high density residential).
◼ Remedial Action Plan (RAP) to define extents and media requiring remediation and/or management to
render the site suitable for the proposed land uses (e.g. residential).
◼ Validation sampling and reporting to confirm remediation objectives have been achieved.
◼ Independent Site Audit by an accredited NSW EPA Auditor which is recommended for residential land
use on a former mine site, to provide independent assurance of site suitability prior to development
consent being granted.
This process will demonstrate that the land is suitable, or can be made suitable, for residential use in
accordance with the NEPM (2013), EP&A Act, State Environmental Planning Policy (SEPP, Resilience and
Hazards) 2021 and relevant NSW EPA legislation and guidelines.

1
Introduction
1.1
Introduction
Aurecon Australasia (Aurecon) was engaged by the Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure
(DPHI) for a variety of engineering services to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie Coal
Complex, herein referred to as “the Precinct” and “Teralba Southgate”. The Precinct and Teralba Southgate
are located approximately 20 kilometres (km) southwest of Newcastle, in the Lake Macquarie region of New
South Wales (NSW). It is noted that Teralba Southgate has been removed from the scope of the Master Plan
and will not be pursued under a State Environmental Planning Policy (SEPP) rezoning process. Instead,
Teralba Southgate is proposed to be rezoned under a Local Environmental Plan (LEP). A figure showing the
location and extent of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is provided in Figure 1, Appendix A.
The Precinct encompasses the former West Wallsend Colliery (WWC), the Macquarie Coal Preparation
Plant (MCPP), the former open-cut Westside Mine, and the former Teralba Colliery (Northgate site). Mining
operations ceased progressively across the Precinct, with Teralba Colliery closing in 2001, Westside Mine
ceasing operations in 2012, and West Wallsend Colliery and Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant concluding
operations in 2016.Current activities are focused on long-term environmental management and rehabilitation
in accordance with the Rehabilitation Management Plan (RMP 2022).
This Contamination Technical Assessment (CTA) builds on the Post Mining Land Use Engineering Analysis
Report (Aurecon 2026) and has been developed to support refinement of the Master Plan and to inform
DPHI's decision making as part of the rezoning process. It is noted that environmental and contamination
investigations completed to date have been undertaken primarily to support mine closure obligations, rather
than to establish suitability for alternative land uses. Accordingly, this assessment reinterprets existing
contamination data within the context of the proposed rezoning and future land use scenarios shown in
Figure 2, Appendix A.
1.2
Objectives
The objective of this CTA is to support refinement of the Master Plan and to inform DPHI's decision making
as part of the rezoning process. Specifically, the objectives of this CTA are to:
◼ Provide a precinct-scale contaminated land evidence base to support rezoning and Master Plan land use
compatibility, building on existing contamination assessments referenced through Enquiry by Design
(EbD) engineering discussions
◼ Confirm whether contamination represents a material constraint to developable land verification for

--- Page 11 [Matched: ['contamination', 'remediation']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
2
1.3
Methodology
The scope of this CTA comprises five integrated components to achieve the rezoning objectives including:
◼ Desktop compilation including a review and synthesis of previous investigations and assessment
documents
◼ Systematic constraints analysis including the identification of Areas of Environmental Concern (AECs)
and assessment of remediation implications under different land use scenarios
◼ Development of a Conceptual Site Model (CSM) to integrate source pathway receptor (SPR) linkages
across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate
◼ Identification of data gaps to inform recommendations for future investigation
◼ Development of a management framework to integrate contamination management into the future
planning and development approval pathway, including staged investigation triggers, regulatory
notification protocols, and interfaces with other engineering and environmental disciplines.

2
Planning context and limitations
2.1
Level of detail and exclusions
This CTA report has been prepared appropriate for a rezoning and Master Plan stage and is structured to
support precinct-scale land use compatibility decisions and developable land verification for the Precinct and
Teralba Southgate. This report builds on the existing information mainly from prior investigations undertaken
across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate at various stages, primarily in support of mine closure obligations
and reinterprets that data within the context of the proposed rezoning and future land use scenarios.
This CTA is not intended or scoped to deliver a development application (DA) level investigation, detailed
remediation design, or construction validation for the Precinct and Teralba Southgate. The assessment has
been prepared to inform the Structure Plan and development of a Master Plan and to support the DPHI’s
decision-making as part of the State-led rezoning process.
Typical items as part of the staged contamination risk assessment and assurance framework are excluded
from this assessment and would need to be addressed progressively through the DA and approvals process
as individual proposals are brought forward. These include the following:
◼ Preliminary Site Investigations (PSIs)
◼ Detailed site investigations (DSIs)
◼ Sampling Analysis and Quality Plans (SAQPs)
◼ Remedial Action Plan (RAP) or management plans relating to contamination including remediation
costing.
2.2
Reliance on data and information
This report has been prepared based on documentation and information provided by DPHI and Glencore
listed below and referenced as part of this report. Aurecon has not independently verified the accuracy or
completeness of this information. of existing studies and data, except as otherwise noted in this report. The
findings, advice, and recommendations presented in this report are reliant on this material being accurate
and complete. Where data gaps or uncertainties have been identified, these are noted throughout this report
and are discussed further in Sections 5 to 7.
The documents used in the preparation of this report are as follows:
◼ Post Mining Land Use – Engineering Analysis Report (DRAFT), prepared by Aurecon for DPHI
◼ Post Mining Land Use – Deliverability of Pilot Projects – Due Diligence Report – Macquarie Coal
Complex, prepared by Aurecon in February 2026

3
Baseline analysis
This section presents the baseline environmental and contamination information for the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate. The baseline analysis draws on a series of previous contamination investigations undertaken
primarily to support mine closure obligations drawing on the existing evidence base compiled from previous
investigations and publicly available information. Existing data and available information for the Precinct and
Teralba Southgate have been reinterpreted within the context of the proposed rezoning and future land use
scenarios identified in the Structure Plan (Figure 1, Appendix A). This section includes summary of the
environmental setting information for the Precinct and Teralba Southgate, identifies key AECs and summary
of previous remediation activities that form the basis for the CSM, and constraint assessment presented in
Section 4.
3.1
Environmental setting
As outlined in Section 1.1, the Precinct is located approximately 20 kilometres southwest of Newcastle in
Lake Macquarie, NSW. It comprises four sub-sites (West Wallsend Colliery, Macquarie Coal Preparation
Plant, Westside Mine, and Teralba Colliery (Northgate) and is currently zoned SP1, Special Activities
(Mining), C2 Environmental Conservation, C3 Environmental Management and RU2 Rural Landscape
pursuant to the Lake Macquarie Local Environmental Plan 2014. Teralba Southgate is currently zoned as
Special Activities (Mining) and occupies an area of approximately 7 hectares (ha). Areas surrounding the
Precinct and Teralba Southgate generally comprise residential communities and mixed-use industrial land
uses. A summary of environmental features within the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is provided in Table
3-1.
Table 3-1 Summary of Environmental Setting
Environmental Feature
Description
Soil
A combined review of soil landscape information provided by eSPADE tool hosted by
the NSW Department of Planning, Industry and Environment (DPE 2025) and previous
investigations completed at the Precinct and Teralba Southgate indicates that surficial
soils generally comprise sandy loam topsoil transitioning to sandy clay, with variable fill
materials (1.9–8.5 m depth) containing coal fragments, concrete, brick and metal.
Geology
A review of geological information sourced from NSW Seamless Geology MinView

--- Page 15 [Matched: ['contamination']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
6
Environmental Feature
Description
Acid Sulfate Soils (ASS)
Review of ASS risk mapping tool hosted by eSPADE (2025) indicates that the majority
of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is unlikely to comprise an ASS risk. However,
areas characterised as having low ASS probability (1-3 m AHD, and >3 m AHD) occupy
the sub precincts 2, 3, 6B and 6C. Areas comprising high probability (between 1–3 m
AHD) are present along the far northern and eastern precinct margins within sub
precincts 6b and 6D. Disturbed terrain classifications (elevation 1–4 m AHD) are
mapped across portions of the eastern boundary, reflecting prior land disturbance that
may have altered the vertical distribution and oxidation state of sulfidic materials. A
figure showing the ASS probability for the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is provided in
Figure 3, Appendix A.
Site-specific ASS investigations completed in sub precinct 1 identified AASS and PASS
in dam sediments, with Dams 2 and 3 confirming active acid generation (Coffey, 2019),
though a subsequent assessment (Coffey, 2020) returned sulfide concentrations
predominantly below action criteria. Maturation Pond sediments within sub precinct 1
were also confirmed acid-generating prior to dewatering, with rehabilitation involving
lime neutralisation and relocation to a lined containment cell. A summary of site-specific
ASS investigations undertaken at the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is provided in
Appendix B.
Salinity
Based on salinity risk maps provided by NSW DCCEEW, the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate is predominantly underlain by very high salinity hazard, covering the majority
of sub precincts 1, 2, 3, 6A, 6B, 6C, 6D and 6E. Moderate salinity hazard is mapped
across portions of the northern and eastern site extents, including parts of sub precincts
2, 6B, 6C, and 6D. Low salinity hazard is present across localised areas within sub
precincts 1, 2, 3, 6A, 6B, 6C, and 6D, and along drainage corridors associated with
Cockle Creek and its tributaries. A very high classification indicates significant potential
for salt-affected soils and saline groundwater across the site, with implications for
infrastructure design, vegetation establishment, and materials selection. A figure
showing the salinity risks for the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is provided in Figure 4,
Appendix A.
