4
Conceptual Site Model
4.1
Sources, pathways, and receptors
Risks associated with potential site contamination are based on evaluating the linkages between sources,
pathways and receptors (SPRs based) on site specific environmental setting, potential design strategies, and
proposed land use scenarios. The development of a conceptual site model (CSM) for the Precinct and
Teralba Southgate facilitates an evaluation of the source-pathway-receptor relationships to assist in
identifying potential contamination risks based on the nature of the contamination sources, degree of
exposure of each receptor to a source, and the sensitivity of the likely receptors. A general description of the
CSM components required to evaluate contamination risk is provided in Table 4-1.
Table 4-1 Summary of key CSM components
Term
Description
Source
A substance that has the potential to cause harm to human or environmental receptors. Sources can be
primary (at the original point of release) or secondary (at a point further down-gradient from the primary
source following temporary storage and / or transformation) and can be either direct (i.e. emanating
from a localised area) or diffuse (i.e. widespread) in nature. In a broader sense, sources can also
include particular ground conditions that may impact on construction materials such as aggressive soils
(i.e. ASS).
Pathway
The route by which the source is brought into contact with the receptor. This can include the transport of
contamination via water (surface and groundwater), airborne dust, vapours, and particulate-matter
deposition. It can also include solubility or chemical transformation from a relatively harmless chemical
species to one that is more toxic.
Receptor
Human beings, other living organisms, and built structures that could be affected by the contamination
source. A receptor will only be affected if an exposure pathway from the source to the receptor is
complete.
Groundwater and surface water systems can be considered as receptors as their quality is regulated by
statutory bodies, as well as being potential pathways for contaminant migration to other receptors.
In a broader sense, a receptor can be building or construction materials from the presence of

4.3
Constraint determination
A constraint rating for each domain was determined by combining the consequence and likelihood ratings
using the matrix in Table 4-3. The matrix applies a conservative approach consistent with the range of
proposed land uses, with greater weight given to consequence where data gaps exist and receptor
sensitivity is high (e.g. residential and conservation precincts). Where a domain transects multiple land use
zones, the more sensitive land use was adopted to derive the constraints rating.

5
Land Use Capability
The following sections present key findings as they relate to the current structure and master plan,
interpreting available contamination data and information including analytical results, data gaps, and
management requirements necessary to support the proposed employment-led uses. Where contamination
is known or potentially present, recommendations are provided to guide remediation priorities and validation
requirements prior to development. A figure showing the locations of the domains as they relate to the
structure plan is provided in Figure 7, Appendix A.
Throughout the following sections, the term ‘investigation level’ is used which refer to Tier I screening values
included in the NEPM 2013 (or its preceding version) under commercial industrial land use, or other
guidance documents such as NEMPv3.1 and ANZG 2018. Tier I screening values are generic
concentrations, exceedance of which indicate a potential risk could be present in a certain area. Exceedance
of investigation levels or Tier I screening values do not automatically require remediation or management,
but rather further investigation and additional tiers of risk assessments are required. It is possible that
quantitative human health and/or ecological risk assessments can be developed to establish site specific
levels that inform the necessary extent of remediation/management required to render a particular area as
suitable for an intended land use.
5.1
Sub precinct 1 – Cockle Creek
Exceedances of investigation levels have been confirmed across multiple contaminants and domains within
the Cockle Creek sub-precinct. Concentrations of zinc, copper, selenium, uranium, PFAS, nutrients and TRH
in soil were reported above the ecological assessment criteria adopted during previous investigations.
Exceedances of human health criteria for industrial/commercial land use were not detected in soil at this sub-
precinct, however fragments of asbestos in fill were identified in fill materials (Domain 11). Detectable
methane gas emissions were also recorded from mine shafts, indicating a potential ground gas hazard.
While this sub-precinct has been subject to a variety of previous investigations and localised remediation
(Domain 10), data gaps and uncertainties exist within this sub-precinct including Domains 7 and 14, ASS
characterisation, asbestos pipework, and targeted assessment of ground gas, soil/sediment, surface water,
and groundwater. Domain 7 may represent the most significant constraint on the basis as an intrusive
investigation has not been undertaken for this domain. These data gaps impact the ability to confidently
verify land suitability for employment-led uses without additional evidence and management measures.
Based on available data, and with consideration to identified data gaps, known or potential contamination is
unlikely to preclude commercial/industrial rezoning but requires targeted investigation of soil, sediment,

--- Page 26 [Matched: ['contamination', 'hydrocarbons', 'remediation']] ---

5.2
Sub precinct 2 – Killingworth
Exceedances of investigation levels have been confirmed across multiple domains within the Killingworth
sub-precinct. Concentrations of TRH (C10–C16) in fill were detected above adopted ecological assessment
criteria in Domain 1 which were associated with historical fuel storage and former UST areas. Exceedances
of EILs for zinc and TRH were also confirmed in Domain 2, and anomalous enrichment of selenium and
uranium was identified at a targeted location in Domain 12. Exceedances of human health investigation
levels were not identified within this sub-precinct.
Data gaps remain within this sub-precinct including Domains 2 and 14 which have not been subject to
intrusive investigations. Domain 3 is lacking data outside the borrow pit area, and limited assessment of ASS
within this sub-precinct. The absence of intrusive data for Domains 2 and 14 represents the most significant
constraint on land suitability determination.
Based on available data, known or potential contamination is unlikely to preclude commercial/industrial
rezoning of the Killingworth sub-precinct but requires targeted investigation of soil, groundwater, surface
water, soil vapour, and ASS across all domains to confirm land suitability for the proposed light industrial
use. Previously reported hydrocarbon data also require reassessment against current NEPM 2013 criteria.
Implementation of appropriate assessment and management measures during future investigation and
design phases will enable the sub-precinct to support employment-led development.
5.3
Sub precinct 3 – West Lake
Exceedances of ecological assessment criteria have been confirmed at Domain 4 within this sub-precinct.
Concentrations of zinc and nutrients in surface sediments were recorded above ecological screening levels
on the north-west margin of the final void of Domain 4. Hydrocarbons and nutrients also exceeded ESLs in
sediment samples from this domain. Exceedances of ecological or human health assessment criteria were
not detected in the borrow pit area within Domain 3, indicating that soil conditions in this domain are suitable
for the proposed commercial-industrial land use based on available data.
Data gaps constrain land suitability determination across this sub-precinct. Domain 3 lacks intrusive
investigation data outside the borrow pit area and requires hydrocarbon data reassessment under NEPM
2013. Domain 4 presents the most significant constraint, with limited available information on bioremediation
operations and surface water quality retained in the void, insufficiently investigated peripheral areas, and
submerged sediments inaccessible for assessment. While Domains 14 and 16 are considered to represent
low risk areas, they have not been subject to intrusive investigations and remain data gaps.
Based on available data and information, known or potential contamination is unlikely to preclude

5.4
Teralba Southgate
Exceedances of investigation levels have been confirmed for multiple contaminants within Teralba
Southgate. Domain 13 (Teralba Southgate) exhibits confirmed exceedances of ecological criteria for zinc,
TRH, and benzo(a)pyrene (BaP) in soil, and groundwater exceedances for hexavalent chromium, nickel,
zinc, and PFAS. Multiple exceedances of sediment criteria have also been recorded for TRH, cadmium,
copper, lead, mercury, nickel, and zinc. Other identified impacts included the confirmed presence of
chrysotile asbestos in fill material near the methane plant, and methane gas concentrations exceeding the
lower explosive limit (LEL) detected during drilling. However, exceedances of human health criteria for
commercial/industrial land use were not detected during previous investigations, indicating the potential
suitability of this site for industrial use from a human health perspective.
Data gaps and uncertainties remain within this site that limit the ability to fully characterise the contamination
profile and assess suitability for the proposed mixed-use development including high-density residential,
recreation, and commercial-industrial uses. Further assessment of soil and groundwater is required,
particularly sampling beneath the workshop, which has not been completed. Asbestos delineation sampling
has not been undertaken to confirm the full extent of ACM-impacted materials. Surface water sampling has
not been completed, and detailed ground gas assessments remain outstanding. Additionally, the proposed
rezoning to high-density residential mixed-use introduces more sensitive land use scenarios than the current
industrial classification, requiring assessment against residential and public open space criteria rather than
commercial/industrial for certain exposure pathways.
Based on available data and with consideration to identified data gaps and limitations, known or potential
contamination presents a constraint to the proposed high-density residential mixed-use rezoning of this sub-
precinct. While the absence of human health criteria exceedances under commercial/industrial land use
confirms likely suitability for continued light industrial purposes, the proposed rezoning to include high-
density residential and recreational uses introduces more sensitive exposure scenarios that require targeted
investigation and risk assessment. The site is likely suitable for the proposed commercial-industrial and
recreation components of the mixed-use development, provided appropriate assessment and management
controls are implemented during future design and construction phases. However, the residential component
requires completion of targeted investigation of ground gas, and to a lesser extent soil, groundwater, and
surface water to confirm land suitability under residential exposure scenario and to delineate the PFAS
plume extent. Implementation of management measures including potential capping of PFAS-impacted
areas, groundwater monitoring, asbestos delineation, and methane management will be necessary to enable
the this site to support the proposed mixed-use development while protecting environmental values in Cockle

5.6.3
Sub precinct 6c
Exceedances of ecological investigation level criteria have been confirmed within domains associated with
this sub-precinct. Domain 12 contains ecological criteria exceedances for zinc and TRH in fill materials, with
anomalous selenium and uranium enrichment at a targeted location. Human health criteria exceedances
have not been detected at Domain 12 during previous investigations. Domain 14 has not been subject to
intrusive investigation and as such, potential exceedances within this domain cannot be determined.
Domain 12 lacks surface water assessment, and selenium and uranium enrichment largely remains
uncharacterised. Domain 14 represents the most significant data gap as no intrusive investigation has been
undertaken across any media. The absence of investigation data in Domain 14 and lack of detailed
ecological risk assessment for Domain 12 prevent confident verification of land suitability.
Based on available data, known or potential contamination is unlikely to preclude conservation rezoning.
Conservation use is considered compatible with the current contamination profile, provided targeted intrusive
investigation of soil and surface water is undertaken in this sub-precinct. Additional investigation and future
management controls will provide defensible evidence for any future use.
5.6.4
Sub precinct 6d
Exceedances of investigation levels have been confirmed across multiple domains associated with this sub-
precinct. Domain 9 exhibits confirmed EIL exceedances for TRH and zinc in soils, with fibrous asbestos
confirmed near former infrastructure. Domain 10 includes ecological exceedances for heavy metals, TRH,
and PAHs in maturation pond sediments, with PFOS exceeding the 99% species protection criterion in
surface water. Asbestos was confirmed in surface soils near demolished buildings in Domain 11. However,
exceedances of human health criteria have been recorded across any domain within this sub-precinct.
Data gaps limit a detailed understanding of the contamination profile for this sub-precinct. Domain 9 presents
incomplete wastewater cell assessment, absent groundwater data, and incomplete asbestos
characterisation. Domain 10 presents incomplete maturation pond sediment characterisation, limited
groundwater data, and asbestos pipework extent undefined. Domain 11 presents uninvestigated building
footprints, absent groundwater and surface water data, and no methane assessment. No intrusive
investigations have been undertaken for Domain 14.
Based on available data and information, known or potential contamination is unlikely to preclude
conservation rezoning of Conservation Area 6d. However, targeted investigation is required within this sub-
precinct, particularly for sediment and surface water bodies that occupy the northern portion of this sub-
precinct and have not been subject to detailed assessment. Other potential contamination sources

Sub precinct 6f

5.7
Interfaces with other constraints
Management of identified COPCs across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate does not operate in isolation, it
interacts with a number of other environmental and engineering constraints that must be considered in an
integrated manner during Master Plan planning, rezoning, and future DA-stage assessments. The following
section summarises other environmental constraints that require further assessment or consideration at the
Precinct and Teralba Southgate.
5.7.1
Waterways and Wetlands
The Precinct and Teralba Southgate is surrounded by sensitive water bodies including Cockle Creek, Lake
Macquarie, and associated wetlands. Several domains contain contaminants, including heavy metals,
hydrocarbons, nutrients, and PFAS, that may represent a risk of migration to these receiving environments
via surface water runoff and groundwater discharge pathways, including Domains 4, 6, 9, 10, and 13.
Surface water and groundwater investigations must be undertaken at the DA stage for proposals adjacent to
or within the waterway and wetland interface zones, with results assessed against relevant environmental
legislation and guidance including (but not limited to) the Australian and New Zealand Guidelines for Fresh
and Marine Water Quality (ANZG 2018). Where PFAS is identified as a COPC, assessment must be
undertaken in accordance with the PFAS NEMP 3.1. Contamination pathways to these receptors must be
identified and controlled during earthworks and development consistent the performance criteria outlined in

--- Page 31 [Matched: ['contamination', 'tsf', 'tailings', 'pfas', 'remediation']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
22
5.7.3
Salinity
As shown in Figure 4 (Appendix A), portions of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate are classified as
moderate to very high salinity hazard zones, associated with low-lying areas, disturbed terrain, and the
Cockle Creek floodplain interface. Elevated salt concentrations reflect historical mining operations,
evaporative concentration in surface water structures, and estuarine influence from Lake Macquarie. Salinity
hazard presents a material constraint to sensitive land use rezoning and may trigger additional assessment
requirements at the development application stage.
Within environmentally constrained areas (6a–6f), elevated salinity may preclude low-salinity native
vegetation establishment, requiring soil remediation, salt-tolerant species selection, or hydrological
management. In areas where structures are proposed, elevated salinity increases corrosivity risk to buried
infrastructure and requires assessment of foundation stability where shallow groundwater and high
evaporative demand may induce heave or differential settlement. Dewatering and excavation require
management to prevent salt-laden leachate mobilisation.
Salinity risk should be integrated into site-wide water cycle management and contamination control planning.
Detailed salinity assessment, including soil and groundwater profiling, should be undertaken for Teralba
Southgate and all sub-precincts proposed for sensitive land uses prior to development approval. Salinity
management measures including hydrological controls, soil amendment, or engineered containment should
be embedded in Environmental Management Plans (EMPs) and validated through post-construction
monitoring to confirm effectiveness and ongoing compliance with land use suitability criteria.
5.7.4
Tailings Storage Facility
The Tailings Storage Facility (TSF) in Domain 7 represents a discrete and material constraint that operates
across multiple environmental and engineering disciplines, requiring integrated management during Master
Plan planning and future development application assessments. Unlike contamination in other domains, the
TSF presents a fundamental constraint to rezoning and redevelopment that extends beyond standard
remediation pathways, necessitating coordinated consideration with water management, geotechnical
stability, dam safety, and regulatory compliance frameworks.
As outlined in Section 3-4, the TSF is currently being remediated to ensure the final landform is stable and
environmentally sound, however has not been subject to detailed investigation to inform potential
contamination risks. TSFs are known to contain tailings materials with unknown geochemical characteristics
including potential acid-forming materials (PAF), spontaneous combustion potential, and salinity risks.
Leachate generation and acid mine drainage (AMD) from tailings represent primary contamination pathways

8
Post exhibition management framework and
approvals pathway
This section describes how contaminated land will be managed through future planning and development
approvals, consistent with the master plan objectives and performance criteria outlined in Sections 6 and 7.
The management framework is structured around the post-exhibition approvals pathway and identifies both
the obligations and opportunities associated with contamination management across the Precinct and
Teralba Southgate.
8.1
Development application
Development applications for land within the Precinct and Teralba Southgate must be supported by site
specific contamination assessments that confirm site suitability for the proposed use, considerate of the
engineering design, and identify any remediation or management measures required. The nature and extent
of assessment required at the DA stage will be determined by the land use sensitivity, disturbance footprint,
and contamination risk profile of identified contamination sources, consistent with performance criteria 1
(PC1) in Section 6.2.
Through this approach, contamination risks will be addressed progressively and proportionately, supporting
the safe and sustainable transformation of the subject site(s) in line with the proposed vision for the Precinct
and Teralba Southgate. The requirement and triggers for contamination assessment during future DA stages
are outlined in Section 7. All contamination assessments must be prepared by a suitably qualified
contaminated land consultant, consistent with LMCCs requirements for development on contaminated land
and must comply with the NSW EPA Guidelines for Consultants Reporting on Contaminated Sites (2020).
Where reports are submitted to the NSW EPA or otherwise required by the consent authority, assessments
must be prepared or reviewed by a consultant certified under an EPA-recognised scheme (CEnvP-SC) or
equivalent.
8.2
Regulatory notifications and duty to report
Under Section 60 of the Contaminated Land Management Act 1997 (CLM Act), a person who becomes
aware of contamination of land in NSW must notify the NSW EPA if the contamination is of a type, nature, or
extent prescribed by the regulations.
As outlined in this report, several domains contain elevated concentrations of COPCs, however available
data does not confirm contamination at concentrations or extents sufficient to trigger a notification obligation
under Section 60 of the CLM Act at this time. Aurecon notes that this conclusion is limited by the data gaps

--- Page 39 [Matched: ['contamination', 'pfas', 'remediation']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
30
8.3
Opportunities
The Precinct and Teralba Southgate present a number of opportunities to manage contaminated and
potentially contaminated materials in a cost-effective and environmentally responsible manner. Identifying
and pursuing these opportunities at future DA stages can significantly reduce off-site disposal costs and
minimise the environmental footprint of future development. The following opportunities are identified for
consideration in future development planning:
◼ Existing data and information – the contamination investigations completed to date provide a
substantial and layered evidence base across multiple domains that can be directly built upon for any
required DSIs. In areas that have already been characterised to an appropriate level, the scope of new
intrusive investigation can be minimised, reducing both cost and program risk for future development
proposals
◼ On-site bioremediation – hydrocarbon impacted soils may be suitable for treatment via an on-site
bioremediation pad, where excavated material is treated in controlled conditions before being validated
for reuse or disposal. This approach can significantly reduce off-site disposal volumes and associated
costs for domains with confirmed hydrocarbon contamination
◼ Onsite reuse as engineered fill – excavated soils that have been subject to an appropriate level of
investigation and meets NEPM land use criteria and does not contain indicators of contamination can be
reused onsite
◼ Offsite reuse as engineered fill – excavated soils that meet the criteria for VENM or ENM may be
reused offsite as engineered fill, subject to further classification and validation. RROs/RREs should be
considered where applicable for aggregates, slag or other eligible materials
◼ Waste characterisation and soil leachate analysis – TCLP analysis may be undertaken on
contaminated soils to assess their potential for leaching and determine appropriate waste streams. This is
particularly relevant for domains with PFAS, hydrocarbon or heavy metal contamination where soils
require disposal to a licensed waste facility
◼ Mine water reuse – treated mine water may present an opportunity for reuse in construction activities or
irrigation across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate, subject to water quality assessment and appropriate
approvals. This should be considered in the context of integrated water cycle planning for the Precinct
and Teralba Southgate.

9
Conclusions and recommendations
9.1
Conclusions
This assessment included a review of publicly available information and previous investigations completed
progressively at the Precinct and Teralba Southgate since 2005 across several Domains or Areas of
Environmental Concern. The findings of this assessment confirm that soil, groundwater, surface water quality
are generally compatible with industrial and commercial land uses through implementation of a staged, risk-
based assessment and remediation pathway. However, more sensitive land uses including residential,
conservation, and recreational uses require targeted investigation and risk assessment to confirm suitability
and establish appropriate remediation and management controls.
Available data and information demonstrate that exceedances of human health investigation levels for
commercial/industrial land use were not identified during previous investigations. However, ecological
investigation level exceedances were reported in soil and sediment, reflecting the legacy of coal preparation
operations. These ecological impacts require management to protect sensitive receptors including Cockle
Creek and Lake Macquarie, but do not preclude employment-led development provided appropriate
assessment and management controls are implemented. Contamination sources including asbestos in fill
materials, PFAS in soil and groundwater, and hazardous ground gases have been confirmed across multiple
domains and require targeted management consistent with the performance criteria outlined in this
assessment.
Significant data gaps constrain the ability to fully characterise the contamination profile across all domains.
Five domains have not been subject to intrusive investigations, and multiple domains lack groundwater,
surface water, sediment, or soil vapour/ground gas assessment data. These gaps are most material for
sensitive land use scenarios including residential, recreation and conservation. The TSF (Domain 7)
represents a material constraint requiring integrated assessment with water management, geotechnical
stability, and dam safety considerations. Implementation of the staged investigation program will
progressively resolve these data gaps through the development application approval process, enabling risk-
based decision-making.
The management framework established in this assessment provides a clear pathway for contamination
management through future planning and development approvals. Development applications must be
supported by site-specific assessments that confirm suitability for the proposed use based on remediation or
management measures required. Six performance criteria have been established to guide contamination
management:

--- Page 41 [Matched: ['contamination', 'tsf', 'tailings', 'pfas', 'remediation', 'site audit']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
32
9.2
Recommendations
Based on the conclusions provided above, and subject to the limitations outlined in Section 11, Aurecon
presents the following recommendations:
◼ Implement a risk-based, staged investigation program for all future development applications, with
Detailed Site Investigations (DSIs) and Tier 2/3 quantitative risk assessments undertaken where Tier I
screening level concentrations exceedances or data gaps are identified, ensuring land use suitability is
confirmed prior to development approval
◼ Prioritise targeted investigation of Domains 2, 5, 7, 14, and 16 (uninvestigated domains), comprehensive
groundwater assessment across all domains, ASS characterisation in low-lying areas, and asbestos
delineation sampling to resolve material data gaps constraining sensitive land use suitability
determination. Additionally, it is also recommended that further assessment of the environmentally
constrained  areas be undertaken, primarily targeting the medium to high-risk domains (1, 7, 9, and 11),
with lower priority assessment of the low-risk domains to provide broader site coverage where data gaps
exist. This is to ensure that COPCs from other domains have not migrated into the environmentally
constrained areas
◼ Undertake integrated assessment of the TSF across water management, geotechnical stability, dam
safety, and regulatory compliance frameworks, including geochemical characterisation of tailings
materials and assessment of leachate generation and AMD pathways to inform remediation and long-
term management strategy
◼ Undertake targeted surface water and sediment quality assessments adjacent to waterway and wetland
interface zones (including recreational areas within West Lake); and assess PFAS-impacted areas in
accordance with the PFAS National Environmental Management Plan (NEMP 3.1)
◼ Undertake comprehensive ground gas assessment in accordance with NSW EPA Assessment and
management of hazardous ground gases (EPA 2020), with particular focus on Domains 11 and 13 where
methane concentrations were reported. If required, implement ground gas mitigation measures during
building design and construction to prevent vapour intrusion risk to future enclosed structures
◼ Confirm land suitability for more sensitive uses (residential in Teralba , environmentally constrained
areas, and recreational uses) through targeted investigation of soil, groundwater, surface water, and
ground gas; implement PFAS plume delineation, asbestos delineation, and methane management
measures; obtain Site Audit Statements from NSW EPA-accredited Site Auditors where remediation or
management measures are implemented
◼ During construction, implement contamination pathway controls during earthworks and development to

Limitations
This report has been written to inform the Master Plan being prepared for the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate. Subsurface conditions relevant to future construction work should be assessed by contractors
who can make their own interpretation of the factual data provided and perform any additional tests as
necessary for their own purposes and determine the suitability of particular techniques and equipment for the
conditions.
In preparing the report, Aurecon has relied upon publicly available databases, data, surveys, analyses,
designs, plans and other information provided by the DPHI, most of which are referred to in the report (the
data). Except as otherwise stated in the report, Aurecon has not verified the accuracy or completeness of the
data. To the extent that the statements, opinions, facts, information, conclusions and/or recommendations in
the report (conclusions) are based in whole or part on the data, those conclusions are contingent upon the
accuracy and completeness of the data. Aurecon will not be liable in relation to incorrect conclusions should
any data, information or condition be incorrect or have been concealed, withheld, misrepresented or
otherwise not fully disclosed to Aurecon.
In accordance with the scope of services, Aurecon has relied upon the data and has not conducted any
environmental field testing, sampling or laboratory analyses in the preparation of the report. The conclusions
are based upon the data sources included in this report, including field surveys undertaken by Aurecon, and
are therefore merely indicative of the environmental condition of the site at the time of preparing the report,
including the presence or otherwise of contaminants or emissions.
Within the limitations imposed by the scope of services, the assessment of the site and preparation of this
report have been undertaken and performed in a professional manner, in accordance with generally
accepted practices and using a degree of skill and care ordinarily exercised by reputable environmental
consultants under similar circumstances. No other warranty, expressed or implied, is made.

Appendix B – Previous investigations summary

--- Page 55 [Matched: ['contamination', 'heavy metals', 'remediation']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B

B-1. Detailed summary of investigations undertaken at each
Domain
Domain 1 – Westside Administration and Workshop Area
RCA Australia (2005) — Phase 2 Environmental Site Assessment, Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant,
Teralba. RCA Australia was engaged by Oceanic Coal Australia Ltd (OCAL) to undertake a Phase 2
Environmental Site Assessment across the MCPP and associated areas. Assessment criteria were based on
NEPM 1999 HIL F (Commercial/Industrial). Soil sampling targeted the administration and workshop area,
former underground storage tank (UST) location, oil lubricant storage area, and vehicle fleet area. No
exceedances of HIL F criteria were recorded. Hydrocarbon impacts were identified in the vicinity of the
former UST and oil storage area. Building footprints and the car park area were not investigated due to the
presence of infrastructure.
SLR (2017) — Contaminated Site Assessment, OCAL Complex (site-wide) (Rev C, 22 February 2017).
SLR undertook a site-wide CSA across Domains 1, 3, 4, 6, 9, 10, 11, 12, and 13 which included soil
sampling at 250 locations, groundwater sampling at seven locations, and laboratory analysis for heavy
metals, TRH, BTEXN, PAHs, phenols, OCPs, OPPs, PCBs, ASS (SPOCAS), pH, asbestos, and nutrients.
No HIL/HSL (NEPM 2013) exceedances were recorded. Exceedance of the ESL criterion for TRH F2 (C10–
C16) was recorded in one fill soil sample indicating limited impacts to the fill/topsoil material across this
domain. Building footprints and the car park area remain largely uninvestigated.
Domain 2 – North Rehabilitation Area
No available investigation data. The domain is identified as a former rehabilitation area associated with the
Westside Mine.
Domain 3 – South-West Pit / Borrow Pit Area
SLR (2017) — Contaminated Site Assessment. Soil sampling was undertaken within the borrow pit area of
Domain 3. No exceedances of health or ecological investigation criteria were recorded No available
investigation data for the broader Domain 3 area outside the borrow pit.
Domain 4 – Westside Final Void / Bioremediation Facility / Sediment Disposal Area
SLR (2017) — Contaminated Site Assessment. Domain 4 was inspected as part of the pre-work
assessment. No available intrusive investigation data. SLR noted that no information was available regarding
the bioremediation facility or the removal of a former fuel storage element. The domain was identified as
containing a proposed final void, bioremediation facility, and sediment disposal area.
Domain 5 – Voluntary Conservation Areas
No available investigation data. The domain is identified as a Voluntary Conservation Area (VCA) partially
associated with the Westside Mine.

--- Page 56 [Matched: ['contamination', 'tsf', 'tailings', 'heavy metals', 'pfas']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
40
Coffey (2019) — Contamination Site Assessment – Catchment Dams 1, 2 and 3 (16 April 2019; Rev1, 7
August 2019). Coffey were engaged to assess three Catchment Dams (Dam 1: ~2.4 ha; Dam 2: ~1.14 ha;
Dam 3: ~5 ha) located within the MCPP, which received runoff from coal processing areas, coarse rejects
emplacement, product coal stockpiles, and former site infrastructure. Dam 3 has a licensed discharge point
(LDP1) to Cockle Creek under EPL No. 1360. The assessment included 81 sediment samples, 6 soil
samples, and 15 surface water samples, analysed for heavy metals, TRH, BTEX, PAH, PFAS,
microbiological indicators, ASS (SCR/AVS), EC, pH, CEC, clay content, iron content, and TOC. Assessment
criteria included both Recreational/Public Open Space and Commercial/Industrial investigation levels.
Exceedances of ecological criteria were recorded for zinc in Catchment Dams 1, 2, and 4, and for chromium
in Catchment Dam 4. PFAS was detected in surface water and sediments. PASS was confirmed in sediment
samples. Statistical appraisal confirmed zinc exceedances above SQGs and EILs in Catchment Dam 1. No
human health criteria exceedances were recorded.
Coffey (2020) — Contaminated Site Assessment – Pit Top Catchment Dams, West Wallsend Colliery.
Additional assessment of the Pit Top Catchment Dams (Dams 1–4) was undertaken in 2020, building on the
previous assessment completed in 2019 and included additional surface water and groundwater sampling
around the Catchment Dams including upstream and downstream locations. Results confirmed residual
heavy metal and PFAS impacts in sediments and surface water. Groundwater data confirmed localised
impacts in the shallow unconfined aquifer.
Coffey (2019) — Sediment and Surface Water Sampling for Presence of PFAS within Dam G,
Westside Mine (11 November 2019) (C105). Targeted PFAS assessment of Dam G (Westside Mine) was
undertaken following identification of PFAS in the broader MCPP catchment area. Results confirmed PFAS
detections in sediment and surface water within Dam G.
SLR (2015) — Hazardous Building Materials Management Survey Report. This HBM survey also
included Domain 6 area as well as other domains to identify hazardous materials in structures including
ACM. ACM was confirmed in multiple building components across Domain 6.
Domain 7 – Tailings Storage Facility (TSF)
No available investigation data. The TSF is understood to contain tailings from coal processing operations at
the West Wallsend Colliery and Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant. A Dam Safety Review (DSR) and dam
break failure impact assessment have been undertaken. The TSF is identified as a major constraint to
rezoning and redevelopment of the broader Precinct.
Domain 8 – Rejects Emplacement Area
No available investigation data.
Domain 9 – MCPP Surface Facilities

--- Page 57 [Matched: ['contamination', 'tailings', 'heavy metals', 'pfas', 'remediation']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B

areas. Exceedances of ecological criteria were recorded for TRH and zinc in soils. Asbestos was confirmed
near former infrastructure. Five samples were tested for ASS in Domain 9, with four sediment samples from
Pond 3 and one from the rail loop. ASS testing was deemed inconclusive. Groundwater data was limited to
the deeper fractured rock aquifer, with limited assessment of the shallow unconfined aquifer.
Coffey (2018) — Desktop Peer Review of Previous Site Assessment Works, West Wallsend Colliery.
Coffey undertook a desktop review of the SLR (2017) assessment, identifying data gaps which included:
inadequate ASS characterisation across closure Domains 9 and 10, absence of shallow groundwater data,
ecological risk uncertainties and need to compare sediment data against NSW EPA Biosolids Guidelines
given treated effluent impacts.
Coffey (2019, 2020) — Catchment Dams Assessment. Catchment Dams 1–3 are located at the Domain
9/10 interface and receive runoff from Domain 9 surface facilities. Refer to Domain 6 summary above.
Domain 10 – Maturation Ponds
SLR (2017) — Contaminated Site Assessment. Domain 10 was assessed as part of the site-wide CSA.
Soil and sediment sampling targeted the maturation pond areas. Exceedances of ecological criteria were
recorded for heavy metals, TRH, and PAHs in maturation pond sediments. Two samples were tested for
ASS in Domain 10, but no sediment samples were tested from the maturation ponds. Available groundwater
data was limited.
Coffey (2019) — Maturation Pond Remediation Validation Report (Draft, 1 July 2019). Coffey undertook
remediation validation of the Maturation Ponds following removal of impacted sediments. Validation sampling
confirmed that remediation objectives had been achieved across most of the pond areas, with the exception
of two residual locations which still returned some ecological criteria exceedances. Sediments from
temporary holding cells were relocated to the Tailings Dam. ASS neutralisation was undertaken and verified.
ACM was identified and removed during rehabilitation works.
Coffey (2021) — Maturation Pond Remediation Validation Report (24 February 2021). Updated
validation reporting confirmed residual exceedances at two locations. Additional groundwater monitoring was
recommended to assess if heavy metal and PFAS concentrations were declining following remediation. The
south-eastern corner of Maturation Pond 5 (former pump/pipework area) was not validated at the time of
reporting.
Domain 11 – Teralba Northgate
RCA Australia (2005) — Phase 2 Environmental Site Assessment. RCA Australia undertook Phase 2
assessment across the Teralba Northgate area, targeting the workshop, ASTs, and former infrastructure.
Hydrocarbon impacts were identified. ACM was observed in soils.
SLR (2017) — Contaminated Site Assessment. Domain 11 was assessed as part of the Precinct and

--- Page 58 [Matched: ['contamination', 'hydrocarbons', 'heavy metals', 'pfas', 'remediation', 'capping']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
42
selenium and uranium were identified at a section of Haul Road. No human health criteria exceedances were
recorded. PASS was identified in low-lying areas. Groundwater data was not available for Domain 12.
Domain 13 – Teralba Southgate
RCA Australia (2005) — Phase 2 Environmental Site Assessment. RCA Australia undertook Phase 2
assessment across the Teralba Southgate area, targeting the workshop, ASTs, methane plant, and surface
water storage dam. Hydrocarbon impacts were identified in the vicinity of the workshop and hydrocarbon
drain facility. Deep ground penetration of hydrocarbons was also observed.
SLR (2015) — Hazardous Building Materials Management Survey. ACM confirmed in multiple building
components across Domain 13.
SLR (2017) — Contaminated Site Assessment. Domain 13 was assessed as part of the Precinct and
Teralba Southgate CSA. Soil and sediment sampling targeted the Southgate surface facilities, methane plant
area, and surface water storage dam. Exceedances of ecological criteria were recorded for heavy metals,
TRH, and PAHs in soils and sediments. Deep hydrocarbon impacts were also identified. Methane gas
facilities were present. Various buildings and structures were inaccessible during the investigation.
Groundwater sampling was undertaken at limited locations, with potential for off-site migration of metals
towards Cockle Bay identified.
Coffey (2018) — Dam Sediment Assessment, Teralba Southgate. Surface water monitoring identified
seepage and overflows as potential contaminant pathways. Seven sediment and three surface water
samples were analysed for COPCs against Australian and New Zealand Guidelines for Fresh and Marine
Water Quality (2018) criteria. Sediment showed exceedances for TRH (C6–C36) and heavy metals
(cadmium, copper, nickel, mercury, zinc) above criteria; surface water samples were below criteria. TRH
concentrations were primarily attributable to coal fines with limited mobility and low subsurface leaching
potential. Metal concentrations in water were low to non-detectable with minimal bioavailability and toxicity
risk. The assessment concluded low risk to human health and the environment for current and proposed light
industrial use; no remedial works required.
Tetra Tech Coffey (2021) — Contaminated Site Assessment, Teralba Southgate (16 November 2021).
Soil sampling from 32 locations and groundwater sampling from six existing monitoring wells were
undertaken to address data gaps and inform future mine closure and rehabilitation planning. No detectable
methane, health-based risks, or ecological risks were identified. TRH (>C16–C34) exceedances were
recorded at depth within fill materials and considered acceptable on a qualitative risk basis. Groundwater
heavy metal exceedances (arsenic, copper, nickel, and zinc) were attributed to regional conditions
associated with prolonged industrial activity. PFOS exceedances were identified in a down-gradient
monitoring well, with detectable PFAS analytes in adjacent wells, likely associated with former firefighting

B-2. Summary of Acid Sulfate Soil and Salinity Assessment
Data
Acid Sulfate Soils Assessment Data Summary
Site-specific ASS investigations have been undertaken across several domains, primarily focused on the
MCPP sediment management areas with key findings summarised below:
◼ Domain 9 (MCPP Surface Facilities and Catchment Dams 1–3). Tested sediment samples (17 of 20) as
part of Coffey (2019) assessment returned exceedances of the the SCR action criteria, indicating the
presence of Actual Acid Sulfate Soils (AASS) and Potential Acid Sulfate Soils (PASS) within dam
sediments. Samples from Dams 2 and 3 exhibited the highest sulfide concentrations and confirmed active
acid generation at that time. Presence of monosulfides (AVS) was also confirmed for Dam 3. Coffey
(2019) recommended preparation of an Acid Sulfate Soil Management Plan (ASSMP) prior to any
dewatering or sediment disturbance. A subsequent assessment of the Pit Top Catchment Dams (Coffey,
2020) assessed four dams (Dams 1–4) at WWC. Sulfide concentrations in sediment samples were all
reported below the ASS action criteria, with the exception of minor detections in Dam 1. No AVS
exceedances were recorded
◼ Domain 10 (Maturation Ponds – MCPP). Sediments within five Maturation Ponds at the MCPP tested as
part of Coffey (2018) were assessed as acid generating once dewatered. Subsequent rehabilitation works
by KCE which included neutralisation with lime treatment applied to sediments from the five ponds prior to
relocation to a temporary holding cell and eventual backfill into the Tailings Dam (Domain 7). Validation
monitoring (Coffey, 2019 — Maturation Ponds Validation Report) recorded pHf values of 6.0–8.1 and
pHfox values of 2.4–7.7 across treated stockpiles, reflecting the difficulty of achieving uniform lime mixing
in heavy clay sediments. However, the risk of further acidification of treated material was assessed as low
given the lined containment cell and lime application records
◼ Domain 12 (Infrastructure / Haul Road). SLR (2017) identified PASS in underlying natural soils based on
SPOCAS analysis. An ASS management plan was recommended. ASS characterisation in this domain
remains incomplete
◼ Domain 6 (WWC). The domain includes low-lying areas associated with former wastewater management
cells (North Dam, Old Transpiration Area, Top Dam, Bottom Dam) that have not been assessed for ASS.
◼ Domain 14. ASS risk is mapped in low-lying areas proximate to Cockle Creek but no intrusive
investigation have been undertaken at this domain to date.
SLR (2017) identified the absence of ASS assessment across the majority of the Precinct and Teralba
Southgate as a data gap.
Salinity Assessment Data Summary

 Provide a framework to support inputs into the State Environmental Planning Policy (Precincts-Regional)

settlement or completion of stabilisation activities), and translate this to ‘short/medium/long-term’ triggers
 Provide a clear pathway for post rezoning phase investigations and considerations (further investigations,
testing, monitoring etc).
1.3
Methodology
The methodology adopted for this Report is desktop based and summarised as follows:
 Review of available information on the TSF regarding the investigation, design and construction
 Review of dambreak & consequence assessment
 Review of surveillance and monitoring practice implemented at the dam
 Review of dam safety risks
 Review of land use planning scenarios within the Precinct
 Analysis of activities required to inform post mining land use planning as part of a State led rezoning.
1.4
Reliance on data and information
The advice and recommendations in this Report rely on the accuracy of the existing input documentation and
advice provided to date. This includes the following documentation:
1. Post Mining Land Use – Deliverability of Pilot Projects – Due Diligence Report – Macquarie Coal
Complex, prepared by Aurecon in February 2026

Regulatory Context
In New South Wales (NSW), dam safety is enforced by the Dams Safety NSW Act 2015 (the Act) and the
Dams Safety NSW Regulation 2019 (the Regulation). Dams Safety NSW is the state regulator for dam safety
and responsible for developing and implementing regulations for effective dam safety management to protect
life, property, and the environment from dam failures. Dam Safety NSW replaces the former Dams Safety
Committee (DSC).
Owners of declared dams, and their professional advisors, are accountable to determine and take
appropriate dam safety management action to prevent the potential failure of their dams and to mitigate
associated consequences. Responsibility for dam safety obligations remains with the declared dam owner
unless formally transferred in accordance with the Act, requiring regulatory approval. Dams Safety NSW will
audit declared dam owners’ policies, processes, and procedures to check compliance with the requirements
of the Regulation.
In accordance with the Regulation, declared dam owners must establish, implement and maintain a Dam
Safety Management System (DSMS) to ensure the ongoing safety of their dam throughout the dam’s
lifecycle. The DSMS provides a comprehensive and integrated system for the management of all aspects of
foreseeable risks in relation to the failure of the dam. It provides a structured approach for the development,
coordination and control of activities undertaken to ensure that these dam failure risks are reduced so far as
is reasonably practicable (SFAIRP). The DSMS must address the following as set out in the Regulation:
a. the procedures for managing risks in accordance with Clause 14,
b. the procedures for reporting incidents in accordance with Clause 19,
c.
the procedures for conducting a safety review in accordance with Clause 20,
d. the procedures for notifying changes to the dam in accordance with Clause 21,
e. the procedures for ensuring compliance with AS ISO 55001:2014,
f.
the procedures for review of the matters in paragraphs (a) to (e),
g. the resources that will be applied for the effective implementation and use of the DSMS.
In accordance with the Regulation, Clause 14, the owner of a declared dam must establish a risk
management framework inclusive of the following components:
 hazard identification process
 a risk analysis process

4.2
Dambreak & consequence assessments
This section provides a strategic, desktop-based assessment of existing dam break conditions, constraints
and opportunities associated with the Tailings Storage Facility (TSF) within the Precinct. It also provides
recommended actions for the post-rezoning phase. The assessment considers the TSF embankments,
capping works, potential dam failures, previous dam break assessments and dam safety requirements, and
included a review of the following documents:
 Dam break failure impact assessment (ATCW, 2023)
 West Wallsend Dambreak Presentation Slides (ATCW, 2024)
 Surveillance review reports (ATCW 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024, 2025).
The TSF has two embankments: the eastern embankment (EE) and northern saddle dam (NSD). Following
the completion of Stage 1 capping, Stage 2 capping commenced in 2021. Thus far, for Stage 2 capping (29
June 2021 to November 2024), approximately 435,000 m3 (~356,000 m3 in-situ after initial settlement) of fly
ash has been hydraulically deposited into the TSF. Stage 2 Phase 3 fly ash deposition has been suspended
to allow conventional capping.
Of the two embankments, the eastern embankment has a wide crest and flat downstream slopes as a result
of the Rejects Emplacement Area (REA) progressively constructed by dumping, spreading and track-rolling
coarse rejects in lifts to an ultimate surface level to that of the eastern embankment. As such, only the failure
of the NSD is envisaged.
The latest dambreak study was undertaken in 2023 (ATCW), which considered three incremental failure
consequences for the NSD. The population at risk (PAR) was updated in 2024 and potential loss of life (PLL)
was also estimated in 2024. Incremental Flood Failure (IFF) considered in the dambreak of NSD in 2023 is
very conservative. A scenario for spillway in operation without obstruction and with downstream concurrent
inflow during PMF (natural flooding) without dambreak compared with dambreak will be more realistic for
IFF.
The dam was re-classified in 2024 as ‘Significant’ consequence category dam for sunny day failure based on
a PLL of 0.6 and a medium severity of damage and loss. The surveillance report (ATCW, 2024) and the
presentation slides of the updated consequence assessment do not provide information on flood failure
related PAR/PLL. TSF is a declared dam under the Dam Safety Act 2015 and the Dam Safety Regulation
2019. TSF must comply with the requirements of the Dam Safety Act 2015 and the Dam Safety Regulation
2019.
Inundation extent during a sunny day failure as shown in the surveillance report (ATCW, 2024) is presented

 Intermediate surveillance inspections and reporting for the dam are undertaken on an annual basis by
Engineer of Record. Five yearly comprehensive surveillance inspection and reporting are undertaken
once every five years by an Independent Dam Specialist
 Given that the risk assessment was undertaken recently in 2025, the next 5-yearly risk review is due in
2030
 The TSF has following instrumentation currently for monitoring of the dam performance:
− There are ten (10) active standpipe piezometers with real time sensors installed within the NSD which
extend into the foundation
− A v-notch weir with automated seepage monitoring installed together with a camera
− Seven (7) monitoring bores were installed within the Reject Emplacement Area (REA) to establish an
understanding of phreatic surface levels in the area. REA is situated immediately adjacent to the TSF,
on the eastern side
− A v-notch weir also exists in the south-east REA toe drain adjacent to the TSF South Ramp
− In November 2023, a ground control network to facilitate aerial (photogrammetry) surveys was
installed. The network consists of thirty-two (32) ground control points and ten (10) check control
points. Twenty-five (25) survey prisms were also installed across the downstream slope of the NSD to
monitor embankment movements.
 The NSD standpipe piezometers and v-notch weir are continuously monitored using eagle.io (with
assigned trigger levels). Embankment movement is monitored fortnightly using birdi.io (with assigned
trigger levels). Additionally, storage and ongoing fly ash deposition are monitored regularly via
photogrammetry processed in the birdi.io platform. The collected data is analysed weekly to understand
the developing trends.
4.4
Dam safety risk
The following information is gathered from the NSD risk assessment and SFAIRP framework report by
ATCW (2025).
Engineering assessment for the dam (embankment and spillway) was undertaken in 2025 following
engineering standards, guidelines and references.
Risk assessment was then undertaken in a sequence starting with failure mode and effect analysis (FMEA),
load partitioning (flood, seismic), event trees (piping and non-piping), estimation of conditional probability of
failure, consequence of failure, risk integration (annual probability of failure, risk estimates) and risk
evaluation.

5.1
Downstream dambreak inundation zone
5.1.1
Dambreak and consequence assessment
The update to population at risk (PAR) and potential loss of life (PLL) undertaken in 2024 by ATCW
considers sunny day failure related PAR and PLL. Flood failure related incremental PAR and PLL is
calculated based on the dambreak inundation extent for PMF events with and without dam failure. The
inundation extent for a flood failure may be different to a sunny day failure inundation extent, although the
incremental PAR/PLL may be insignificant.
Currently, development  is restricted to areas higher than the maximum inundation levels on the downstream
side of the dam as it will have potential to increase the PAR/PLL which may increase the baseline dam
safety risks in contrast to the requirement of Dam Safety NSW (Gazette 113, 2022), which directs that the
risks must be reduced so far as is reasonably practicable (SFAIRP).
5.1.2
Rehabilitation
The areas downstream of the dam that are impacted by natural flooding should be rehabilitated after each
flooding event. This might include maintaining the walking tracks, erosion protection works, routine
vegetation management along the floodplain, construction of flood levees to protect areas of significance
from the next flooding etc. Severe change to the inundation zone may have significant impact on the
dambreak inundation flow characteristics (flow depths, velocities and extent) compared to what has been
estimated in the 2023/2024 dambreak modelling.
5.1.3
Development within the inundation zone
The area downstream of the NSD may be suitable for the development of walking trails, subject to
appropriate risk management constraints. It may also be utilised for low-impact biodiversity enhancement
works, provided that such activities do not obstruct potential dam break flood pathways or increase
inundation levels.
Following the completion of TSF rehabilitation works, and as the phreatic surface within the TSF
progressively lowers to a stable condition, updated dam break assessments should be undertaken
periodically. These assessments will refine the predicted inundation extents over time.
As inundation extents reduce, portions of land downstream may be progressively released for potential
development, subject to confirmation of geotechnical stability and safety constraints. Concurrently, land-use

--- Page 23 [Matched: ['monitoring']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU State Led Rezoning_ Dam Report_RevB_CLEAN COPY.docx  2026-07-01
Revision B
18
5.4
De-declaration of NSD
5.4.1
Natural infiltration
Currently, the rehabilitation of the TSF is being undertaken, and a final landform will be created which will
allow channelling run-off from the TSF area to drains on the finished impermeable surface and route the run-
off away from the TSF. This will prevent rainfall infiltration into the TSF. The stored tailings will rely on natural
infiltration for reduction in the phreatic level. Once the TSF does not have significant volume that can flow
downstream during a potential dambreak event and that there is no PAR/PLL, the dam will be a candidate
for de-declaration. Even then, the inundation extent (much reduced compared to the baseline extent) shall
be avoided for any development that will have the potential to introduce PAR/PLL.
5.4.2
Accelerated dewatering
The de-declaration process may be accelerated by providing additional drainage pathways within the
tailings, such as through the installation of horizontal or vertical drains. The effectiveness of these measures
should be assessed during the concept design stage, particularly given the relatively low permeability of the
tailings.
The current water level within the tailings is not well defined; therefore, installation of additional groundwater
monitoring wells is recommended to better characterise groundwater levels and flow directions.
5.5
Conclusions and recommendations
The TSF is a declared dam under the Dam Safety Act 2015 and Dam Safety Regulation 2019. This requires
complying with the Dam Safety legislation to ensure that the dam is operated and maintained safely,
required assessments are undertaken from time to time and implementation of remedial or safety measures
mandated by the authority are undertaken.
The dam is currently compliant with the recommendations stipulated in various Australian National
Committee on Large Dams (ANCOLD) guidelines and Dam Safety NSW guidelines based on the
surveillance report from 2025. Dambreak and consequence assessments were undertaken in 2023 and
updated in 2024. A safety review of the dam has been undertaken in 2025 followed by risk assessment and
‘so far as is reasonably practicable’ (SFAIRP) assessment. A staged capping programme is implemented at
the dam and is still ongoing.
Until the dam is de-declared, the dam safety monitoring and surveillance activities shall continue to be

Figure 5-6
Recommendations to support staged development of the TSF
The overarching aims of the recommendations to be translated into the Master Plan are as follows:
 To enable safe, adaptive, and long-term development of the TSF area by embedding a risk-informed
planning framework that recognises and responds to the Tailings Storage Facility and associated dam
break hazards over its rehabilitation and post-closure phases until de-declared.
 To maintain a high level of dam safety by continuing regular inspections, monitoring, and maintenance so
that any issues are identified early and addressed quickly.
 To regularly update understanding of flood risk from a potential dam failure as conditions improve over
time, so that more land can safely be considered for future development.
 To minimise risk to people by carefully controlling development near the TSF, limiting sensitive land uses
in affected areas, and ensuring infrastructure is safely designed, until the dam is no longer declared.

Executive summary
Lake Macquarie City Council engaged Aurecon to undertake an economic, social and market assessment of
redevelopment opportunities for the Macquarie Coal Complex (Precinct) and Teralba Southgate, collectively referred
to as the “Site”. The assessment has been undertaken to provide an evidence base for future land use decision-making
as the Site transitions through closure and rehabilitation towards productive post-mining uses. This report ("Package
E") brings together baseline demographic, labour market, economic, social and market evidence to support the Master
Plan process. It tests the land use directions emerging from the Enquiry by Design process and considers their
feasibility, economic contribution, social implications, market readiness, risks and dependencies.  The assessment is
prepared for an early planning and pre-investment context. It provides a structured evidence base to support land use
decision-making, future business case development and further technical testing.
Market assessment and land use opportunities for employment
The Site's scale, industrial legacy, proximity to established communities, access to regional labour markets and
potential infrastructure links favour employment-generating uses. The land use opportunities are grouped into three
categories. Table 0-1 summarises land use opportunities by priority, time horizon and employment.
Table 0-1: Market assessment summary of potential post-mining land uses1
Land use
Priority
Time horizon
Employment
generation (ongoing)

Circular

advanced

Up to 40 jobs per

manufacturing uses.

--- Page 6 [Matched: ['demand', 'employment', 'jobs', 'data centres', 'manufacturing', 'logistics']] ---
6
The categories above reflect the relative readiness and strategic role of each land use within the Site, having regard to
market demand, alignment with site attributes, infrastructure and servicing requirements, and the extent of further
investigation and delivery planning required. These are defined below:
▪ Highly feasible: circular economy, industrial and advanced manufacturing, waste and resource recovery, and
energy transition infrastructure and renewables. These uses have near-term feasibility, alignment with the
Site's scale, industrial legacy and workforce. They can proceed earliest and anchor investment.
▪ Conditional: freight and logistics, data centres, and community, tourism and agriculture. These uses have
strategic merit but depend on conditions being met first, principally infrastructure capacity, market maturity,
anchor tenants and policy support. They are sequenced to later horizons as those conditions are resolved.
▪ Complementary: residential uses support the Site's function, liveability and public benefit but are not the
primary economic drivers.
The Site’s key opportunity lies in its flexibility to accommodate a range of employment-generating, infrastructure-led
and complementary uses as market, infrastructure and policy conditions evolve. In total, these uses have the
potential to support around 1,130 ongoing jobs.
Social implications and consideration for land use
▪ Minimising social impacts and sustaining social outcomes will depend on how effectively economic benefits
are accessed, shared and supported over time.
▪ Transport access is the key consideration across all land uses. Limited public transport accessibility will
reduce future access to jobs and services, particularly for lower-income households, older residents, people
with disabilities and those without private vehicles.
▪ Employment-generating uses (freight, logistics, data centres) offer strong economic potential but introduce
trade-offs such as increased traffic, amenity impacts and pressure on local services. Industrial and circular
economy uses align with regional transition objectives, but their social outcomes depend on how well impacts
are managed and local workforce pathways are enabled.
▪ Complementary uses (community infrastructure, housing, recreation, health services) are likely to deliver
more direct social benefits, supporting liveability, wellbeing and place identity.
▪ Cumulative impacts from workforce growth, concurrent regional activity, and construction and operational
phases will increase demand for housing and services and require coordinated planning.
▪ Social outcomes can be strengthened through improved transport access, local employment pathways,
Aboriginal participation and community benefit sharing.
Key concluding considerations and next steps
▪ The Site has credible potential to contribute to Lake Macquarie's economic transition and employment land
supply and is not immediately market-ready for all uses. Success will depend on staged de-risking, clear
planning pathways, infrastructure sequencing, targeted market testing and land use flexibility.

Introduction and background
This Section focuses on project overview, Site, the purpose of the report, strategic alignment and relationship to other
packages.
1.1 Project overview
The Macquarie Coal Complex Pilot Project was initiated as part of the NSW Government’s wider response to the
Beneficial and productive post-mining land use Parliamentary Inquiry. 4  In July 2025 the NSW Government committed
to undertake two pilot projects to demonstrate how alternative post-mining land uses can be delivered under the
existing planning and regulatory framework.
The Macquarie Coal Complex Master Plan was prepared by the Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure
(DPHI) in partnership with Lake Macquarie City Council (Council), Net Zero Economy Authority (NZEA) and relevant
NSW Government agencies involved in post-mining land use, regional development and investment delivery. The
Department and Council have undertaken master planning informed by technical studies, to support the coordinated
transition of a former mine site to alternative employment generating land uses in the Precinct
The Rezoning Proposal applies to approximately 1,160 hectares of land which includes the Macquarie Coal Complex
Precinct and Teralba Southgate, collectively referred to as the “Site”.
The Precinct sits adjacent to the Lake Macquarie Northwest Catalyst Area.  It is bounded by the suburb of Barnsley to
the north, Cockle Creek and the Central-Coast Newcastle main passenger and freight train line to the east, Teralba and
Wakefield to the south and Killingworth to the west. The M1 Pacific Motorway and Sugarloaf State Conservation Area
adjoin the western boundary of the Site.
Teralba Southgate is located at 91 York Street, Teralba, approximately 1.5 km southeast of the Precinct, 400 m from
Teralba Train Station and 250 m from Booragul Train Station.
Strategic road and rail connections position the Site to support established and emerging industries and contribute to
a diverse post-mining economy. The Site also provides opportunities to protect and enhance natural systems, cultural
and historic assets, and support long-term community and environmental benefit.
The Site is located in a region with a strong and transferable skills base in mining, heavy industry, logistics,
construction and land management. As regional mining operations transition toward closure, these skills represent a
significant asset that can be redirected to support new and emerging industries.
The Master Plan identifies the arrangement of developable areas, environmentally constrained areas, recreational
areas, movement networks and key constraints across the Site and outlines the spatial logic for future development at
a precinct scale, providing a framework within which detailed design, subdivision and development assessment will
occur.
The Site is a non-operational mining site located within the Lake Macquarie Local Government Area (LGA) undergoing
rehabilitation. The Site forms part of a broader regional landscape that is undergoing economic, environmental and

--- Page 13 [Matched: ['employment', 'jobs']] ---
3
This assessment tests how former mining land can be planned, enabled and transitioned to support employment-
generating land uses, regional economic resilience and broader community benefit.
Figure 1-1: Site context and Master Plan land use areas
Figure 1-1 shows the Site context and Master Plan land use areas, including developable areas, environmentally
constrained areas and further investigation areas.
1.2 Lake Macquarie's strategic role in regional transition economy
Lake Macquarie is strategically positioned within the Greater Newcastle and Hunter regional economy, with a large
employment base and strong connectivity to the Port of Newcastle, established freight networks, major transport
corridors, the Central Coast and Sydney. 5  These locational advantages support the area’s role in the Lower Hunter
economic system and provide a basis for future employment-generating land uses linked to regional transition.
The Lake Macquarie community strongly supports the economic transition away from coal alongside a clear
expectation that redevelopment delivers new jobs, affordable housing, and improved infrastructure.6 Residents place
high value on environmental quality, public spaces, and overall liveability, while expressing concerns about housing
affordability, transport connectivity, and involvement in decision-making.7
5 Lake Macquarie City Council (2024) North West Catalyst Area Place Strategy.
6 Lake Macquarie City Council (2025) Community Strategic Plan 2025–2035.
7Place Score (2023) 2023 Australian Liveability Census Lake Macquarie City Council - Priorities, Values and Performance Report

--- Page 14 [Matched: ['demand', 'employment', 'jobs', 'manufacturing', 'logistics']] ---
4
Forecasts indicate that Lake Macquarie may require around 113 hectares of additional employment land under the
most likely growth scenario, with current vacant serviced employment land falling short of projected demand by
around 54 hectares by 2046.8 This highlights a key constraint on economic development, particularly where land is not
appropriately located, serviced, or zoned to meet industry needs.
Lake Macquarie’s key employment land use planning goals are:
◼
Maintaining a long-term pipeline of serviced employment
◼
Aligning infrastructure provision with growth
◼
Enabling adaptive reuse of post-mining and energy land
◼
Supporting emerging industries such as the circular economy.
Figure 1-2 provides a snapshot of the Lake Macquarie LGA.
Figure 1-2: Lake Macquarie LGA economic snapshot9
Lake Macquarie LGA reflects the Hunter’s broader regional economic transition. The economy is shifting from a goods-
producing base toward service-oriented industries, with manufacturing and mining declining from 37% to 29% of total
output over time.10 Major assets such as Eraring Power Station and associated mining operations have historically
underpinned employment and supply chains.
Mining contraction has had cascading impacts across freight, logistics, engineering, manufacturing, and port
operations with a loss of 1,200 coal mining reliant jobs between 2011 and 2021 in the Lake Macquarie LGA.11 Future
employment growth is expected to be concentrated in healthcare, education, retail, construction and hospitality, with
up to 15,000 additional jobs by 2046 within the Lake Macquarie LGA.12 This growth is expected to drive increasing
demand for employment land, particularly for larger-scale commercial construction, industrial services and business
support sectors which rely on well-serviced, appropriately zoned employment land to operate efficiently.
8Lake Macquarie City Council (2026) Draft Employment Land Use Strategy.
9Ibid.
10Ibid.
11Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS) (2021) Industry of employment (INDP).
12Lake Macquarie City Council (2026) Draft Employment Land Use Strategy.

--- Page 15 [Matched: ['employment', 'jobs', 'logistics']] ---
5
The Precinct is therefore strategically relevant because it seeks to address constrained employment land supply while
supporting broader economic transition objectives. The Site should therefore be considered as part of a broader
transition landscape, not as an isolated land parcel.
The strongest strategic fit is likely to be for uses that:
◼
Leverage existing infrastructure and access advantages
◼
Support employment generating activity
◼
Align with circular economy, industrial, energy transition and logistics opportunities
◼
Avoid areas affected by significant environmental, flooding, contamination, subsidence or rehabilitation
constraints
◼
Delivered through a staged and infrastructure aligned pathway.
1.3 Purpose and scope of this report
The purpose of this report is to establish the strategic case for the Site by testing whether the land use directions in the
draft Master Plan are market-grounded, socially equitable and aligned with regional development goals.
This report consolidates the findings of the staged scope of work, emerging through the Enquiry by Design (EbD)
workshop, 13  to identify the opportunities, constraints, risks and dependencies associated with future post-mining land
use. The report specifically seeks to:
◼
Establish the demographic, labour market, economic and social context relevant to the Site and its
surrounding communities.
◼
Identify the current and potential future economic role of the Site within Lake Macquarie, the broader Hunter
and Central Coast context, and the NSW economy.
◼
Assess the alignment between existing economic conditions, workforce capability, infrastructure context and
potential land use directions.
◼
Consider the social implications of potential land use directions, including community benefit, social
infrastructure, access to jobs, equity, inclusion and Aboriginal participation.
◼

2 Preliminary land use feasibility assessment
This Section presents a preliminary land use feasibility assessment of the Site to identify key opportunities and
constraints that may influence future development. The assessment provides a high-level review of the existing land
use context and site characteristics to support the identification of suitable land use and employment outcomes.
2.1 Land use feasibility assessment framework
Figure 2-1 shows the land use feasibility assessment framework. The assessment identified land uses based on market
assessment aligned to the Master Plan, these are defined as high feasibility, conditional, complementary, and optional
land uses. For example, a land use is considered highly feasible when it demonstrates strong market fit, is appropriate
for the Site, and can be delivered within a five-year timeframe based on the Site’s readiness.

of the market, informed by:

Research
◼
Benchmarking analysis
◼
Market soundings and insights from comparable projects
