Appendix D: Policy and legislation scan
The policy framework collectively emphasises a coordinated shift from traditional industrial land uses toward
diversified, liveable and sustainable urban environments supported by infrastructure, housing diversity, and economic
transition. Across state and local strategies, there is strong alignment in directing growth to strategic centres,
enhancing connectivity, and promoting new industries such as renewable energy, services, and innovation, alongside
high-quality public spaces and inclusive community outcomes. These priorities are highly relevant to post-mining land
use, as they support the redevelopment of former mining sites into mixed-use, employment-generating, and liveable
precincts that respond to population growth, workforce transition, environmental rehabilitation, and evolving regional
economic needs.

Income related vulnerability
Compared to NSW, the SSA and ESA have less lower income earners and more middle-income earners (See Figure
9-3). However, income vulnerability is observed indirectly through housing stress and employment access, rather than
through income figures themselves. This vulnerability is heightened by the ESA’s exposure to carbon‑intensive
industries and planned coal‑fired power station closures, which may concentrate transition impacts in particular
communities and supply chains.195
Figure 9-3: Total income weekly across the SSA, ESA and NSW196
Housing related vulnerability
Housing characteristics further shape sensitivity. Home ownership outright is common in the SSA (18%) and ESA (17%)
however is below the NSW state average (32%). Less people rent in the SSA (12%) and ESA (14%) compared to NSW
(33%).
However, large parts of Lake Macquarie are classified as unaffordable, severely unaffordable or extremely
unaffordable for lower-income households, including single pensioners and single-parent families.197 Housing
affordability throughout the SSA is considered a major and unresolved challenge with community highlighting issues
with stress, rising costs, and structural barriers within the housing system.198
Cultural and linguistic diversity related vulnerability
Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people comprise around 6% of the SSA population (7,359 residents) and 7% in the
ESA (32,733 residents), which is higher than the NSW state residents who make up 3% (278,043 residents) of the
population.
The SSA has a largely homogenous language groups with only 4% of the population (4,706 residents) speaking a
language other than English at home in the SSA and 4% in the ESA (20,553 residents). There is high English proficiency
in the SSA, with 0.4% (466 residents) speaking English not well or not at all and 0.5% in the ESA (2,380 residents). This
195 Net Zero Economy Authority (2025) Regional Profiles: Hunter.
196 Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS) (2021) Total personal income (weekly) (INCP)
197 SGS Economics & Planning (2026) Lake Macquarie Housing Affordability and Liveability Study: Interim Housing Demand and Affordability Report.
198 Lake Macquarie City Council (2025) Lake Macquarie Housing Forum Detailed Outcomes Report.

--- Page 110 [Matched: ['employment']] ---
Project number 531148-000  File Document5  9 July 2026  Revision C
13
is lower than the NSW state population with 20% of the population (1,573,216 residents) speaking a language other
than English at home and 4% (302,812 residents) speaking English not well or not at all.
Socio-economic profile
The Socio-Economic Indexes for Areas (SEIFA) is a measure used to rank geographic areas in Australia according to
levels of socio-economic advantage and disadvantage199. SEIFA draws on data relating to income, education,
employment, occupation, housing and family structure to describe area-level socio-economic conditions. Figure 9-4
reflects the total SEIFA indicators throughout the SSA in which the higher the vulnerability score the more advantaged
the area.
Figure 9-4: SEIFA IRSD index within SSA200
Within the SSA, there are highly advantaged rural and coastal suburbs as well as areas experiencing significant
disadvantage. Jilliby – Yarramalong is the most advantaged suburb (top 76% of NSW suburbs). In contrast, Wyong is
the most disadvantaged suburb (bottom 10% of NSW suburbs). Wide spread of scores indicates pronounced
socio-economic inequality within the SSA, with communities experiencing different levels of access to resources,
employment opportunities and social advantage. Within the ESA, there are fewer extreme values at either end of the
SEIFA distribution. The most advantaged ESA suburbs range from Maryland–Fletcher–Minmi scoring (top 71% of NSW
suburbs). The most disadvantaged is Cessnock LGA (bottom 20% of NSW suburbs).
These findings suggest that service planning and resource allocation should be tailored at a local level, particularly
within the SSA, to address pronounced inequities that may not be fully visible in broader ESA averages.201
The most advantaged and disadvantaged suburbs in the SSA and ESA are listed in Table 9-13.
199 Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS) (2023)  Socio‑Economic Indexes for Areas (SEIFA).

Executive summary
This Flooding and Water Cycle Management Report (This Report) presents a strategic flooding and water
cycle management (WCM) assessment for the Macquarie Coal Complex Precinct (the Precinct) and Teralba
Southgate to support a state-led rezoning and Master Plan. The assessment confirms that the Precinct can
be developed with a coordinated flooding and water cycle management strategy that supports future land
use while protecting people, property, infrastructure and the surrounding environment.
The assessment has been undertaken at a master planning scale using available flood studies, water cycle
data, site investigations and topographic analysis. It focuses on identifying key constraints, opportunities and
system behaviours rather than detailed design. On this basis, it demonstrates that flooding, stormwater,
water quality and emergency management outcomes are practically achievable across the Precinct through
an integrated, precinct-scale approach.
Flooding Strategy and Precinct Response
Flooding is a defining characteristic of the Precinct, driven predominantly by regional flooding from Cockle
Creek, supported by local overland flow processes and downstream tidal influences. These mechanisms
establish the spatial framework for development by identifying where land should be protected, where
development can occur, and where mitigation is required.
The Master Plan adopts a fundamental strategy of avoidance first, followed by selective, managed
encroachment in lower impact areas where development can be supported without increasing risk. This
approach preserves key floodplain functions while maximising viable development land.
The recommended flooding strategy is based on several core principles:
 Preservation of primary floodways and conveyance corridors
 Retention of flood storage areas and natural drainage pathways
 Direction of development toward areas of lower flood risk
 Integration of flood mitigation measures to support safe development
This approach ensures that development responds to the floodplain rather than working against it,
maintaining the hydraulic integrity of the system while enabling growth.
Protection of Surrounding Environment and Property
A key outcome of the assessment is that the proposed strategy protects both the environmental values of the
Precinct and the surrounding catchment, while avoiding adverse impacts on adjacent landholdings.
The Precinct drains to sensitive receiving environments including Cockle Creek, Burkes Creek, Diega Creek
and Lake Macquarie, which contain important ecological features such as riparian corridors, wetlands and
key fish habitat.
The master plan integrates these systems into the development framework by:
 Maintaining riparian corridors as functional flood conveyance and ecological buffers

--- Page 4 [Matched: ['water quality']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Final Flooding & WCM Engineering Report_CLEAN.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
Deliverability of Mitigation Within the Precinct
A significant finding of the assessment is that the majority of required mitigation measures can be
accommodated within the Precinct footprint. This includes:
 Floodway corridors and overland flow paths
 Regional and local detention systems
 Stormwater conveyance networks
 Water quality treatment infrastructure
 Landform adjustments to manage flow and storage
These measures can be embedded into the structure of future development, allowing flood risk and water
cycle objectives to be addressed without reliance on extensive off-site works or unacceptable external
impacts.
This outcome demonstrates that the Precinct is not constrained in a way that precludes development, but
instead requires a coordinated and integrated design response at the master planning level.
Master Plan Intent and Flexibility
The master plan establishes a clear, achievable and flexible framework for future development. It defines the
spatial logic, performance objectives and key principles required to guide design and approvals, rather than
prescribing a single fixed engineering solution.
Importantly:
 The strategy is intended to be implemented progressively
 Future proponents can refine and adapt solutions
 Any modifications must remain consistent with the overall objectives
 All outcomes must comply with relevant legislation, policy and performance criteria
This ensures the plan remains practical and adaptable to future land use changes, evolving design
approaches and updated technical standards, while maintaining a consistent approach to flood risk and
water management.
Demonstrated Feasibility at Strategic Level
Based on the site review and strategic assessment, all key aspects of flooding and water cycle management
are considered feasible across the Precinct.
This includes:
 Flood risk management and land use compatibility
 Stormwater collection, conveyance and discharge
 Water quality management and protection of receiving environments
 Emergency access and evacuation planning
While further modelling and design will be required at later stages, the assessment confirms that there is a

--- Page 5 [Matched: ['water quality']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Final Flooding & WCM Engineering Report_CLEAN.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
Potential strategies include:
 Stormwater harvesting for non-potable reuse at lot or precinct scale
 Rainwater capture systems to reduce runoff volumes
 Passive irrigation of landscapes and green infrastructure such as through the use of street trees
 Distributed bioretention, detention and infiltration, where ground conditions allow
 Integration of water sensitive urban design (WSUD) across the development
These strategies can:
 Reduce demand on downstream drainage infrastructure
 Improve water quality outcomes
 Enhance resilience to climate variability
 Support more efficient and sustainable long-term servicing
Collectively, they represent a pathway toward a lower impact, more adaptive water management system for
the Precinct.
Conclusion
Overall, the flooding and water cycle management assessment supports a master plan that is practical,
flexible and environmentally responsible. It demonstrates that development within the Precinct can proceed,
provided that key floodplain functions are maintained and an integrated strategy is implemented.
The proposed approach protects surrounding communities and environmental systems, accommodates
required mitigation within the site, and establishes a robust framework to guide future planning, design and
delivery.

1
Introduction
This report confirms that the Precinct can be planned with a practical and achievable flooding and water cycle
management strategy that supports future development while protecting people, property, infrastructure and the
surrounding environment. The assessment has been undertaken at a strategic master planning level and is based
on a review of the site, available flood studies, water cycle management information, topography, waterways,
receiving environments and existing infrastructure. On that basis, the overall conclusion is that the key flooding,
stormwater, water quality and evacuation outcomes for the Precinct are capable of being achieved through a
coordinated precinct-scale approach, subject to detailed design in later phases.
Flooding is a defining consideration for the Precinct, particularly in relation to Cockle Creek, its tributaries, local
overland flow paths and downstream tailwater influences. However, the report demonstrates that these constraints
do not preclude future land use planning. Rather, they establish a clear framework for where development should
occur, where flood conveyance and storage should be maintained, and where supporting mitigation is required.
The proposed flooding strategy is built around preserving key floodways, accommodating regional flow paths,
providing detention and storage where necessary, and locating future development in areas that can achieve an
acceptable level of flood risk management. This creates a structure for growth that responds to the floodplain rather
than working against it.
A key message of the assessment is that the proposed strategy protects the environmental values of the site and
the surrounding catchment, while also avoiding the transfer of flood impacts to neighbouring landholdings. The
Precinct contains and drains to sensitive receiving environments, including Cockle Creek, Burkes Creek, Diega
Creek and Lake Macquarie. It also contains riparian corridors, wetlands, key fish habitat and other ecological
values that require protection. The recommended approach maintains these systems as important components of
the masterplan, allowing them to continue functioning as flood conveyance corridors, ecological buffers and water
quality protection features. In this way, the strategy is not only about making development possible, but about
ensuring that the surrounding environment and adjoining properties remain protected from increased flood risk,
water quality decline or inappropriate drainage outcomes.
Importantly, the report finds that many of the mitigation measures needed to support future development can be
accommodated within the Precinct footprint. This includes the retention of floodway corridors, provision of regional
or local detention, integration of stormwater conveyance pathways, on-site detention, water quality treatment
systems and landform responses that work with the site’s natural drainage patterns. While detailed sizing and
alignment of infrastructure will need to be resolved by future proponents, the strategic assessment demonstrates
that sufficient opportunities exist within the Precinct to embed these measures into future development layouts.
This is a significant outcome because it shows that the masterplan can establish a viable pathway forward without
relying on unreasonable off-site intervention or unacceptable impacts to external land.

--- Page 12 [Matched: ['water quality']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           2
may include stormwater harvesting for non-potable reuse, rainwater capture at lot or precinct scale, reuse of
treated water for landscape irrigation or industrial purposes, passive irrigation of open space, distributed retention
and infiltration where ground conditions allow, and broader water sensitive urban design initiatives that reduce
runoff volumes while improving water quality outcomes. Such measures could lessen the burden on downstream
conveyance and treatment systems, improve resilience and create a more efficient long-term water servicing model
for the Precinct. Overall, the report supports a masterplan that is practical, flexible and environmentally responsible,
and which provides a sound basis for future proponents to progress detailed design while meeting flood, water
cycle and planning objectives.
1.1
Site location and context
Located on Awabakal Country within the Lake Macquarie Local Government Area, the Precinct comprises of
approximately 1160 hectares of land across the suburbs of Killingworth, Wakefield, Barnsley.and Teralba
Southgate. The Precinct forms part of the broader Lower Hunter region and is strategically positioned within an
established industrial and infrastructure corridor, with proximity to key regional centres. Figure 1-1 illustrates the
regional context of the Precinct. Macquarie Coal Complex is a former mining operation comprising West Wallsend
Colliery (underground mine), Westside Mine (open cut), the Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant (MCPP), Tailings
Storage Facility (TSF) and associated infrastructure. Coal mining has been a defining land use in the area since
operations commenced in 1969, shaping both the physical landscape and the economic profile of surrounding
communities. The site is currently closed and undergoing rehabilitation, presenting a large, consolidated
landholding with significant potential for transition to new employment-generating uses.
At a regional scale, the Precinct is well positioned to leverage existing transport and infrastructure networks that
have historically supported mining operations, including arterial road connections linking to the Hunter Expressway,
M1 Pacific Motorway and broader freight networks connecting to the Port of Newcastle. This strategic connectivity
supports the potential for the site to accommodate a range of employment uses, including advanced
manufacturing, logistics and emerging industries, consistent with regional economic transition objectives.
The broader land use context includes a mix of urban residential communities, industrial lands and environmental
corridors. Similar to other legacy industrial sites transitioning within NSW, the Macquarie Coal Complex site
represents a significant interface between former heavy industry and surrounding communities, requiring careful
planning to manage land use compatibility, environmental constraints and future development outcomes.
The scale, single ownership (Glencore) and relative separation from sensitive urban uses provide a unique
opportunity to deliver a coordinated, master-planned transition of the site. Large tracts of underutilised and
rehabilitated land offer flexibility to accommodate new industry typologies, while enabling the retention and
enhancement of ecological areas and landscape buffers.
Transition of the Macquarie Coal Complex aligns with the strategic directions of the Hunter Regional Plan 2041,

Existing Flood
Studies
Limitation
The Lake Macquarie Waterway Flood Risk Management Study and Plan (2012) is based
on modelling that adopted ARR 1987 rainfall and methodologies. The flood levels and
advice have not been updated to reflect the latest ARR 2019 Version 4.2, however is
understood to still be aligned with Council’s current flood planning advice.
Strategic Level
Assessment
Limitation
The assessment has been prepared to support Master Plan development and is not
intended to replace detailed flood impact assessments, drainage design or development
application investigations.
Existing Flood
Models
Dependency
Flood extents, hazard mapping and planning outcomes are dependent on the continued
suitability of the adopted Council flood models and their underlying assumptions.
Climate Change
Allowances
Limitation
Existing flood studies predate the latest ARR2019 Version 4.2 guidance relating to PMF
and 1% AEP climate change impacts. Future modelling updates may alter the extent of
flood constraints and developable land.

State Environmental Planning
Policy (Resilience and Hazards)
2021
The State Environmental Planning Policy (Resilience and Hazards) 2021 provides the
primary statutory framework for the consideration of flood risk in land use planning across
New South Wales. The policy requires that development proposals on flood-prone land
are compatible with the flood hazard, do not adversely affect flood behaviour, and
incorporate appropriate risk management measures. It also ensures that development
does not result in increased flood impacts on surrounding properties and that flood
storage and conveyance functions are maintained. This policy underpins the assessment
of rezoning and subdivision proposals within the Precinct.
Fisheries Management Act 1994
(FM Act)
The FM Act aims “to conserve, develop and share the fishery resources of the State for
the benefit of present and future generations”. To meet the aims, Part 7 of the FM Act
outlines legislative provisions to protect fish habitat and Part 7A outlines provisions to
conserve threatened species of fish and marine vegetation and their habitat.
Key Fish Habitat (KFH) are defined under the FM Act (NSW). The presence of KFH is an
indication of ecological value of the watercourse. There is mapped KFH (shown on the
Fisheries NSW Spatial Data Portal) associated with the following waterways:
 Cockle Creek
 Burkes Creek
 Diega Creek

National Water Quality
Management Strategy (WQA,
2018)
The National Water Quality Management Strategy is a nationally agreed upon strategy
and framework to enable effective water quality management for the delivery of fit-for-
purpose water that supports community values.
This strategy contains guidelines and policies relevant to the project including:
 The NSW Water Quality and River Flow Objectives (DECCW, 2006)
 The Australian and New Zealand Guidelines for Fresh and Marine Water Quality
(ANZG, 2018).
 The project considers water quality management strategies such that the
environmental values of the sensitive receiving watercourses are not adversely
impacted.
NSW Aquifer Interference Policy
The purpose of this policy is to clarify the water licensing and assessment processes for
aquifer interference activities under the Water Management Act 2000 and other relevant
legislative frameworks. More specifically the policy:
 clarifies the requirements for obtaining water licenses for aquifer interference activities
under NSW water legislation; and
 establishes and objectively defines considerations in assessing and providing advice
on whether more than minimal impacts might occur to a key water-dependent asset
as a result of aquifer interference.
The project area contains GDEs which are discussed further in Section 3.2.8.

NSW Groundwater Dependent
Ecosystems Policy (DLWC,
2002)
The NSW state groundwater dependent ecosystems policy is a framework to manage the
state's groundwater resources to sustain environmental, social and economic uses for the
people of NSW / prepared by the Department of Land & Water Conservation. This policy
identifies and describes what groundwater and inflow dependent ecosystems are.
The project area contains GDEs which are discussed further in Section 3.2.8.
NSW State Groundwater Quality
Protection Policy (DLWC, 1998)
The NSW state groundwater quality protection policy is a framework to protect
groundwater resources against pollution and to slow/halt or reverse any degradation of
groundwater resources, ensure sustainability of groundwater dependent ecosystems,
maintain the full range of beneficial uses to these resources and maximise economic
benefit to the region, state and nation. As such, this framework focuses on three pillars
which include: groundwater quality protection, groundwater quality management and
groundwater dependent ecosystems.
The project area contains GDEs which are discussed further in Section 3.2.8.
NSW Water Quality and River
Flow Objectives
The NSW Water Quality and River Flow Objectives are the agreed environmental values
and long-term goals for NSW's surface waters (NSW Government, 2006).
The NSW Water Quality Objectives (WQOs) set out:
 The community's values and uses for our rivers, creeks, estuaries and lakes (i.e.
healthy aquatic life, water suitable for recreational activities like swimming and
boating, and drinking water) (NSW Government, 2006); and
 A range of water quality indicators to help us assess whether the current condition of
our waterways supports those values and uses (NSW Government, 2006).
The NSW WQOs provide environmental values for NSW waters and the ANZECC 2000 /
ANZG (2018) Guidelines provide the technical guidance to assess the water quality
needed to protect those values (NSW Government, 2006). The values include protection
of aquatic ecosystems, visual amenity, secondary and primary recreation, livestock

Risk-Based Water Quality Criteria & NSW Estuary Health Risk Dataset
Given the high ecological value of the downstream Lake Macquarie Estuary, the NSW Governments policy on
establishing stormwater quality targets for sensitive receptors; Risk-based Framework for Considering Waterway
Health Outcomes in Strategic Land-use Planning Decisions was considered for nutrient loading limit targets for
Precinct development. The former Office of Environment and Heritage (OEH) applied the first two steps of the Risk-
based Framework and produced the NSW Estuary Health Risk dataset. The aim of the dataset is to spatially
prioritise where further studies and/or management actions in a catchment would help achieve outcomes for
coastal environment areas, coastal wetlands and littoral rainforests areas specified in the Coastal Management Act
2016. The dataset identifies areas in a catchment that pose relatively higher risks to the health of estuaries and
areas where land use intensification is best avoided and/or more stringent management controls are needed.
The risk of impact on the Lake Macquarie Estuary has been spatially represented in Figure 3-1. The nutrient
loading limits for the Lake Macquarie Estuary catchment applied at different zones intersecting with the project
boundary have been summarised in Table 3-3.

urban areas targets are based on best practice load-based reduction targets when compared to an unmitigated
developed scenario. For greenfield sites (rural and forested sites), current site performance must be modelled, and
these will be used as the target for any further development i.e. NorBE. The targets set by Council have been

The LMCC DCP states that on site detention (OSD) may be required for residential developments larger than a
dual occupancy and for all commercial and industrial developments. OSD is the temporary capture and storage of
stormwater to ensure that peak runoff from a development site does not exceed existing conditions. It is designed
to prevent localised flooding and any adverse impacts to downstream development/properties. There are a number
of OSD systems that Council consider including:

Planning Requirements
Controlled activities carried out in, on, or under waterfront land are regulated by the Water Management Act 2000
(WM Act). The Natural Resources Access Regulator (NRAR) administers the controlled activity provisions of the
WM Act which are outlined in the Guidelines for Riparian Corridors (RC) on Waterfront Land. Waterfront land
includes the bed and bank of any river, lake or estuary and all land within 40 metres of the highest bank of the river,
lake or estuary.
The Guidelines recommend a vegetated riparian zone (VRZ) width based on watercourse order as classified under
the ‘Strahler’ system, see Section 3.1.3 Local Watercourse Orders. The width of the vegetated riparian zone should
be measured from the top of the highest bank on both sides of the watercourse, refer Figure 3-5.
A summary of the vegetated riparian zone and total riparian corridor width requirements are presented in Table 3-6.
These riparian corridor widths are also stipulated in the Lake Macquarie City Council DCP.
The Guidelines also outline certain works and activities that can occur on waterfront land and in riparian corridors
through the riparian corridor matrix, see Table 3-7.

3.2.3
Watercourses
The major watercourses within the Precinct are Cockle Creek and two of its tributaries, Burkes Creek and Diega
Creek. There are an additional 4 unnamed watercourses which flow through the Precinct.
Cockle Creek is a 3rd and 4th order waterway within the Precinct boundary and turns into a 5th order waterway just
south of the Weir Road. It is a non-perennial waterway and is one of the major tributaries of Lake Macquarie. It is
tidal just upstream of the Weir Road, discussed further in Section 3.2.4. Burkes Creek is located to the north of the
Precinct and is a 4th order, non-perennial waterway. Diega Creek is located in the south of the Precinct and is a 4th
order, non-perennial waterway before its confluence with Cockle Creek, to the north of the Final Void Pit.
The Precinct discharges to Cockle Creek to the north and east of the boundary. The lower parts of Cockle Creek
can be flooded by elevated ocean levels caused by high tides and storm surge, as well as intense rainfall over the
catchment (NSW Government SES, 2020).
Cockle Creek ultimately discharges to Lake Macquarie. Lake Macquarie is an estuary which has a permanently
open, trained entrance. It supports diverse habitats including seagrass meadows, mangroves, saltmarsh, rocky
reefs, oyster reefs, sand flats, mud flats and deep open waters and provides recreational value for water-based
activities (OEH, 2026).

To understand the health of receiving waterways for the project, data was assessed from sparse water quality
monitoring available from Lake Macquarie Council as part of their estuary health management and Glencore
annual reports as part of their environmental protection licences (EPLs) for their licensed discharge locations.
Water quality monitoring data was collected by Council at Cockle Creek and Lake Macquarie at Speers Point. This
data was collected from 2019 to 2025 and has been compared with the WQOs as described in Section 3.1.1 NSW

3.2.4
Tidal Limits
Cockle Creek is subject to tidal influence from Lake Macquarie upstream to Barnsley Weir (The Weir Bridge),
located approximately 50 m upstream of The Weir Road crossing. The weir forms a significant hydraulic control
within the Cockle Creek system and represents the upstream limit of tidal influence under non-flooding conditions.
Based on Council survey, the weir has a crest level of about 0.44m AHD. The mapped tidal limit (Latitude: -
32.93690, Longitude: 151.59911) is shown on Figure 3-10.
Figure 3-10 Tidal Limits

Although the Precinct is located upstream of the tidal limit, drainage infrastructure servicing the eastern portions of
the Precinct may ultimately discharge to receiving systems that are hydraulically influenced by the Barnsley Weir
and downstream tidal conditions within Cockle Creek and Lake Macquarie. Elevated downstream water levels
associated with tidal cycles or coincident flood events have the potential to reduce gravity drainage capacity,
increase upstream tailwater levels and extend local drainage ponding durations.
These downstream hydraulic controls should be considered during future design of stormwater infrastructure,
particularly where drainage outlets are located at relatively low elevations downstream of the weir. The potential for
backwater effects and tidal ingress should also be considered in the design of stormwater quality treatment
infrastructure located near receiving waterways. Low-level wetlands, bioretention systems and other water quality
treatment measures may be susceptible to prolonged inundation or reverse flows where outlet structures are
influenced by elevated tailwater conditions.
Accordingly, future drainage strategies should consider the need for local drainage controls, such as flap gates or
tide gates where appropriate, together with the selection of drainage infrastructure and water quality treatment
components that are capable of operating effectively under periodic tailwater or tidal influence.
3.2.5
Soils
An assessment of the Hydrological Soil Group dataset (DCCEEW, 2026) indicates that the Precinct is generally
underlain by low permeability soils consisting of Soil Group C. This soil group is described as:
 C - soils having slow infiltration rates when thoroughly wetted and consisting chiefly of soils with a layer that
impedes downward movement of water, or soils with moderately fine to fine texture. These soils have a slow
rate of water transmission.
Sections surrounding the Pit Void Dam and the Tailings Storage Facility to the northeastern boundary are ‘not
assessed’ and thus the soil group is unknown. The Tailing Storage dam is to be clay capped and thus is assumed
to be largely impermeable. The Hydrological Soil Groups are shown in Figure 3-11.
An assessment of the soil landscapes of the Precinct using the eSPADE spatial service (DPIE, 2026) indicates the
site is dominated by Cockle Creek and Killingworth soil landscapes. Table 3-12 indicates the soil landscapes of the
study area and the dominant topsoil defined under these landscapes. The site area also contains unknown soil
landscapes referred to as ‘disturbed terrain’ around the mining activity locations. The exact dominant topsoil of
these areas is not known. See Figure 3-12 for spatial representation of soil landscapes.
The NSW 1:25,000 Acid Sulfate Soil Risk Mapping, accessible via eSPADE v2.2 indicates the following risk
classifications across the Precinct area:
 Around West Wallsend Colliery (WWC) / Westside Mine areas) were mapped as having no known ASS
occurrence (Class N). Site topography ranges from approximately 17–21 m AHD, which is above the typical
estuarine threshold for coastal ASS formation.

3.4
Mine Rehabilitation and Post-Mining Water Context
The Precinct is located within a post mining landscape where there is existing infrastructure including water
storage, water treatment and water diversions on site. The landscape within the Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant
area has been rehabilitated to some degree which has included capping of existing water management
infrastructure and rerouting of flows to other locations. There are a number of retained ponds through this site and
the West Wallsend Colliery site which continue to be used under existing conditions to provide treatment or
settlement prior to discharge at the licensed locations. Most of the water treatment infrastructure is located within
the 100year ARI flood extent and tidal inundation zones from Cockle Creek. The water quality and sediment quality
of these basins is largely unknown but is anticipated to be impacted from the historical mining usage and is
therefore expected to have some contamination associated. The tailings dam also remains uncapped at this stage
and retains runoff. There is a dam failure area associated with the tailings dam. Additionally, the pit lake void
remains a large water feature of the site, and includes an overflow to Cockle Creek. Comprehensive water quality
analysis of Pit Lake Void has been undertaken over the last two years, although this data was not available during
the time of reporting and hence has not been reviewed for this report. The water quality of the Tailings Dam is
unknown at this stage. Further detail about the potential presence of contamination is contained in the
Contamination Technical Assessment (CTA).
The EPL License 1360 is still current for the two discharge locations at Burkes Creek and Cockle Creek. It is
understood that there is a water access license held as part of the West Wallsend Colliery site (license
20BL169793) which is understood to be valid following mine closure.
The opportunities to reuse water management infrastructure in the future will be limited by:
 Suitability of size of infrastructure. This includes suitable size of infrastructure for conveyance, detention and
treatment.
 Location and flooding/tidal inundation. Repurposing of these areas may be limited by inundation levels and
whether this is acceptable for the proposed infrastructure (e.g. detention basins or treatment infrastructure).
 Final landform and levels of infrastructure. Levels will dictate what infrastructure has the potential to be
repurposed for future development
 Contamination and safety. Further understanding of potential contamination and safety of these assets will
further identify their reuse potential
Outside of the disturbed sites, the landform is fairly natural and is anticipated to flow through natural flow paths
towards Cockle Creek and its tributaries. Through the undisturbed landscape, new water management
infrastructure including conveyance pipes/channels, culverts and treatment infrastructure will be required.
Development across the site will also need to consider protected areas, sensitive environments and riparian
corridors with appropriate buffers and distances provided between these locations and water management

--- Page 48 [Matched: ['1% aep', 'pmf', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           38
latest guidance from Australian Rainfall and Runoff, Version 4.2. The key difference with the latest edition is the
update to the climate change factors, which may have an impact on the flood risk areas. This would particularly be
the case with the PMF flooding, which historically had not included climate change, but is now recommended.
Consistent with the strategic nature of the Master Plan, the assessment focuses on identifying flood risk and
floodplain constraints that influence land use allocation, developable land, infrastructure planning and future
staging, rather than undertaking detailed flood modelling or design. Where appropriate, recommendations are
provided identifying matters requiring further investigation during subsequent planning, subdivision and
development assessment stages.
4.1
Flood Mechanisms and Flood Behaviour
Flooding within the Precinct is influenced by a combination of catchment and downstream hydraulic processes. The
Precinct experiences flooding from three primary mechanisms, which can be broadly defined as:
 Regional Flooding – flooding associated with Cockle Creek and its major tributaries during significant
catchment flood events;
 Local Flooding - flooding resulting from rainfall runoff generated within the local catchments and conveyed
through the local overland flow and drainage network; and
 Tidal Inundation - elevated water levels associated with tidal fluctuations within Lake Macquarie and their
influence on downstream reaches of Cockle Creek.
These mechanisms operate at different spatial scales and influence flood behaviour across the Precinct to varying
degrees. Regional flooding from Cockle Creek represents the dominant flood mechanism affecting the Precinct and
has therefore informed the adopted flood planning levels and strategic flood risk assessment. Local flooding may
occur independently of regional flooding where drainage capacity is exceeded during intense rainfall events, while
tidal inundation primarily influences downstream water levels and tailwater conditions within the lower reaches of
Cockle Creek.
An understanding of each flooding mechanism is important for informing future land use planning, drainage design
and flood risk management within the Precinct. The characteristics and implications of each mechanism are
discussed in the following sections.
4.1.1
Regional Flooding
As noted, flooding associated with Cockle Creek is considered regional flooding and represents the dominant flood
risk affecting the site as it crosses the site from a southwest to northeast direction, draining to Lake Macquarie. The
regional flood risk varies in impact on the floodplain to varying degrees and can be generally classed into the
following categories (note that these are not related to Bureau of Meteorology flood warning categories):
 Minor Flooding: Cockle Creek water levels may remain largely within channel; however, elevated lake water

--- Page 49 [Matched: ['1% aep', 'pmf', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           39
Figure 4-1 PMF and 1% AEP Flood extents map
4.1.2
Local Flooding
Local flooding within the Precinct is associated with intense rainfall falling directly on the local catchments and
resulting in overland flow, minor watercourse flooding and temporary ponding in low-lying areas. Unlike regional
flooding from Cockle Creek and its main tributaries, local flooding is generally driven by the capacity of the local
drainage system and the topographic characteristics of the surrounding land rather than large-scale catchment
inflows.
The Precinct contains a number of natural drainage depressions and ephemeral flow paths that convey runoff
towards Cockle Creek and its tributaries. During high intensity rainfall events, these drainage pathways may
exceed their conveyance capacity, resulting in shallow overland flow and temporary inundation of adjacent low-
lying land. The final developed Precinct landform and local topographic variations may further influence the
direction and extent of local runoff and drainage behaviour.
At the Master Plan level, detailed local drainage behaviour has not been assessed and will be dependent on future
landform modification, infrastructure provision and development layout. Consequently, the extent and severity of
local flooding should be confirmed through detailed drainage modelling and design in accordance with Council
requirements at subsequent planning and development stages.
Future development should maintain existing overland flow paths where practicable, avoid creating adverse
drainage impacts on adjoining land and provide local stormwater infrastructure designed to safely convey runoff
while accounting for downstream tailwater conditions and the potential interaction with regional flood events. The
detailed design of future drainage infrastructure should demonstrate that appropriate levels of service are achieved
and that local flooding risks are managed without increasing flood impacts elsewhere within or downstream of the
Precinct.

--- Page 50 [Matched: ['1% aep', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           40
Figure 4-2 Local overland flow paths
4.1.3
Tidal Inundation
Tidal inundation within the Precinct is associated with elevated water levels in Lake Macquarie and the resulting
hydraulic influence on the lower reaches of Cockle Creek. Cockle Creek discharges to Lake Macquarie, which in
turn drains to the Pacific Ocean via the narrow and shallow 4 km long Swansea Channel. Under normal conditions,
the lake level is approximately 0.1 m AHD and tidal fluctuations are relatively small, typically in the order of ±0.05 m
due to the hydraulic restriction imposed by Swansea Channel. However, elevated ocean levels associated with
high tides, storm surge and wave setup, together with inflows from the surrounding catchments during major rainfall
events, can significantly increase lake water levels and the tailwater conditions experienced within lower Cockle
Creek. Barnsley Weir is known as the tidal limit of Cockle Creek however there is limited information to confirm
details of the hydraulic control. Based on the flood mapping of ocean dominated events, the weir appears to be
overtopped indicating that it’s influence is limited to frequent (eg. daily tide) events.
The Lake Macquarie Waterway Flood Risk Management Study and Plan (2012) recognised that peak lake water
levels are governed by the coincidence of elevated ocean conditions and catchment runoff. During historical
events, including the February 1990 and June 2007 floods, peak lake levels reached approximately 1.0 m AHD,
contributing to elevated water levels within Cockle Creek and its lower tributaries.
For flood planning purposes, the adopted design coastal inundation level for the 1% AEP event under the 2100 sea
level rise scenario (+0.9 m) is approximately 2.32 m AHD. This level represents a joint probability event, combining
an elevated ocean condition with a coincident catchment rainfall event, consistent with the methodology adopted in
the Lake Macquarie Waterway Flood Risk Management Study (2012). Consequently, the adopted design level
reflects the combined influence of coastal processes and catchment runoff rather than a coastal storm occurring in
isolation.
Figure 4-3 illustrates both the projected permanent inundation extent under the 2100 sea level rise scenario and
the 1% AEP coastal inundation extent based on a peak lake level of 2.32 m AHD. These figures demonstrate that

--- Page 51 [Matched: ['1% aep', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           41
the eastern portions of the Precinct may be influenced by future coastal inundation, particularly in low-lying areas
adjacent to Cockle Creek and associated drainage corridors.
Areas subject to permanent inundation represent a significant development constraint. Development within these
areas would require substantial engineering intervention, such as filling to achieve suitable finished surface levels
or the construction of elevated structures supported above projected future water levels. Such measures are likely
to increase development costs, require careful consideration of flood storage and hydraulic impacts, and may
introduce additional environmental and planning constraints. Consequently, avoidance of permanently inundated
land is considered the preferred planning outcome where practicable.
Similarly, development within areas affected by the adopted 1% AEP (2100) coastal inundation level should
demonstrate that future land uses can safely accommodate the combined effects of elevated lake levels and
coincident catchment flooding. Future planning and detailed design should consider the implications of elevated
tailwater conditions on flood behaviour, stormwater drainage performance and finished surface levels to ensure
that development remains resilient to the projected effects of climate change and coastal inundation.
Figure 4-3 Coastal hazards
4.2
Hydraulic Categorisation (Flood Function)
In addition to flood extent and hazard, the hydraulic function of the floodplain is an important consideration for
future land use planning and development. The Upper Cockle Creek Flood Study (WMAwater, 2019) categorises
the floodplain into Floodway, Flood Storage and Flood Fringe areas based on the hydraulic behaviour of
floodwaters.
 Floodways- are areas that convey a significant proportion of flood flows and play a critical role in transporting
floodwaters through the floodplain. Development within floodways has the greatest potential to increase
upstream flood levels and should generally be avoided where practicable.

--- Page 52 [Matched: ['pmf']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           42
 Flood Storage- areas provide temporary storage of floodwaters during flood events and assist in attenuating
flood levels. The loss of flood storage through filling or development may increase flood levels unless
appropriate compensatory measures are implemented.
 Flood Fringe- areas comprise the remaining flood-prone land outside of floodways and flood storage areas and
generally have a lower hydraulic significance. Subject to detailed assessment and appropriate mitigation, these
areas may provide greater opportunities for future development than floodways or flood storage areas.
Hydraulic categorisation provides an additional layer of information to support the flood constraint framework and
refinement of the Structure Plan. As a general planning principle, future development should avoid floodways
where practicable, minimise encroachment into flood storage areas and direct development towards lower
constraint areas of the floodplain.
Recognising the strategic nature of the Precinct, there may be opportunities for targeted encroachment into
floodway or flood storage areas where supported by detailed hydrologic and hydraulic modelling. In these
circumstances, the proposed development and associated mitigation measures should demonstrate that changes
to flood behaviour, including flood levels, flow velocities, flood hazard and flood conveyance, remain acceptable
and do not result in unacceptable impacts on surrounding land or infrastructure. This approach provides flexibility
for future precinct planning while maintaining the primary objective of preserving floodplain function and managing
flood risk to an acceptable level.
4.3
Flood Hazard, Life Safety and Land Use Compatibility
4.3.1
Flood Hazard and Life Safety
Flood hazard provides an indication of the potential risk to people, vehicles and property during a flood event and is
influenced by the depth, velocity and rate of rise of floodwaters. Areas subject to deeper and faster flowing
floodwaters present a greater life safety risk and may significantly restrict emergency access, evacuation and future
development opportunities.
Figure 4-4 presents the PMF flood hazard covering the Precinct. The hazard is categorised as either Low or High
hazard and has been simplified from the hazard classifications in accordance with Australian Rainfall and runoff
2019 (Version 4.2). The high hazard recognises that higher hazard areas represent an increasing risk to people
and vehicles and may become unsafe during flood events. In addition to mapped hazard classifications, floodways
and areas characterised by deep or fast-flowing floodwaters should be recognised as key flood constraints due to
their role in conveying flood flows and the associated safety risks.
At the strategic planning level, these areas should be considered when refining the Master Plan and locating future
development, transport infrastructure and essential services. Development within areas subject to significant flood
hazard may require substantial mitigation measures and should demonstrate that flood risk to occupants and

--- Page 53 [Matched: ['probable maximum flood', 'pmf', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           43
occupants may have limited ability to safely evacuate prior to flood isolation. The introduction of these land uses
into flood-constrained areas has the potential to increase life safety risk and place additional demand on
emergency management resources.
The Master Plan should therefore seek to direct future development towards areas of comparatively lower flood risk
while recognising flood-prone land as an important constraint that influences developable land boundaries and
future staging. Land use planning should avoid introducing additional population or evacuation-sensitive
development into areas subject to significant flood hazard unless detailed assessment demonstrates that an
acceptable level of flood risk can be achieved and appropriate mitigation measures implemented.
Figure 4-4: Land use compatibility in flood prone areas
4.4
Extreme Event Risk
4.4.1
Probable Maximum Flood (PMF) Risk
The Probable Maximum Flood (PMF) represents the largest physically possible flood event and is adopted for
emergency management, life safety assessment and the planning of critical infrastructure. Although the probability
of a PMF event is extremely low, the potential consequences are significant due to the depth, extent and velocity of
flooding that may occur. Accordingly, consideration of PMF flood risk is an important component of strategic land
use planning and infrastructure planning within the Precinct.
The current understanding of PMF flood behaviour within the Precinct (refer Figure 4-5) is based on the Upper
Cockle Creek Floodplain Risk Management Study and Plan (2019) and the Winding Creek and Lower Cockle
Creek Flood Study (2017). These studies provide the best available information for strategic planning; however,
they predate the latest guidance contained within Australian Rainfall and Runoff (ARR2019 Version 4.2), which
recognises that Probable Maximum Precipitation (PMP) and consequently PMF flood events may also be
influenced by climate change. Future updates to the flood modelling incorporating current climate change guidance

--- Page 54 [Matched: ['pmf']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           44
may therefore increase the extent and severity of PMF inundation, potentially affecting areas currently identified as
having lower flood risk. Accordingly, the extent of PMF flooding should be reviewed during subsequent planning
and design stages as updated modelling becomes available.
There is limited planning guidance that defines development suitability solely based on PMF inundation. Instead,
PMF mapping should be used to inform life safety considerations, emergency management planning, infrastructure
resilience and the suitability of critical or evacuation-sensitive land uses.
From a strategic planning perspective, the following principles should be considered:
 Development that is evacuation-sensitive, critical or accommodates large numbers of people should generally
be avoided within areas subject to significant PMF flood hazard unless detailed assessment demonstrates that
an acceptable level of life safety risk can be achieved.
 Future land use planning should consider the potential for rapid onset flooding, flood isolation and restricted
emergency access during extreme flood events when determining the suitability of development within the PMF
extent.
 Critical infrastructure and essential community services located within the PMF extent should be assessed to
ensure that failure during an extreme flood event would not result in unacceptable risks to surrounding
communities or emergency response capability.
 Buildings and infrastructure that are required to remain operational during or immediately following extreme
flood events should be designed to withstand the hydraulic and debris loading associated with PMF conditions,
including flood forces, buoyancy effects and debris impact where appropriate.
 Essential utilities and services, including transport infrastructure, power, communications, water and sewer
infrastructure, should consider PMF flood impacts where service disruption could adversely affect surrounding
communities or emergency management operations.
 The resilience of key transport corridors should be considered where PMF flooding has the potential to restrict
emergency access, evacuation or post-event recovery. Future upgrades to strategic transport infrastructure
should balance the required level of service with the engineering complexity and floodplain impacts associated
with achieving higher flood immunity standards.
Given the strategic nature of the Master Plan, the PMF assessment should be regarded as a high-level planning
tool that informs land use compatibility, infrastructure resilience and emergency management considerations.
Detailed PMF assessment, incorporating the latest flood modelling, climate change guidance and proposed
development layouts, should be undertaken during subsequent planning and design stages where development or
infrastructure is proposed within or adjacent to the mapped PMF extent.
4.4.2

The Precinct will inherit a Tailing Storage Facility (TSF) that is in the process of being capped at the time of this

4.5
Emergency Management
4.5.1
Emergency Management Strategy
Flood emergency management should be an important consideration in the future planning and development of the
Precinct. As the site is largely greenfield, there is an opportunity to incorporate emergency access and evacuation
considerations into the Master Plan to improve community safety and reduce future flood risk.
Regional flooding from Cockle Creek is the dominant flood mechanism affecting the Precinct. The Upper Cockle
Creek Flood Study (2019) identified the 36-hour storm as the critical duration for the 1% AEP event and the 6-hour
storm as the critical duration for the PMF event. While these durations indicate the storm events that generate the
highest flood levels, the available information does not clearly define the available warning time before flooding
occurs. In addition, there is currently no dedicated flood warning system for the Cockle Creek catchment.
Accordingly, evacuation should be adopted as the preferred emergency management strategy for future
development. Future planning should seek to maintain access to higher ground and provide transport networks that
remain functional for as long as practicable during flood events.
At this stage, shelter-in-place is not considered necessary, as the preliminary flood emergency response
assessment indicates that evacuation is generally achievable (refer to Section 4.4.2). Furthermore, shelter-in-place
is unlikely to be appropriate within areas subject to significant PMF flood hazard due to the potential life safety risks
associated with extreme flooding.

--- Page 56 [Matched: ['probable maximum flood', 'pmf', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           46
There is currently limited guidance for emergency management of non-residential development exposed to regional
flooding. Consequently, future development proposals should demonstrate that appropriate evacuation
arrangements can be achieved and that emergency access is maintained where required. The emergency
management strategy should be reviewed and refined to account for local flooding as development layouts,
transport networks and flood modelling are updated during subsequent planning and design stages.
4.5.2
Flood Emergency Response Classification
A high-level flood emergency response classification has been undertaken to provide an initial understanding of the
emergency management characteristics of the Precinct under extreme flooding conditions. The assessment is
intended to support strategic land use planning and identify areas where future development may require more
detailed emergency management consideration. It is not intended to replace a detailed flood emergency response
assessment or Operational Flood Emergency Management Strategy, which should be prepared as future
development proposals and infrastructure layouts are refined.
The emergency response classification has been determined using the decision flowchart presented in Figure 17 of
the NSW Flood Risk Management Manual – Support for Emergency Management Planning (EM01) and applied to
the Probable Maximum Flood (PMF) extent across the Precinct. The PMF extent adopted for this assessment was
derived from the Upper Cockle Creek Flood Study (WMAwater, 2019) hydraulic model outputs and interpreted from
the available flood mapping.
The Figure 17 framework classifies flood-affected areas through a sequential assessment of:
 whether the land is inundated during the PMF event;
 whether pedestrian or vehicle evacuation is practical before evacuation routes become flooded; and
 whether isolated land remains above flood level or becomes fully submerged.

--- Page 57 [Matched: ['pmf', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           47
The resulting emergency response classification mapping for the Precinct is presented in Figure 4-6.
Figure 4-6 Flood emergency response classification map of the development area
The following assumptions have been adopted in undertaking the preliminary assessment:
 Existing unsealed roads have been assumed to remain trafficable for emergency egress, or access for
emergency services, where they provide rising access and are not directly inundated by floodwaters.
 The rising road connection to the Pacific Highway is assumed to provide an emergency access point,
notwithstanding that direct public access to the highway may not currently exist. It is assumed that emergency
services would be able to access this location if required.
 Existing roads located within the mapped PMF extent may remain above the PMF water surface for evacuation;
however, these roads have not been adopted as evacuation routes due to the potential for embankment
instability or isolation during extreme flooding. Preference has therefore been given to routes located outside the
mapped PMF extent.
 The emergency response classification should be reviewed as development layouts, road infrastructure and
flood mitigation measures are refined to ensure that appropriate evacuation opportunities and emergency
response arrangements can be achieved for future land uses.
The emergency response classification has been undertaken at a strategic planning level using the currently
available PMF mapping and topographic information and should be regarded as indicative only. The classification
has been derived from visual interpretation of the PMF mapping and is intended to inform the Master Plan by
identifying broad emergency management constraints and opportunities. As future land use planning, infrastructure
design and development staging are refined, the emergency response classifications should be reviewed and
confirmed using the underlying hydraulic model outputs from the Upper Cockle Creek Flood Study (WMAwater,
2019), or any subsequent update, together with detailed terrain data, proposed road networks, finished surface
levels and flood mitigation measures.

--- Page 58 [Matched: ['1% aep', 'pmf', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           48
4.5.3
Flood Sensitive Transport Links
The transport network servicing the Precinct has an important role in supporting future development, emergency
access and evacuation during flood events. As such, flood-sensitive transport links represent a key planning
consideration and should be considered during the refinement of the Master Plan and future infrastructure
planning.
Future development should demonstrate that suitable access to higher ground can be achieved, that the transport
network is resilient to flood isolation where practicable, and that emergency vehicle access and evacuation
arrangements are appropriate for the proposed land uses. The required flood immunity of future transport
infrastructure should be commensurate with its intended function and level of service.
The proposed crossing of Cockle Creek and the Diega Creek floodplain, together with the proposed Lower Hunter
Freight Corridor, will require detailed flood assessment to determine appropriate alignment, levels and structural
arrangements. These crossings have the potential to influence flood behaviour and will require careful
consideration to balance transport connectivity, flood risk and constructability.
Existing transport links within the Precinct are also affected by flooding to varying degrees. Wakefield Road is
mapped as being overtopped during the 1% AEP flood event; however, the flooding appears to be largely confined
to the creek crossing locations based on the Upper Cockle Creek Flood Study (2019). Consequently, relatively
localised road raising and bridge or culvert upgrades may provide a practical means of improving flood immunity
across this corridor.
The Weir Road traverses flood-prone land near the existing rail loop before crossing eastward towards lower
Cockle Creek. The Winding Creek and Lower Cockle Creek Flood Study (2017) indicates that sections of this road
are inundated during the 1% AEP flood event, reducing its reliability as an evacuation or emergency access route
during major floods. Proposed road corridors should see to improve this with connectivity south through the site.
The existing rail loop is generally above the 1% AEP flood level but lies partially within the PMF extent. While this
indicates a relatively high level of flood immunity for more frequent events, consideration should be given to the
potential impacts of extreme flooding on network resilience and emergency response planning.
Any future road or rail crossings that traverse the Cockle Creek floodplain should consider the required level of
service and corresponding flood immunity appropriate to their intended function. Higher immunity standards may
require increased structure heights, longer bridge spans or greater embankment raising, which may increase
construction complexity, cost and potential impacts on flood behaviour. Accordingly, flood immunity objectives
should be balanced against floodplain impacts and the operational requirements of the transport network during the
detailed planning and design stages.

--- Page 59 [Matched: ['cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           49
5
Drainage Feasibility and Existing Constraints
The feasibility of future development within the Precinct is influenced not only by flooding constraints but also by
the ability to effectively collect, convey and discharge stormwater runoff. At the Master Plan stage, the assessment
has been limited to a strategic review of topography, drainage pathways, receiving waterways and flood behaviour
to identify areas where drainage may influence future land use planning, infrastructure provision and development
staging. Detailed stormwater modelling and drainage design have not been undertaken and should be completed
during subsequent planning and development phases.
Drainage feasibility across the Precinct is expected to vary according to local gradients, available discharge
locations and downstream hydraulic conditions. In general, flatter terrain presents greater drainage challenges due
to reduced hydraulic gradients, requiring larger drainage infrastructure or open channels to convey runoff.
Conversely, steeper areas generally provide improved gravity drainage opportunities but may require additional
drainage structures, erosion protection and energy dissipation measures to safely manage runoff velocities. In
addition, elevated downstream water levels associated with regional flooding and tidal influences may reduce
drainage capacity and should be considered during future infrastructure planning.
5.1
Drainage Constraints and Development Implications
The Precinct contains a number of physical characteristics that have the potential to influence future drainage
feasibility and the cost and complexity of development. These include:
 extensive low-lying floodplain areas;
 high groundwater table within the lower floodplain areas;
 interaction with Cockle Creek flood levels;
 downstream tailwater influences;
 shallow drainage gradients; and
 local depressions and natural storage areas.
Collectively, these characteristics may limit conventional gravity drainage opportunities in some parts of the
Precinct and require more extensive drainage infrastructure than would otherwise be required on unconstrained
land.
Table 5-1 Drainage implications based on slope constraints
Slope (%)
Equivalent
Slope (°)
Drainage Characteristics

--- Page 60 [Matched: ['cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           50
Areas characterised by shallow gradients typically require larger drainage conduits or open drainage channels to
achieve acceptable hydraulic performance while maintaining practical pipe grades. Conversely, areas with steeper
grades generally provide more efficient drainage but may require additional pits, erosion protection measures and
energy dissipation structures to manage increased flow velocities and minimise scour at drainage outlets.
Drainage feasibility is also influenced by downstream flood levels within Cockle Creek and associated waterways.
During regional flood events or periods of elevated lake levels, tailwater conditions may reduce the capacity of
gravity drainage systems, surcharge outlets and increase local ponding durations. Future drainage infrastructure
should therefore be designed having regard to both local runoff conditions and downstream hydraulic controls.
The combination of topographic constraints, flood interactions and drainage servicing requirements is likely to
influence the developability and staging of future development areas. Higher land with favourable drainage
gradients and unconstrained discharge opportunities is expected to provide more cost-effective development
opportunities and may be more suitable for early activation. Conversely, low-lying land subject to flood interaction
or constrained drainage conditions may require substantial earthworks, drainage infrastructure augmentation or
other engineering measures before development can proceed.
Figure 5-1: Indicative Drainage Constraints
5.2
Overland Flow Paths and Outlet Points
The existing landform contains numerous natural drainage pathways that convey runoff towards Cockle Creek,
Burkes Creek and associated tributaries. These overland flow paths perform an important hydraulic function and
should be recognised during future land use planning to avoid creating adverse flooding or drainage impacts.
Future development should maintain the function of existing drainage pathways and provide suitable conveyance
corridors where modification is unavoidable. The obstruction or redirection of established overland flow paths has
the potential to increase local flooding, alter flow distribution and transfer drainage impacts to adjacent land.

--- Page 61 [Matched: ['cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           51
The location and capacity of future drainage outlets will require detailed assessment during subsequent planning
and design stages. Particular consideration should be given to outlets discharging to flood-affected waterways
where elevated downstream water levels may reduce drainage performance during major rainfall events. In these
locations, additional drainage infrastructure, outlet controls or local storage may be required to achieve acceptable
levels of service.
The drainage network should be planned to accommodate both minor and major storm events while preserving
existing flow paths and maintaining compatibility with the surrounding floodplain. This approach will assist in
minimising flood risk, reducing infrastructure costs and support staging of future development across the Precinct.
Figure 5-2: Indicative Existing Overland Flow Paths
5.3
Groundwater, ASS and Salinity Considerations
There is limited, sparse information about the hydrogeology within the Precinct, however some information
suggests shallow groundwater around Cockle Creek and tributaries in low lying areas. Groundwater recharge rates
are unknown but based on hydrological soil type are anticipated to be slow.
Groundwater considerations that are relevant to the project include:
 Shallow groundwater – groundwater levels pose a constraint due to possible inundation risk and development
feasibility particularly in low lying areas near the waterways. Groundwater levels also pose a risk to stormwater
management infrastructure including detention basins and bioretention basins which need to be designed to
account for groundwater levels.
 Groundwater aquifer sustainability, ecosystems and recharge – there is a risk that development could impact
infiltration and recharge of groundwater. Precinct planning should identify and protect critical groundwater areas
and dependent ecosystems during the planning stage.

Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           53
6
Water Cycle Management Assessment
6.1
Integrated Water Cycle Management Pathway
Water cycle management (WCM) is a key consideration for future development within the Precinct. WCM is defined
as the holistic approach that addresses competing demands placed on a regions water resources whilst optimising
social benefits and protecting environmental values of receiving waterways.
Diega Creek and Cockle Creek are the two key waterways located within the Precinct. Burkes Creek is also a
waterway which is located at the northern side of the Precinct. Existing stormwater management within the
Precinct is through natural flow paths within the undeveloped areas and via water management infrastructure
across the disturbed site which includes catchment dams, sediment dams, storage dams, spillways and drainage
systems. Cockle Creek is the main receiver of stormwater runoff from the Precinct, receiving flows from upstream
tributaries, natural flow paths and water directed from the disturbed areas via two licenced locations.
Key variables and interactions associated with groundwater within the Precinct include presence of groundwater
dependent ecosystems (GDEs), groundwater levels and flow paths, and groundwater quality
and potential contamination. The Precinct is largely underlain by soils with slow infiltration rates which
may indicate limited or slow recharge of groundwater aquifers within the Precinct.
Key sensitive receiving environments that have been considered include:
 Coastal wetlands, key fish habitat and groundwater dependent ecosystems (GDEs) within the
Precinct, concentrated largely within the riparian corridor of Diega and Cockle Creeks.
 Downstream sensitive receiving environments including Lake Macquarie and Cockle Creek estuarine
environments
Drainage of stormwater within the Precinct will aim to minimise changes to sub-catchments boundaries and direct
flows via existing drainage paths where possible. Formalisation of drainage will occur to capture and direct water to
stormwater treatment prior to discharge to the downstream receiving environment. Onsite stormwater
detention (OSD) will be required within the Precinct and the stormwater treatment strategy will consider options
for both on-lot and end-of-pipe treatment to allow for flexibility of development size and staging. Stormwater
treatment opportunities include rainwater tanks, gross pollutant traps, bioretention, wetlands and street trees.
For water supply (potable and non-potable), refer to the Utilities report.
The adoption of an integrated approach to water cycle management prioritises capture, treatment and reuse
where feasible and aims to minimise water quality and quantity impacts to sensitive receiving environments.
6.2
Water Cycle Management Opportunities
There are various water cycle management opportunities for the Precinct such as utilising existing infrastructure for

--- Page 64 [Matched: ['water quality']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           54
6.2.2
Harvesting and Reuse
There are opportunities for an integrated water cycle management such as harvesting stormwater and reuse on
site.  Stormwater can be harvested at an on-lot or precinct scale. At an on-lot scale stormwater from rainwater
tanks can be used for non-potable re-use such as flushing of toilets and landscape irrigation. At a precinct scale
stormwater can be used to supply water to local industry, irrigation or for other water uses. Urban development
typically impacts volume, frequency and quality of stormwater runoff. Harvesting can reduce the volume of water
flowing into the drainage system and so reduce stream erosion and minimise the impacts of urbanisation on
aquatic ecosystems. Further, water quality can be improved through re-use by abstracting a proportion of polluted
stormwater for re-use, trapping pollutants in on-line storages and returning treated stormwater to receiving
waterways. Finally, stormwater harvesting can also substitute in part for existing mains water uses whilst balancing
the need to provide flows for urban creeks and rivers (DEC, 2006).
6.2.3
Treatment
Water quality reduction targets can be achieved by using typical Water Sensitive Urban Design (WSUD) treatment
trains using Stormwater Quality Improvement Devices (SQIDs). Depending on the scale of the development and
planning agreements, SQIDs can potentially be provided at the lot, street, subdivision or precinct scales. Examples
of typical treatment trains from the Lake Macquarie City Council SQID Guidelines 2013 (LMCC, 2013) are listed in
Table 6-1.
The local constraints may prohibit the implementation of these typical treatment trains in some sections of the
Precinct. These constraints are discussed in Section 4.5.3.

7
Structure Plan
7.1
Proposed Structure Plan
The proposed structure plan has been developed by GHD and is the principal spatial framework for the Master
Plan. It identifies the arrangement of developable areas, environmentally constrained areas, recreational areas,
movement networks and key constraints across the Precinct. The structure plan outlines the spatial logic for future
development at a precinct scale, providing a framework within which detailed design, subdivision and development
assessment will occur. The boundaries between land use categories shown on the structure plan are indicative and
may be refined through detailed technical investigation and assessment at the development application stage.
The structure plan has five developable sub-precincts with the last sub-precinct consisting of the remaining area
made up of the environmentally constrained area.
The following categories are used to describe land within the structure plan as shown in Figure 7-1:
 Developable areas – Land assessed as most likely to be suitable for development, subject to further technical
investigation and site-specific assessment at the development application stage. Developable areas are
concentrated in locations with relative geotechnical stability, accessibility to existing or planned transport
networks and proximity to utility infrastructure or feasible servicing solutions.
 Environmentally constrained areas – Land identified as having high biodiversity values or where natural
features (watercourses, wetlands, steep slope, flooding) constrain development for employment uses. Detailed
environmental investigations are required prior to development or disturbance of vegetation in these areas.
Environmentally constrained areas form an interconnected network across the Precinct that underpins
ecological connectivity, water cycle management and landscape identity.
 Recreational areas – Land suitable for active and passive recreation facilities, balanced with protecting
biodiversity and cultural values. Recreational areas are primarily associated with the West Lake sub-precinct
and the conservation corridor network, where the landscape and environmental values support nature-based
tourism, walking and cycling trails and community recreational facilities.
 Further investigation areas – Land constrained in the short to medium term by geotechnical, dam safety, flood
or biodiversity factors requiring resolution before development can proceed. Further investigation is required, as
described in this Master Plan, prior to development. The release of this land for development is contingent upon
the completion of relevant technical investigations and the achievement of applicable staging triggers. These
areas have been mapped mainly in the Cockle Creek sub-precinct with small areas mapped in the eastern
section of the Killingworth sub-precinct.
The rail loop to the northeast has been retained, along with most of the local roads within the Precinct. The six sub-
precincts are shown in Figure 7-2 and the land use and areas summarised in Table 8-1
