8
Flooding and Water Cycle Management Strategy
8.1
Flood Management Strategy
Flooding presents a significant constraint to future development within the Precinct and should be considered as a
key input to the Structure Plan. The preferred approach is to avoid development within the most flood-sensitive
areas, including areas defines as floodways and important flood storage areas, wherever practicable.
Where avoidance is not practical, there may be opportunities for targeted development within lower constraint
areas of the floodplain, supported by appropriate flood mitigation measures. In practice, a combination of land use
planning, structural mitigation and non-structural management measures is likely to be required to balance
development outcomes with acceptable flood risk. The suitability and effectiveness of these measures should be
confirmed through detailed flood modelling and concept design during subsequent planning stages.
8.1.1
Regional Detention Basins
Regional detention basins may reduce downstream flood levels by temporarily storing runoff during major rainfall
events and releasing flows at a controlled rate following the flood peak. This approach primarily can mitigate
increases in peak flow runoff associated with future urban development (refer Section 8.2.1) or it may be used to
manage the existing catchment flows from Cockle Creek and Diega Creek upstream of the Precinct. Either way,
the approach manages the flood risk downstream to mitigate impacts of development or potentially unlock areas
previously at risk of flooding.
The effectiveness of detention basins depends on their size and location within the catchment. Due to the
dispersed nature of the proposed development areas and the varying topography, a distributed basin network may
be more practical than a single regional basin when directly mitigating increases in peak flow from development
(also refer Section 8.2.1). Where detention basins are used to offset and unlock downstream development, areas
such as the Final void pit or along (offline) from the main creek lines provide opportunities.
8.1.2
Floodway Corridors
The provision of dedicated floodway corridors may improve flood conveyance through the Precinct and reduce the
hydraulic impacts associated with future development for local and possibly regional scale flooding. Measures may
include localised channels, diversion channels or the preservation of broad overland flow corridors through the
development area.
Maintaining flood conveyance is particularly important where development may encroach into flood risk areas, as
reducing the available flow area may increase flood levels in other areas. Future development planning stages
should therefore consider retaining sufficient floodway corridors through the Precinct to preserve floodplain

--- Page 71 [Matched: ['1% aep', 'stormwater detention']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           5
modelling to demonstrate that flood impacts can be managed to an acceptable level while maximising development
opportunities.
8.2
Stormwater Management Strategy
8.2.1
Stormwater Detention
Stormwater detention will be required for development within the Precinct, as the increase in impervious surfaces
will lead to increased runoff being generated under developed conditions.
As part of the proposed stormwater management approach, stormwater detention will be required to be
incorporated at either an on-lot or sub-precinct scale to ensure post-development discharge rates do not exceed
pre-development conditions. Stormwater detention prevents urban flash flooding, protects local infrastructure and
reduces erosion. Opportunities for detention are to:
 Provide on-lot on site detention (OSD) through rainwater tanks (or storage tanks for larger allotments) to reduce
runoff at the source, reducing peak flows entering the downstream network. This also benefits the council piped
network as it reduces the pipe size show against catchments. On lot OSD design must be in line with Council
DCP guidelines outlined in Section 3.1.2. Stormwater detention of roads and public infrastructure or open space
would be required to be managed separately by Council where on-lot detention is provided.
 Manage detention via a precinct or sub-precinct scale which would involve providing regional basins at the end
of sub-catchments to provide detention for all upstream development. This type of system would manage
cumulative catchment flows, providing a centralised detention function that supports efficient conveyance and
reduces reliance on oversized pipe infrastructure. Refer to Section 8.1.1. Stormwater detention of roads and
public infrastructure or open space would be incorporated into a regional basin detention strategy.
The advantages and disadvantages to each detention strategy should be considered when determining the best
option for development within the Precinct.
 Rainwater tanks should be chosen when there is limited space available but moderate to low runoff attenuation
required. Rainwater tanks offer the easiest opportunity for reuse on site and have fairly simple maintenance
requirements, however they are normally sized for smaller events and may not effectively manage extreme
storm conditions or large catchment areas.
 Storage tanks are usually designed for larger runoff volumes than rainwater tanks and can more effectively
manage higher peak flow reduction. Tanks can be combined with a rainwater harvesting strategy to provide
reuse on site. Tanks are usually suited to larger development allotments due to high design and cost
requirements as well as complex maintenance regime.
 Regional detention basins offer an economical solution to large catchment areas which can achieve high peak
flow reduction. Regional basins attenuate peak flows but do not largely reduce total runoff volume. Stormwater

--- Page 72 [Matched: ['1% aep', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           6
Developable Land subject to Further Investigation. Impervious surfaces within West Lake, Cockle Creek,
Killingworth and Barnsley West sub-precincts encroach small areas of the 1% AEP extent and therefore no
widespread impacts are expected.
Further assessment will be required for the West Lake sub-precinct, where an existing lake is located within the
proposed development footprint. Decisions regarding whether the lake is retained, modified or filled will influence
both the available floodplain storage and the Water Management Strategy for the site, including the location,
volume and configuration of any required detention infrastructure.
The Killingworth sub-precinct also includes areas mapped as floodway, where development is generally
discouraged due to the need to maintain flood conveyance. Future design stages should therefore seek to avoid or
minimise encroachment into these areas where practicable.
No flood modelling has been conducted as part of this assessment, and earthworks volumes are not yet available.
It is therefore not currently possible to quantify the volume of floodwater displaced or confirm the required size,
location and configuration of detention basins. These matters should be assessed during subsequent design
stages once more detailed development layouts, earthworks information and stormwater management strategies
are available.
Figure 8-3 Percentage impervious surfaces of each sub-precinct and 1% AEP flood extent.
8.2.2
Stormwater Conveyance
The Stormwater Management Strategy will aim to ensure the protection and function of waterways. The Precinct
will be designed to work with nature to use the existing landform to dictate the stormwater conveyance network.

--- Page 73 [Matched: ['water quality', 'norbe', 'cockle creek']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           7
The minor drainage system should be designed to capture and convey minor system (or nuisance flows) as per the
LMCC DCP. The minor system flows are defined as localised flowing with an ARI of 2-5 years. The major drainage
system is part of the public drainage system designed to convey the major storm flows.
Catchment runoff should be directed through a clearly defined pipe or channel network that conveys flows to
designated treatment nodes before discharge to receiving environments. Major overland flow paths should be
preserved if possible, ensuring they remain unobstructed and integrated into the Precinct layout to safely convey
exceedance flows.
Drainage of stormwater within the Precinct will aim to minimise changes to sub-catchments boundaries and direct
flows via existing drainage paths where possible to protect and maintain the hydrological regime of the receiving
waterways. Formalisation of drainage within the Precinct will capture and direct water to stormwater treatment and
detention prior to discharge to the natural water systems to mitigate impacts from increased runoff. Scour
protection and erosion controls should be implemented upstream of discharge locations to natural waterways.
In addition, if no sub-precinct scale or end of pipe stormwater retention or treatment scheme is developed for a site,
development should demonstrate that impervious areas are captured for reuse or managed on lot through
infiltration and retention prior to discharge to the drainage system.  Lake Macquarie City Council DCP indicates that
only 10% of impervious areas on a site can be discharged directly to the receiving drainage system.
8.2.3
Natural Water Systems
The stormwater strategy recommends development considers natural water systems including those within the
Precinct and those downstream. In alignment with Section 3.1.3 and the Lake Macquarie City Council DCP, natural
water systems including waterways and estuaries should be protected and not adversely impacted by
development. This will be done by retaining creeks and natural drainage pathways within their vegetated riparian
zone (VRZ). Preliminary mapping of the VRZ for the Precinct is shown in Figure 3-17 and further discussed in
Section 3.2.9.
Natural systems including all natural watercourses through the Precinct should be maintained in a natural state in
alignment with the Controlled Activity Approvals on Waterfront Land. This will require buffer distances from
waterways to be in place to maintain the riparian vegetation and retain ecological processes. Proactive
rehabilitation of waterways is also recommended where development has the potential to exacerbate an issue or
where additional vegetation buffers between development and the waterway is required.
8.3
Water Quality Management Strategy
The Water Management Strategy adopted will be dependent on what targets are required to be achieved based on
existing/ historical land use. For brownfield development, the strategy will be required to meet best practice
pollutant load reduction targets. For greenfield development, the strategy will be required to meet no net increase

8.3.2
Water Sensitive Urban Design Treatment Train
A combination of on-lot and precinct scale SQIDs have been proposed to support the Master Plan and deliver
water quality targets adopted to protect the highly valued environment downstream of the Precinct and respond to
the local constraints. The key proposed WCM measures are summarised in Table 8-3 and further discussed in

considered suitable for most circumstances but would need to be refined and modelled in detail in subsequent

Table 8-3 WSUD Measures proposed
WSUD Measure
Description
Rainwater tanks
Rainwater tanks to collect water from roofs and supply non potable water demand such as

Gross Pollutant Trap
(GPT)
A GPT is proposed for larger allotments to remove litter and sediment from stormwater runoff prior
to discharge to the street drainage system. A GPT may also be provided at the end of pipe and
may be more suitable for upstream residential development or devlopmet of smaller allotments
where on-lot application is not feasible. For larger allotments and industrial and commercial
development, a GPT should be proposed on lot to manage litter prior to discharge to the drainage
system.
Biofiltration bains
Biofiltration basins are proposed as on-lot and end-of-pipe treatment measures as the preferred
treatment option. These basins are able to meet the water quality targets within a smaller footprint
when compared with constructed wetlands. Additionally, Lake Macquarie City Council has
indicated a preference towards biofiltration basins in recent years.
Wetlands
In some locations, constructed wetlands may be proposed in place of biofiltration to achieve water
quality targets. Constructed wetlands are more suitable for end-of pipe treatment and can be
utilised within integrated landscape planning as a visual amenity, linking public open space with
stormwater management infrastructure.
Street Trees
Street trees should be considered where biofiltration basins or other infrastructure is unable to be

--- Page 76 [Matched: ['water quality']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           10
8.3.3
On-lot Treatment
Rainwater Tanks
For the proposed water quality management strategy roof runoff was assumed to be captured in rainwater
harvesting tanks and reused for non-potable uses such as landscape irrigation and/or toilet flushing.
Preliminary MUSIC modelling was conducted to model a typical treatment train with rainwater tanks paired with
biofiltration basins for treatment, see Figure 8-5. The roof area was considered 60% of the developable area which
was routed to the rainwater tanks with the remaining area going through a GPT. Ultimately, rainwater tank sizing
would be further informed on an individual lot basis, dependent on the downstream infrastructure and achievement
of water quality and detention targets for the site and Precinct. Further development of the potential onsite reuse
will also determine sizing and capture rates for the rainwater tanks. The tanks could be used for irrigation and other
non-potable uses such as toilet flushing.
Gross Pollutant Traps
On lot gross pollutant traps (GPTs) are proposed to be incorporated to provide source-control treatment for
individual larger allotments. These devices capture coarse sediments, litter, organic debris, and other gross
pollutants before runoff enters the downstream drainage network, reducing pollutant loads conveyed to
precinct-scale treatment assets. This helps maintain performance and longevity of downstream end of pipe
infrastructure. On-lot GPTs also support compliance with water quality objectives by distributing treatment across
the catchment rather than relying solely on end-of-pipe systems. Their placement within accessible locations
ensures ongoing maintenance can be undertaken efficiently, preserving treatment effectiveness over the life of the
development.
A nominal GPT unit was adopted for the basic modelling conducted for the removal of gross pollutants, sediment
and some nutrients. If other units are proposed at a later stage, then analysis to confirm equivalent performance
would be required. While on-lot measures support flexible delivery time frames, there is a high risk of on-lot
measures failing due to poor management and maintenance practices in private ownership.
8.3.4
Road Treatment
If no end of pipe treatment is available and water quality is being managed on lot, public roads should have an
independent treatment management approach.  The approach to on street treatment would be via bioretention
basins where size and routing of flows to larger downstream locations is possible. Where space is not available or
for smaller portions of roads, street trees could be considered for implementation along public roads in the Precinct.
The replacement of natural vegetated landscape with impervious areas increases urban temperatures and
stormwater runoff. Street trees will be passively irrigated by directing stormwater into their tree pits, allowing them
to naturally absorb moisture and helping to create a cooler, greener streetscape. Figure 8-7 is an example of a

Integrated Spatial Planning Implications
9.1
Flooding Constraint Classification Framework
The Precinct has been classified into high, moderate and lower flood constraint areas based on the strategic flood
assessment undertaken for the Precinct. The classification provides a framework for identifying areas where
flooding is likely to constrain future development and where additional investigation may be required.
The flood constraint classification is summarised in Table 9-1 and spatially presented in Figure 9-1. The framework
supports an avoidance-first approach, directing future development towards lower constraint areas while
recognising that targeted encroachment into parts of the floodplain may be feasible where supported by
appropriate flood mitigation measures and detailed assessment. This approach seeks to balance development
opportunities with the need to preserve floodplain function and manage flood risk.
The characteristics and planning implications of each flood constraint category are discussed in the following
sections.

10
Master Plan Objectives and Performance Criteria
The objectives and performance criteria in this section have been developed to guide future land use planning,
infrastructure provision and development within the Precinct. They are informed by the flooding and water cycle
management investigations and seek to ensure that future development responds appropriately to flood risk,
drainage constraints, water quality considerations and broader water management outcomes. The objectives
establish the desired strategic outcomes for the Precinct, while the performance criteria provide a framework for
assessing future development proposals and identifying where further investigation or management measures may
be required.
10.1
Proposed Objectives
The proposed objectives establish the desired outcomes for flooding and water cycle management within the
Precinct, having regard to the opportunities and constraints identified through the technical investigations.
 Direct future development towards areas with comparatively lower flood risk and drainage constraints.
 Protect the floodway and flood storage functions of the waterways and associated floodplain areas.
 Avoid intensification of development within areas subject to significant flood hazard or life safety risk.
 Support a movement network that considers flood resilience to future climate risk, emergency access and flood
isolation risks.
 Ensure future development is compatible with the flood risk (hazard, function) and does not increase flood risk
to surrounding land, infrastructure or communities.
 Establish a framework for future detailed flood risk, drainage and emergency management assessments to
demonstrate any management and mitigation measures are suitable for developing in areas of the floodplain.
 Recognise drainage feasibility as a key consideration in determining developable land and future staging.
 Ensure future development incorporates drainage infrastructure that is compatible with downstream hydraulic
controls, including flood and tidal influences.
 Implement Water Sensitive Urban Design (WSUD) measures to achieve urban water cycle management
objectives
 Minimise water quality and quantity impacts from surface water runoff to waterways and sensitive receiving
environments including GDEs, sensitive wetlands and downstream estuarine environments
 Ensure compliance with Lake Macquarie Council Development Control Plan including OSD and stormwater
pollution reduction targets
 Ensure compliance with NSW Estuary Health Risk Dataset which defines nutrient load targets for estuary
catchments
 Preserve groundwater recharge where feasible and minimise impacts to groundwater levels and quality

--- Page 86 [Matched: ['water quality']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           20
 Development is to demonstrate that it will not unreasonably increase flood levels, flood extents, flow velocities
or flood hazard on surrounding land outside of the precinct.
 Development within flood-affected or drainage-constrained land is to demonstrate that flood risk and drainage
constraints are acceptable for the development type or have been mitigated to achieve an acceptable level of
impact.
 Future road, access and movement network planning is to consider flood risk, flood immunity to align with level
of service required and flood isolation risks.
 Flood-constrained land identified through the Master Plan is generally not intended for intensive and/or sensitive
development, evacuation-sensitive land uses or critical infrastructure unless supported by a detailed
assessment and suitable management measures are implemented.
 Future development applications undertake detailed flood, drainage and emergency management
investigations.
 Development must achieve applicable stormwater quality targets including pollutant load reduction and nutrient
load limits relevant to the Lake Macquarie catchment.
 High-priority groundwater dependent ecosystems must be protected in accordance with the applicable policy
framework.
 Development must demonstrate an integrated water cycle and WSUD response that minimises hydrological and
water quality impacts on downstream sensitive receivers.
 WSUD measures must be designed to treat frequent storm events, including flows up to and including the three-
month ARI event
 Development must maintain, to the extent practicable, natural flow paths, sub-catchment function and flow
direction and must not rely solely on piped or heavily engineered stormwater systems where this would increase
erosion, scour or downstream impacts.
 Public domain, landscape and street design must integrate WSUD assets to support urban cooling, biodiversity,
amenity and place identity.
 Construction-phase sediment and erosion controls must be implemented in accordance with the Blue Book

opportunities, constraints and areas requiring further investigation. Given the strategic nature of the Master Plan,
detailed engineering investigations have not been undertaken at this stage.
Future planning, subdivision and development proposals will require progressively more detailed investigations to
demonstrate that flooding, drainage, water quality, groundwater and related environmental constraints have been
appropriately considered. The staging triggers outlined below identify the circumstances under which additional

12
Conclusions and Recommendations
The flooding and drainage assessment has identified that flood risk is a key consideration for future planning and
development within the Precinct. Regional flooding from Cockle Creek, local overland flow paths and downstream
tailwater influences create varying levels of flood constraint across the site, influencing land use suitability,
infrastructure planning and development staging.
While significant flood-prone areas exist, the assessment indicates that opportunities may be available to facilitate
future development through appropriate land use planning, flood mitigation measures and detailed flood and
drainage investigations. The findings provide a strategic framework to guide refinement of the Master Plan and
support future planning decisions.
Water cycle management is a key consideration for future development within the Precinct. Diega Creek, Cockle
Creek and Burkes Creek are the key waterways located within the Precinct. Existing stormwater management
within the Precinct is through natural flow paths within the undeveloped areas and via water management
infrastructure across the disturbed site which includes catchment dams, sediment dams, storage
dams, spillways and drainage systems. The adoption of an integrated approach to water cycle management
prioritises capture, treatment and reuse where feasible and aims to minimise water quality and quantity impacts to
sensitive receiving environments.
12.1
Summary of Findings
The following lists the key findings of the assessment:
 Flooding within the Precinct is primarily influenced by regional flooding from Cockle Creek, together with local
flooding, drainage and downstream tidal influences.
 Flood constraints vary across the Precinct and have been classified into high, moderate and lower constraint
areas to assist future planning and Master Plan refinement.
 Floodways and major flood storage areas should generally be retained to preserve floodplain function and
minimise increases in flood risk.
 Several proposed development areas intersect flood-constrained land and will require further flood and drainage
investigations to demonstrate development feasibility and identify appropriate mitigation measures.
 Emergency management should adopt evacuation as the preferred strategy, with future transport planning
considering flood resilience and flood isolation risks.
 Flood mitigation measures, including detention basins, floodway corridors, flood storage management and
development footprint optimisation, may provide opportunities to increase developable land, subject to detailed
investigation and hydraulic modelling.
 The assessment has been undertaken at a strategic Master Plan level using the latest available flood studies

--- Page 90 [Matched: ['water quality', 'norbe', 'stormwater detention']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           24
 The Water Management Strategy adopted will be dependent on what targets are required to be achieved based
on existing/ historical land use. For brownfield development, the strategy will be required to meet best practice
pollutant load reduction targets. For greenfield development, the strategy will be required to meet no net
increase to pollutant loads (considered NorBE).
12.2
Recommendations for the Master Plan
A site-wide integrated Flood and Stormwater Management Strategy should be prepared following exhibition and
prior to finalisation of development footprints, staging and infrastructure planning. The strategy should assess the
cumulative impact of the proposed development across the wider catchment in greater detail, including changes to
impervious area, runoff volumes and peak flows, floodplain storage, flood conveyance, flood levels and emergency
access. This should be supported by detailed flood modelling, hydraulic modelling and MUSIC modelling
undertaken post-exhibition, including testing of future development layouts, earthworks assumptions and
stormwater management options.
The current assessment is considered suitable to support exhibition of the Master Plan, noting that further detailed
assessment will be required to inform subsequent planning, design and development application stages. The
following recommendations are therefore provided to guide the next stages of planning and development of the
Precinct:
 Use the flood constraint mapping to guide refinement of the Master Plan and future development staging.
 Direct future development towards areas of comparatively lower flood risk where practicable.
 Preserve key flood conveyance corridors and flood storage areas within future land use planning.
 Undertake detailed flood modelling, drainage investigations and emergency management assessments for
areas proposed for future development within or adjacent to flood-prone land.
 Investigate flood mitigation measures, including detention basins, floodway corridors, flood storage offsets and
development footprint optimisation, to maximise development opportunities while maintaining acceptable flood
risk outcomes.
 Review flood planning assumptions against the latest Australian Rainfall and Runoff (ARR2019) climate change
guidance during subsequent planning and design stages.
 Ensure future development demonstrates appropriate flood immunity, drainage performance, emergency
access and evacuation arrangements consistent with Council requirements and contemporary flood risk
management practice.
 Utilise a whole of catchment approach coordinating surface water management across all sub-precincts while
responding to local topography, land use, and hydrological characteristics.
 Adopt a Stormwater Management Strategy which considers stormwater detention, stormwater conveyance and
natural water systems.

Executive summary
This Traffic and Transport Report has been prepared to support the State led rezoning and associated
master planning of the Macquarie Coal Complex (the Precinct) and Teralba South Gate sub-precinct
(collectively referred to as the Site), a large post mining landholding west of Cockle Creek in the Lake
Macquarie Local Government Area. The Site would be located between Teralba and Barnsley, framed by the
M1 Pacific Motorway to the west, the B89 George Booth Drive and Main Road corridor to the north and the
B53 arterial to the east. Wakefield Road is the key north-south spine through the area.
The Site would comprise approximately 211,100 m2 of large format warehousing across the Cockle Creek
and Killingworth employment cores, and a mixed-use core of around 18,100 m2 at West Lake. At full
development, the Site is anticipated to generate in the order of 12,470 daily trips of which 1,030 trips are
expected to be heavy vehicles and 300 RAV 19 metre B-doubles. Peak hour volumes are expected to be in
the order of 1,400 vehicles in the AM peak and 1,300 in the PM peak.
Midblock and intersection performance was reviewed across the base year and the short, medium and long
term, with and without the Precinct and a new Cockle Creek crossing. In the base year the network is
expected to operate at Level of Service (LoS) A to B, with only the Northville Drive corridor toward the B89
approaching LoS C to D.
Background growth is expected to drive the Northville Drive corridor to LoS E by the long term. The southern
and western network would remain at LoS A to C with the Precinct throughout. The Northville Drive and The
Weir Road intersections on the northern approach to the B89, would have adjoining links reaching LoS D in
the short term and level of service F by the long term and consequently have been identified for potential
upgrade, with proportionate contributions where they reach capacity under background growth.
Cockle Creek is a major line of severance for access in the wider area resulting in an already congested
network to the southeast of the precinct. Reducing future vehicle demands on existing creek crossings
through traffic management, mode shift, infrastructure upgrades and potential new infrastructure will be
appropriately incorporated within the onward planning for the precinct.
Three structural findings drive the network strategy:
◼ The approved RAV B-double network does not extend into the Precinct, and heavy vehicle access would
need to be newly established. Upgrading The Weir Road would provide the first compliant RAV route in
the short term, the Wakefield Road route to the M1 would follow in the medium term as the principal
freight route avoiding Barnsley, Holmesville and nearby schools. RAV access from the east through
Teralba could be considered in the long term but would likely require a new road and creek crossing to
bypass the grade constrained Rhondda Road
◼ Rhondda Road climbs features a sustained uphill climb of approximately 1 km for eastbound traffic with
grades ranging from 5 to 10%, followed by a sustained downhill descent of over 1 km at similar grades

1
Introduction
1.1
Introduction
This Traffic and Transport Report (this Report) has been prepared by Aurecon on behalf of the Department
Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI) to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie Coal
Complex (the Precinct).  This Report assesses the proposed rezoning and accompanying Master Plan in
relation to traffic engineering and transport planning related matters.
This Report is structured as set out in Table 1-1.

application of rates also does not consider any discounts to account for trip-chaining and/or self-containment
within the Site given the early stage of planning. This further supports that the assessment is conservative.

This section provides a strategic, desktop-based assessment of existing traffic and transport conditions,
constraints and opportunities of the Precinct. It also provides recommended actions for the post-exhibition
phase. Table 3-1 provides details of the following:
◼ The performance of existing multimodal transport networks surrounding the site, with a particular focus on
the areas likely to present capacity constraints to development if not addressed
◼ Identifies any traffic and transport projects (including both service and capital improvements) currently
proposed, in planning or under development for the surrounds
◼ Provides a high-level indication of the quantum of traffic demand from future land-use options based on
the benchmark of IPG Badgerys Creek Road Master Plan
◼ Provides guidance on prioritisation of transport improvements to ensure the surrounding network can
support planned growth and improved precinct access can be established
◼ Identifies opportunities to incorporate active and public transport to support proposed land uses.

◼ There are current capacity issues at Five Islands Road / The Esplanade roundabout

Fatal and serious injury crashes
Three crashes over the period were fatal and a further 65 resulted in serious injury, so killed and seriously

distributed evenly across the period, at 12 to 14 per year, indicating a persistent rather than improving trend.

24
A global background traffic growth rate of 1.2 per cent per annum has been adopted for the assessment,
based on the STFM growth rates documented in the Draft West Wallsend Rezoning TIA (GHD, 2025). The
available traffic count stations are located on surrounding arterial roads rather than lower-order local roads
within the Precinct; therefore, the STFM growth profile was considered a suitable basis for applying a
consistent background growth allowance.
Review of the STFM data indicates that the highest forecast growth occurred during the 2021–2026 period,
with annual growth rates of approximately 1.9 to 2.0 per cent. This period also aligns with substantial recent
development activity evident from historical satellite imagery, with much of the surrounding area now
appearing largely built out. For the subsequent five-year periods from 2026 to 2041, the STFM indicates
lower annual growth rates, generally ranging from approximately 1.0 to 1.59 per cent. As most base traffic
volumes used in the assessment are from 2024, an adopted growth rate of 1.2 per cent per annum, rounded
for assessment and reporting purposes, is considered to appropriately reflect the forecast growth profile
beyond the initial high-growth period.
3.4
Existing Trip Patterns
Existing trip patterns confirm a strongly car dependent baseline, with private vehicles accounting for around
63 to 66 per cent of journeys to work and only 0 to 1 per cent by public transport and 1 to 2 per cent by
active transport.
This reflects the current regional, lower density context and limited public and active transport connectivity
rather than a desired end state. Consistent with the mode shift sought by NSW Future Transport, the
Movement and Place framework and Council's active transport strategies, the Precinct is planned to improve
on this baseline rather than perpetuate it, through land use, density and connectivity that support higher
walking, cycling and public transport mode share over time. As such the car-based trip generation adopted in
this assessment represents a conservative approach pending the delivery of those improvements.
3.5
Existing Midblock Performance Results
The 2026 baseline AM and PM Peak hour midblock performance assessment was undertaken for locations
illustrated in Figure 3-3. The 2026 baseline indicates how the network performs in its present state, before
any Precinct demand, and identifies where the spare capacity sits and where it does not. LoS A to C
indicates free to stable flow with little delay, LoS D indicates the onset of congestion where flow becomes
sensitive to small increases in demand, level of service E indicates operation at or near capacity, and level of
service F indicates demand exceeding capacity with queuing and breakdown.
◼ The Wakefield Road spine operates at LoS A to B in both directions and both peaks, the strongest result

--- Page 37 [Matched: ['rail loop', 'public transport']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Final_Traffic & Transport_Engineering Report Structure.docx  2026-07-13  Revision C

3.8
Rail Network
The Precinct is served by the Central Coast & Newcastle Line (CCN), which provides intercity rail
connections between Newcastle Interchange and Sydney Central via the Central Coast. The line serves as
the primary heavy rail corridor for the region, providing connections to major destinations including
Newcastle CBD, Gosford, and Sydney, with broader interchange opportunities available at key nodes along
the corridor.
Four stations are located in the surrounding area particularly on the eastern side, as shown in Figure 3-5.
Teralba Station is located east of the Precinct providing the most direct rail access. The southern parcel of
the Precinct sits between Teralba Station to the north and Booragul Station to the south, affording this
portion of the site particularly strong access to the rail network. Cockle Creek Station, located to the north,
serves as a broader interchange station and key public transport node for the locality. Fassifern Station,
located further south beyond the study area boundary, serves as the most significant station in the broader
locality, accommodating CCN services as well as intercity and regional services to Brisbane, Casino, Grafton
and Armidale.
CCN services at Teralba, Booragul and Cockle Creek stations operate at a frequency of approximately 2
services per hour in each direction during the AM peak (7:00 am 8:00 am) and PM peak (5:00 pm 6:00 pm)
periods, resulting in a combined frequency of approximately 4 services per hour across both directions.
Services are operated by the Mariyung (D-Set) fleet, which operates in configurations of 4 to 10 cars with a
seated capacity ranging from 325 passengers (4-car) to 1,312 passengers (10-car) per service. Based on a
conservative assumed 4-car consist, this equates to a theoretical capacity of approximately 650 passengers
per hour in the peak direction.
3.9
Freight Network
3.9.1
Road freight network
The road freight network in the vicinity of the Precinct is served by multiple approved heavy vehicle routes.
B-double approved routes, as identified on the National Heavy Vehicle Regulator (NHVR) network, in the
immediate area include the Pacific Motorway (M1), the B53 arterial corridor (Five Islands Road and The
Esplanade), the B89 arterial route, Northville Drive and TC Frith Avenue. These routes are approved for B-
double vehicles up to 19m, 23m and 25/26m configurations.
Within and immediately adjacent to the Precinct, Rhonda Road along the southern boundary, a section of
Wakefield Road within the western portion of the study area, and York Street east of the southern parcel are

--- Page 47 [Matched: ['traffic generation']] ---
Project number 531168-000  File Final_Traffic & Transport_Engineering Report Structure.docx  2026-07-13  Revision C
40
4.2.2
Benefits of the proposed road network
For general vehicles the proposed network delivers three principal benefits:
◼ Demand is distributed across three to four directions of approach rather than loading a single access,
keeping every access within a manageable range so all internal corridors are expected to operate at LoS
C or better at full development once appropriate upgrades to the surrounding network have been
delivered
◼ The hierarchy is legible, with a single sub arterial spine collecting from collectors and locals, so through
movements and freight stay on the roads designed for them and the residential streets are protected from
intrusion
◼ Resilience is built in, with multiple accesses and internal connections forming a connected network that
offers alternative paths when an access is closed by incident, flood or maintenance. Future connectivity
improvements, including a potential Cockle Creek crossing, would further strengthen this network over
time.
The Wakefield Road spine gives the employment cores a direct, sensitive receiver free run to the M1 Pacific
Motorway, the highest capability freight corridor in the region, while Northville Drive provides a second
classified route to the B89 for the port and Newcastle industrial markets.
Significant or major freight generating lots front the spine and the principal collectors, allowing heavy
vehicles to navigate without traversing local streets. The separation of the residential sub-precincts from the
employment cores by the conservation lands and the creek corridors allows freight amenity impacts to be
managed by geography as much as by regulation. The deferred central link, contingent on declassification of
the tailings storage facility, will shorten internal freight paths between Cockle Creek and Killingworth and
remove circuitous routing via the external network.
4.2.3
Freight access points
The Precinct has three defined freight access points, each with a distinct role:
◼ South Access from Wakefield Road. The primary RAV gateway to the M1 Pacific Motorway, carrying the
Sydney, Central Coast long haul markets. Its upgrade and NHVR approval are the critical enabling aspect
◼ North Access from Northville Drive and the B89. Serves the Port of Newcastle, Newcastle industrial and
Hunter Valley markets and long haul routes on the M1 Pacific Motorway to northern regions
◼ East Access from Powerhouse Road, Teralba. Limited to general heavy vehicles for local servicing in the
short term and enabled for RAV access in the long term. A potential new Cockle Creek crossing is one
option that could alleviate heavy vehicle traffic through Teralba and improve connection to the eastern

4.6
Integration of Transport Opportunities
4.6.1
Planned Transport Upgrades
Four committed or planned external upgrades shape the assessment:
◼ Speers Point Transport Improvements (TfNSW, committed). Address existing B53 congestion with travel
time reductions of up to 50 per cent, assumed in the short term assessment
◼ Cockle Creek Station accessibility upgrade (TfNSW, committed). Strengthens the main public transport
hub for the area
◼ M1 to Raymond Terrace extension (under delivery). Addresses motorway capacity north of the area and
improves long haul reliability for the Port of Newcastle and Brisbane markets
◼ Place Strategy items. The Teralba and Booragul stations multimodal upgrades (S13 and S16) and the Pitt
Street intersection upgrade (L19), adopted as performance criteria.
4.6.2
Strategic Transport Opportunities
The principal strategic opportunity is a new Cockle Creek crossing.
Depending on the form of this crossing this could provide a key sustainable transport link between the
precinct and Cockle Creek Station and residential areas to the east of the creek, significantly shortening the
travel distance and making active and public transport trips more viable.
While this connection has also been raised as a new general traffic connection, to relieve pressure on the
northern corridor, similar results may be able to be achieved through other upgrades and improvements and
will be subject to further modelling and investigation.
The Lower Hunter Freight Corridor between Fassifern and Hexham strengthens the case for freight-based
land uses subject to delivery timing, as such land uses relying on the corridor are treated as a longer-term
proposition while local freight planning remains consistent with it.
The Sydney to Newcastle High Speed Rail scheme includes a Lake Macquarie station at Morisset, and while
a station within the Precinct is not part of the current scheme the alignment is still being finalised, so
development must not prejudice the corridor, noting that even without a local stop the project would improve
regional accessibility through interchange via the Northern line and could release capacity for additional local

Outcomes:
◼ Reduced reliance on long-haul road freight
◼ Reduced congestion and emissions
◼ Enhanced economic competitiveness of the employment cores.
Rail freight connection via the Lower Hunter Freight Corridor
A future rail freight connection could be provided by linking the Precinct to the ARTC network and the
planned Lower Hunter Freight Corridor, giving the eastern employment land an intermodal interface with the
regional and interstate freight task. Land uses relying on this corridor are treated as a longer-term
proposition subject to delivery timing, and the structure plan should protect the alignment and rail loop
options so that staging decisions do not foreclose a future rail-served capability.
Outcomes:
◼ A protected pathway to rail-served freight
◼ Reduced long haul road dependence over time
◼ A more diverse and resilient freight offering.
4.6.4
Potential Upgrades
The staged upgrades represent a potential program based on the scenario assessed. Short term works
enable freight access and first stage development, the medium term could include the internal duplication
associated with the full build out, and the long term would secure the strategic M1 Pacific Motorway and rail
connections. These staging outcomes should be treated as indicative, and deviation from this program
should be considered as the project progresses and trip volumes become clearer. The road upgrades
attributed to each horizon are illustrated in Figure 4-8 summarised in Table 4-13.
A potential future Cockle Creek crossing is identified separately as an opportunity to improve eastern
connectivity into the Precinct rather than a committed upgrades, and its need, timing and form would be
subject to further investigation as the project progresses.

Design of Roads and Streets guide
While these Council instruments set an indicative baseline, they pre-date current practice and are oriented
primarily towards vehicle movement. To deliver streets that better support walking, cycling and public
transport across all levels of the hierarchy, the Precinct cross sections should also draw on the TfNSW
Design of Roads and Streets guide (DORAS, 2024), which applies the NSW Movement and Place
framework to street design.
DORAS does not assign a single fixed cross section to each road type. Instead, the cross section is derived
from two things together, being the movement function of the road and the place context it sits within, so that
the same nominal road type is treated differently in a residential street and an employment corridor. This
document sets out the key principles, considerations and design aspects to carry into the Precinct road
types, to complement and where appropriate exceed the minimum DCP cross sections.
DORAS sets out nine principles for NSW roads and streets. These principles frame every cross section and
are summarised in Table 4-15, with the active transport implication for the Precinct noted for each.
Table 4-15: NSW roads and street principle (TfNSW Design of Roads and Streets (TS 00066:1.0), 2024))
Principle
Application to the Precinct
On Country
Roads and streets offer pathways for connecting to and building relationships with
Country, integrated through early engagement and landscape design.
For people
Streets are welcoming, equitable, inclusive and accessible for people of all ages,
genders and abilities, not designed for vehicles alone.
Safe
The Safe System approach is applied so that human or mechanical error does not
result in death or serious injury, with lower speeds where vulnerable users and
vehicles interact.
Healthy
Design enables walking and cycling for everyday trips and reduces congestion,
urban heat, noise and emissions.
Public spaces
Streets are treated as part of the network of public spaces, supporting activity and
social life rather than movement only.
Ecological network

5
Conclusions
The assessment demonstrates that the Precinct can be supported by a feasible and appropriately staged
access strategy. At full theoretical development of the Structure Plan land use schedule, being approximately
211,100 m2 GFA of large format warehousing, a mixed use commercial core of around 18,100 m2 gross
leasable area at West Lake and around 10 dwellings at Barnsley West, the Precinct is anticipated to
generate in the order of 12,470 daily trips of which 1,030 trips are expected be heavy vehicles and 30 RAV
19 metre B-doubles. Peak hour volumes are expected to be in the order of 1,400 vehicles in the AM peak
and 1,300 in the PM peak.
Comparing the baseline with the Precinct impact shows that the constraints on the network are driven
primarily by background growth rather than by the development.
◼ In the 2026 base year the network has substantial spare capacity. The Wakefield Road spine, Rhondda
Road, The Broadway and the western local roads operate at LoS A to B, and only the Northville Drive
corridor toward the B89 is close to desirable its limit, at LoS C to D before any Precinct traffic.
◼ Background growth alone, without the Precinct, pushes the Northville Drive corridor to LoS E by the long
term (Northville Drive north of The Weir Road reaching a volume to capacity ratio of around 0.97),
confirming the constraint exists independent of the Precinct.
◼ The southern and western network carries the Precinct comfortably in every scenario, with Rhondda
Road at the southern access remaining at LoS A throughout, as such, the impact is concentrated on the
northern corridors rather than spread across the network.
Cockle Creek is a major line of severance for access to the Precinct resulting in an already congested
access route linking the Precinct to the east, and limited capacity for both background growth and precinct
traffic to the north. Reducing the impact of this line of severance through traffic management, mode shift,
infrastructure upgrades and potential new infrastructure will be appropriately incorporated within the onward
planning for the precinct.
Potential upgrades as identified at this stage have been staged so that each is triggered by an indicative
outcome of this assessment, and the indicative program distinguishes capability and access works from
background driven capacity works to which the development contributes proportionately.
Development is to be staged consistent with the assessed capacity of each access point, land uses
generating RAV movements are to be located on the approved or proposed RAV network, and detailed
SIDRA modelling is to confirm intersection geometry, control type and treatment at the development
application or subdivision stage. Subject to the staged upgrade program identified in this report, there are no
presently known transport issues that would make the proposed rezoning inherently inappropriate.

Figure A- 3: Comparison of motor vehicle ownership in 2021 between Bolton Point – Teralba (SA2) and NSW
Journey to work
The following travel characteristics relating to modes of transport used to travel to work by Lake Macquarie -
West residents (refer to Figure A- 4), workers travelling to Lake Macquarie - West, and NSW were identified:
◼ Private vehicle travel is the dominant mode for both residents and workers, accounting for approximately
63 to 66% of journeys - significantly higher than the NSW average (48%).
◼ Public transport usage is very low at 0-1%, well below the NSW average of 3%, reflecting the limited
availability of higher-frequency services in the area.
◼ Active transport modes account for a small proportion of journeys (1 to 2%), slightly below the NSW
average (3%).
◼ A higher proportion of 'other' modes, including working from home, is recorded for residents (35%) than
for incoming workers (31%), with both below the NSW average (46%).
◼ The major trip attractors within Lake Macquarie - West are health care and social assistance facilities,
construction, education and training, and retail trade, which together account for approximately 58 per
cent of all workers travelling to the area. Mining also represents a notable attractor, reflecting proximity to
Hunter Valley coal operations.
Overall, the data indicates a strong reliance on private vehicles within Lake Macquarie - West, likely
reflecting the regional context, lower density land use, and public and active transport connectivity.

1.4
Relevant Legislation
1.4.1
Commonwealth Environment Protection and Biodiversity
Conservation Act 1999
The EPBC Act is the Commonwealth Government’s central piece of environmental
legislation.  It provides a legal framework to protect and manage nationally and
internationally important flora, fauna, ecological communities and heritage places – defined
in the EPBC Act as Matters of National Environmental Significance (MNES).  Under the
EPBC Act, any action (which includes a development, project or activity) that is considered
likely to have a significant impact on MNES (including nationally listed threated communities
and species and listed migratory species) must be referred to the Australian Government
Minister for the Environment.  The purpose of the referral is to allow a decision to be made
about whether an action requires approval on a Commonwealth level. If an action is declared
a “controlled action”, then Commonwealth approval is required.
This report considers the potential for MNES to occur within the subject land.
1.4.2
NSW Environmental Planning and Assessment Act 1979
The NSW Environmental Planning and Assessment Act 1979 (EP&A Act) is the overarching
planning legislation in NSW that provides for the creation of planning instruments that guide
land use. The EP&A Act also provides for the protection of the environment, including the
protection and conservation of native fauna and flora species.  This includes threatened
species, populations and ecological communities, and their habitats, as listed under the BC
Act and FM Act.
1.4.3
NSW Biodiversity Conservation Act 2016
The BC Act is the key piece of legislation in NSW relating to the protection and management
of biodiversity and threatened species. The purpose of the BC Act is to maintain a healthy,

11
create a spatial database that was used to interpret and interpolate the data to produce a
vegetation map of the subject land.
2.3.2
Preliminary Habitat Constraints Assessment
Field surveys within the subject land included a preliminary assessment of habitat
constraints and microhabitats for predicted species credit flora and fauna species 28-29
April, 27 May, 1-5 June, and 9 June 2026.
Preliminary habitat assessments were carried out across selected parts of the subject land,
with a focus on the developable areas, and involved an assessment of site habitat
characteristics and identification of microhabitats suitable for threatened flora and fauna
species. Features that were assessed include, but are not limited to:
•
Broad vegetation type (e.g., shrubland, open woodland, grassland) and associated
PCTs;
•
Presence of water bodies;
•
Tree height and presence of mature trees;
•
Presence of hollows and size of hollows;
•
Presence of nests;
•
Presence of rock outcrops or surface rock;
•
Presence of cliffs, overhangs or escarpments; and
•
Incidental flora and fauna sightings.

2.5
Survey Limitations
Coverage of the entire subject land during field surveys was not possible due to constraints
associated with timing of this assessment in combination with safety hazards in some
sections of the subject land where existing mining rehabilitation works is ongoing. Based on
instructions from DPHI, the focus of the surveys was therefore on the potential developable
areas of the subject land, as shown on the Structure Plan. Based on the results from the
desktop assessment, it is considered that the areas identified as environmentally
constrained areas in the Structure Plan are highly likely to comprise the biodiversity values
identified in this assessment and would therefore restrict the potential for future
development. Nonetheless, where time permitted, parts of the environmentally constrained
areas were also subject to surveys.
Due to the restricted timing for this assessment, the entirety of the rehabilitated vegetation in
the western parts of the subject land were not subject to field traverses. However,
representative areas were surveyed along the boundaries of the rehabilitated areas. In
combination with the flora planting list that SLR was provided, which details the flora species
planted within the rehabilitation areas, the assessment of PCTs and associated TECs in
these areas are considered sufficient for this BAR. Nonetheless, future detailed
assessments at the DA stage will need to further assess the vegetation mapping in the
rehabilitation areas, as well as all other areas of the subject land subject to a future DA.
Although condition states for PCTs were mapped where time permitted, in line with the
mapping of vegetation zones under the BAM, further refinement of vegetation zones is
required at the DA stage for relevant technical assessments.
The weather conditions at the time of the flora surveys were generally favourable for plant
growth and production of features required for identification of most species. Shrubs,
grasses, herbs and creepers were readily identifiable in most instances. Despite this, it is
expected that not all flora species present within the subject land would have been recorded
during surveys as no floristic BAM plot surveys were undertaken in the subject land.

4.0
Native Vegetation
4.1
Native Vegetation Extent
Parts of the subject land have been subject to limited vegetation verification field surveys by
SLR for the purpose of this BAR. The native vegetation extent within the subject land was
determined through aerial photograph interpretation and field surveys, in combination with
the use of the SVTM (DCCEEW (NSW), 2020) in areas that were not subject to field surveys
by SLR. The native vegetation extent within the subject land is shown in Figure 12. It
occupies approximately 904 ha, which represents approximately 78% of the subject land.
The native vegetation extent within the subject land comprises both remnant native
vegetation and planted native vegetation, including vegetation planted as part of the
rehabilitation requirements of the former mine.
4.1.1
Changes to the mapped native vegetation extent
No additional differences between the aerial photographs using in this assessment and the
native vegetation cover shown in Figure 12 have been identified.
4.1.2
Areas that are not native vegetation
The remaining land within the subject land comprises cleared land and exotic vegetation,
totalling an area of approximately 255 ha. In accordance with Section 4.1.2 of BAM, these
areas do not require further assessment, unless they provide habitat for threatened species
or are proposed for restoration as part of an offset.
4.2
Plant Community Types

mapping (DCCEEW (NSW), 2020), in the subject land are summarised in Table 2 and
shown in Figure 13.

4.2.4.5 Alignment with Threatened Ecological Communities
BC Act Status: EEC
EPBC Act Status: Critically Endangered Ecological Community (CEEC)
This PCT is associated with the River-flat Eucalypt Forest (RFEF) TEC, which is listed as an
EEC under the BC Act as River-Flat Eucalypt Forest on Coastal Floodplains of the New
South Wales North Coast, Sydney Basin and South East Corner Bioregions, and as a CEEC
under the EPBC Act as River-flat eucalypt forest on coastal floodplains of southern New
South Wales and eastern Victoria.
An assessment has not been made against the condition criteria for the EPBC Listed TEC,
however based on the observations from the field surveys, the vegetation in the subject land
generally matches the BC Act and EPBC Act listed TECs (NSW Scientific Committee,
2011a; Threatened Species Scientific Committee, 2020) due to:
•
Occurring on periodically inundated alluvial flats and drainage lines associated with
coastal floodplains;
•
Having a canopy dominated by Eucalyptus saligna and Angophora floribunda, and
understorey dominated by Melaleuca styphelioides and/or Melaleuca linariifolia;
•
Having a shrub layer including species such as Breynia oblongifolia, Acmena smithii,

--- Page 45 [Matched: ['pct', 'threatened species']] ---
Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure
Biodiversity Assessment Report
1 July 2026
SLR Project No.: 610.033744.00001
SLR Ref No.: 610.033744.00001-R02-BAR-
v1.0 20260701.docx

together have most fidelity to the PCT. Other species present can include any of the species
of the adjoining PCTs, from either drier or damper communities. Species can include
Eucalyptus amplifolia subsp. amplifolia, Corymbia gummifera, Corymbia maculata,
Eucalyptus fibrosa, Eucalyptus racemosa, Eucalyptus piperita, and Eucalyptus sparsifolia.
A defining characteristic is the presence of a sub-canopy of taller Melaleuca spp., mostly
Melaleuca decora or Melaleuca sieberi, coupled with a moderately dense understorey of
Melaleuca nodosa. This differs from PCT 3436, in which eucalypts and taller Melaleuca spp.
occur as sparser emergents over a very dense shrub layer of Melaleuca nodosa.
Some other shrub species can be common, with Leptospermum polygalifolium subsp.
cismontanum frequently present, along with Acacia longifolia subsp. longifolia and Banksia
collina.
The ground layer is frequently dominated by Entolasia stricta, Microlaena stipoides var.
stipoides, and Ptilothrix deusta, with Entolasia marginata common. Gonocarpus tetragynus
and Dianella caerulea subsp. producta are forbs that are often present.
In the north of the subject land, some occurrences of this PCT and to a lesser extent PCT
3436 have a unique canopy dominated nearly entirely by hybrids of the threatened species
Angophora inopina and Angophora floribunda. These trees are generally taller than
Angophora inopina and often have leaf petioles greater than 8mm (petioles less than 8mm in
length is one of the defining features of Angophora inopina). Leaves however are thicker,
shorter, and narrower than is typical for Angophora inopina. This situation is not mentioned
in the LMCC mapping (S. A. J. Bell, 2016), but is mentioned in (S. Bell, 2004), where it is
described as:
In addition, representation of Angophora inopina in other geographically
restricted vegetation types not described here may also have significant
conservation value. These include an Angophora inopina x Angophora floribunda
– Melaleuca Thicket Forest from the northern Lake Macquarie area, where
hybrid zones of Angophora inopina x Angophora floribunda occur with Melaleuca
linariifolia, Melaleuca nodosa, Melaleuca decora and Eucalyptus resinifera,

--- Page 48 [Matched: ['pct', 'threatened species']] ---

1 July 2026

39
absent and replaced by a wide variety of other coastal eucalypt species including Eucalyptus
longifolia, Eucalyptus fibrosa, Eucalyptus tereticornis or Corymbia maculata.
The main diagnostic attribute is the closed canopy of the mid-stratum that almost always is
completely dominated by Melaleuca nodosa. Other Melaleucas are occasionally present
such as Melaleuca sieberi and Melaleuca decora, however only as scattered individuals. The
dense mid-stratum shades much of the light and sclerophyll shrubs are sparse and only
occasionally recorded, including Acacia longifolia, Breynia oblongifolia and Leucopogon
juniperinum. The ground layer is a mid-dense to dense cover of grasses, mainly dominated
by two species, Entolasia stricta and Microlaena stipoides, with other species such Imperata
cylindrica less abundant. Other very frequent species are graminoids and forbs including
Dianella caerulea, Lomandra longifolia and Lobelia purpurascens.
This PCT is recorded on alluviums, fine grained Permo-Triassic and Carboniferous
sediment, typically on flats and depressions, occasionally also found on adjoining gentle
gradient hill slopes that retain soil moisture from subsurface water. It grades into PCT 3435
with gradual increase in elevation or improved soil drainage.
4.2.8.2 Subject Land Description
PCT 3436 occurs in areas of alluvial flats, broadly across the north of the subject land on the
fringes of floodplains associated with Burkes Creek and Cockle Creek. PCT 3436 occupies
similar areas of habitat to PCT 3435, though often occurs in slightly lower areas where soils
are more likely to be more frequently waterlogged.
Canopy species are similar to PCT 3435, with Angophora costata and Eucalyptus resinifera
subsp. resinifera the most likely generally to be present and together have most fidelity to
the PCT. However, in this PCT, eucalypts mostly occur as scattered emergents over a
dense scrub of Melaleuca nodosa. Other Melaleuca spp. occur as juveniles in the scrubby
understorey, or as more mature emergents with eucalypts, and include Melaleuca decora
and Melaleuca sieberi.
The shrub and ground layer besides denser occurrences of Melaleuca nodosa are similar to
PCT 3435. Other shrub species can be common, with Leptospermum polygalifolium subsp.

v1.0 20260701.docx

heterophylla subsp. eglandulosa, Gonocarpus tetragynus, and Dianella caerulea var.
producta. The grass tree Xanthorrhoea latifolia subsp. latifolia is very common in the ground
later in this community.
4.2.10.3 Condition States
Within the subject land PCT 3582 occurs in one condition state, comprising largely intact
vegetation.
Intact
The Intact form of PCT 3582 comprises all the occurrences within the subject land.
Vegetation is as described above in the subject land description. This condition is generally
intact, though likely to have higher occurrences of exotic species due to edge effects where
it adjoins areas associated with historical clearing in the subject land.
Exotic species overall are rare in this condition zone. The exotic shrub Lantana camara is
occasionally present with a scattered distribution.
An example of this condition state is provided below in Photo 12.
Photo 12  PCT 3582 Intact within the subject land
4.2.10.4 Justification of PCT Selection
A review of regional mapping shows the majority of the areas where SLR mapped 3582
have been mapped as PCT 3581, with the exception of the north-west where the SVTM
(DCCEEW (NSW), 2020) and SLR polygons of PCT 3582 overlap. Some areas have also
been mapped as PCT 3244 and PCT 3234. The latter two PCTs are not a suitable match as
they are dominated by Corymbia maculata, which does not occur in these areas.
PCT 3582 is the best fit PCT for these areas due to the lack of Eucalyptus piperita and other
species which are indicative of slightly higher soil fertility. The vegetation present is a good
match for PCT 3582 floristically, and the topographical description of PCT 3582 occurring on

--- Page 57 [Matched: ['pct', 'bos']] ---
Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure
Biodiversity Assessment Report
1 July 2026
SLR Project No.: 610.033744.00001
SLR Ref No.: 610.033744.00001-R02-BAR-
v1.0 20260701.docx
48
and Bolboschoenus species dominate individual sites, reflecting the floristic diversity of this
PCT.
This community often occurs in disturbed environments and may potentially include derived
states. The assessment of new non-woody freshwater wetland plots against this PCT should
primarily consider environmental domain. The grassy wetland PCT 4055 also occurs on
alluvium on the far South Coast, however has a much higher median elevation (known
between 100 and 200 metres asl), and includes Isachne globosa, Lachnagrostis filiformis,
Carex gaudichaudiana and Cyperus sphaeroideus which are all unknown or very rare in
PCT 3975.
4.2.11.2 Subject Land Description
PCT 3975 occurs within water bodies within the subject land. Many of these water bodies
are dams or artificial water bodies that are associated with history of use of the site for coal
mining. These have been colonised with native, aquatic plant species. In some cases,
aquatic plant species have likely been spread into the water bodies by bird species. Some of
the water bodies in the north-east however appear to have been created in what would
historically have been part of a large floodplain system of Cockle Creek, and many species
in these areas may have persisted despite disturbance of the area.
These areas were not studied in detail due to time constraints, but are likely to contain a
large array of locally native aquatic species across the water bodies subject land, due to the
large areas of habitat present. Some species observed include Typha orientalis, Eleocharis
sphacelata, Phragmites australis, Ranunculus inundatus, Juncus continuus, and Persicaria
spp.
It is likely these areas contain many exotic aquatic plant species, but all observed water
bodies were dominated by native species.
4.2.11.3 Condition States
Within the subject land no attempt was made to assess condition of individual occurrences
of PCT 3975 due to time constraints. As such, they are all broadly grouped within the one
condition state, Intact, however no qualitative assessments were made at the patch scale.

4.2.11.4 Justification of PCT Selection
PCT 3975 is the only PCT within the Wyong IBRA subregion associated with the Freshwater
Wetlands on Coastal Floodplains TEC, with the exception of 3962 which is specifically
dominated heavily by Phragmites australis. That species is present in some areas, but not
specifically dominant.
4.2.11.5 Alignment with Threatened Ecological Communities
BC Act Status: EEC
EPBC Act Status: Not listed
This PCT relates to the BC Act listed Freshwater Wetlands on Coastal Floodplains of the
New South Wales North Coast, Sydney Basin and South East Corner Bioregions TEC.
Paragraph 4 of the final determination (NSW Scientific Committee, 2011c) for this TEC
states “Artificial wetlands created on previously dry land specifically for purposes such as
sewerage treatment, stormwater management and farm production, are not regarded as part
of this community, although they may provide habitat for threatened species”. The TEC
occurs in a number of artificial waterbodies within the subject land, and in some natural
areas. Many of the artificial waterbodies in the east of the subject land appear to be in areas
that would have historically been floodplains and swamps, and therefore not previously dry
land. As a precautionary approach all areas have been considered to conform to the TEC,
pending future detailed consideration of all evidence of historical water bodies and water
courses.
Vegetation within the subject land otherwise fits the TEC because it:
•
Occurs within coastal floodplains;
•
Comprises areas subject to periodic or semi-permanent inundation with freshwater;

Degraded
The degraded form of PCT 4042 consists of isolated patches in the east of the subject land,
where vegetation can be traced back through historical aerial photographs (NSW Spatial
Services, 2026b) to comprise remnant/regrowth vegetation, in small areas that have
remained uncleared or have regrown following clearing. These small patches have remnant
or regrowth trees of Eucalyptus amplifolia and Eucalyptus tereticornis, however occur over
an understorey and ground lay largely comprise weeds, with the exception of some common
native species such as the grass Microlaena stipoides var. stipoides.
Rehabilitation Plantings
This condition occurs as a large patch in the west of the subject land, which can be seen
through historical aerial photographs (NSW Spatial Services, 2026b) to have been mined
and then filled. Plantings in this area predominately consist of sporadic planted Eucalyptus
tereticornis trees and sparse Melaleuca spp. plantings. This is an elevated area, that may
have formerly contained a minor creek line, but would not have contained a floodplain area
suitable for supporting PCT 4042. As most plantings are species consistent with PCT 4042,
this is the best fit PCT. Due to there being no attempt generally to restore an ecosystem in
this location, careful analysis in the future of the Planted Native Vegetation module under the
BAM is likely to result in consideration of this area as containing planted native vegetation,
and not a PCT. With the exception of sporadic plantings, this area is heavily dominated by
tall occurrences of the exotic grass Setaria sphacelata.

4.2.15.4 Justification of PCT Selection
A review of regional mapping shows the areas of 4042 have been mapped generally as PCT
4042, with some areas mapped as PCT 3583 (DCCEEW (NSW), 2020). PCT 3583 is a dry
sclerophyll forest PCT areas dominated by Eucalyptus haemastoma, which does not match
these wetland areas dominated by Eucalyptus amplifolia and Angophora floribunda.
PCT 4042 fits these areas well as it is described as being dominated by the red gums
Eucalyptus amplifolia and Eucalyptus tereticornis with Angophora floribunda, and matches
the River-flat Eucalypt Forest  (RFEF) TEC. There is no other coastal PCT from the Wyong
IBRA subregion dominated by red gums that is also associated with the RFEF TEC.
These areas are consistent with the MU 38a Floodplain Redgum – Rough-barked Apple
Forest from the LMCC Mapping. Stephen Bell notes Eucalyptus tereticornis as the dominant
red gum, however Eucalyptus amplifolia is mostly dominant in the community between the
subject land and West Wallsend, with Eucalyptus tereticornis occurring occasionally.
Sporadic individuals with large buds and more than 7 buds in an umbellaster indicate there
is hybridisation between the species in the locality.
4.2.15.5 Alignment with Threatened Ecological Communities
BC Act Status: EEC
EPBC Act Status: CEEC

Sydney Basin and South East Corner Bioregions, and Critically Endangered under the
EPBC Act as River-flat eucalypt forest on coastal floodplains of southern New South Wales
and eastern Victoria.
An assessment has not been made against the condition criteria for the EPBC Listed TEC,
however this vegetation generally matches the BC Act and EPBC Act listed TECs (NSW
Scientific Committee, 2011a; Threatened Species Scientific Committee, 2020)due to:

5.0
Threatened Species
No targeted threatened species surveys were undertaken for this BAR. However, this
chapter provides an assessment of the threatened flora and fauna species that are likely to
require further assessment at the DA stage for a future development in the in the
developable areas of the subject land, in accordance with the BAM.
5.1
Identifying Threatened Species
The BAM-C generates a list of threatened species requiring assessment utilising a number
of variables. The following criteria have been utilised to predict the threatened species
requiring further assessment for a future development:
•
IBRA Subregion: Wyong;
•
Associated PCTs (limited to PCTs in the developable area only):
•
Percent native vegetation cover in the assessment area: 60%;
•
Patch size: ≥100 ha; and
•
Credit type: Ecosystem and/or species.
Based on the above variables, the BAM-C generated a list of ecosystem credit species and
species credit species. Ecosystem credit species and species credit species are assessed
further in Section 5.2 and Section 5.3, respectively.
In addition to the species predicted by the BAM-C, species that occur in the subject land or
are likely to occur in the subject land, based on a likelihood of occurrence assessment
(Appendix A) of previous records held in BioNet and species generated by a PMST search,
have been added to the relevant lists of ecosystem credit species and species credits

Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure

69
These fauna habitats are described further below.
Riparian Environments and Dams
Several existing watercourses that are tributaries to Cockle Creek flow through the central
and western portion of the subject land, including Burkes Creek. These watercourses range
from first to fourth order streams. Some of these watercourses and associated riparian
corridors occurs within or adjacent to the developable areas.
The riparian corridors within the subject land provide suitable foraging and breeding habitat
for a number of fauna species, including amphibians, birds, mammals and reptiles. Pools of
water in the drainage lines would provide a valuable drinking water source in hot and dry
periods.
Terrestrial Habitat Features
Terrestrial habitat features occur in the vegetated areas of the subject land. Features such
as fallen logs, debris and leaf litter provide shelter for small to medium sized terrestrial fauna
species known from the locality of the subject land. In addition to providing habitat for
terrestrial fauna, fallen logs and shrub vegetation provide foraging perches and calling
locations for small birds.
Stick Nests
Stick nests were recorded in the subject land during field surveys that provide breeding
habitat for native bird species. Stick nests were categorised in terms of size, to determine
their suitability for threatened species. Only small and medium stick nests were recorded.
Hollow-bearing Trees and Stags
Tree hollows are an essential resource for a number of fauna species that rely on them for
refuge and nesting (Newton 1994, Gibbons and Lindenmayer 2002, Heinsohn et al. 2003,
Cockle et al. 2010). A number of hollow-bearing trees were observed within the developable
areas, which mainly comprised small hollows (<10cm diameter) in mature living trees and
stags. Due to the relatively young age of the rehabilitation areas, these areas are not
expected to comprise many tree hollows. The tree hollows and stags within the developable
areas and wider subject land provide shelter, roosting and nesting habitat for arboreal fauna

6.0
Constraints and Opportunities Assessment
This chapter provides a discussion of the existing biodiversity constraints that are present
within the subject land, based on the result from the desktop assessment and field surveys.
It also includes a justification around the selection of potential developable areas as well as
environmentally constrained areas as part of the Structure Plan, based on existing
biodiversity values. The findings of this assessment have informed and shaped the Master
Plan in relation to general areas that could have potential future development outcomes.
6.1
Constraints Classes
The subject land has been subject to wide-spread disturbance from the previous mining
operations. Nonetheless, there are parts of the subject land that comprises biodiversity
values that would, or have the potential to, constrain future development in relevant areas of
the subject land.
Key biodiversity constraints identified within the subject land in this assessment include:
•
Presence of native vegetation, including TECs and rehabilitation areas;
•
Known and potential habitat for threatened species, including habitat features;
•
Land mapped as Coastal Wetlands, Key Fish Habitat, and land within riparian
corridors; and
•
Land subject to existing VCAs.
This assessment has identified five constraint classes relevant to the above listed
biodiversity constraints, based on how likely they are to restrict future development: very
high, high, moderate-remnant native vegetation, moderate-rehabilitation areas, and low
ecological constraint.  The definition for each constraint class is provided below in Table 7.
